饭后，约翰和南妮进了客厅，约翰抽烟，南妮给他弹琴。温特家这架钢琴不错，可露西弹得平常，加上操劳过度、身子单薄，早就不练了。他们常念叨买一架自动钢琴，可琴行要的差价太高，这份奢侈终究没到手。
迪米帮着母亲收拾桌子、洗碗碟，活儿干完，娘儿俩也到客厅去陪约翰和南妮。不过迪米很快犯困，露西领他去睡觉，守着他讲故事、唱摇篮曲，直到他睡熟。
她回到客厅时，约翰和母亲正谈得起劲。
「过来，露西。」约翰从卧榻上唤她，「南妮又给我讲了些她和你父亲的纠葛。我真想不到他待她那么坏。真是太不像话了！」
「我不能谈论爸爸，约翰。我记得我们说好不谈过去的，妈妈。」露西的声音发紧。
「她没怪罪任何人。」约翰把露西拉到身边的卧榻上，「别这么神经过敏。」他搂住她，吻了吻她的额头。
露西开始抚平他蓬乱的头发。
「瞧这对卿卿我我的小鸽子。」南妮微笑着喃喃道，「我真希望你们永远这样。」她抬手掩住眼睛，「我的生活只剩了不幸——一场纯粹的悲剧——若不是还有那份友情和理解——那个人你们谁都不认识——」她停住了。
「可怜的南妮！」约翰伸手握住她的手。
「我也需要一点疼爱啊。」她握紧他的手恳求道。约翰把她从椅子上拉过来，安置在卧榻上，自己坐进了她和露西中间。
「跟我们住在一起，你会快活起来，忘掉那些烦恼。露西，如今南妮来了，咱们得多出去走走。这能让她不再想过去，免得老在心里盘算。」
露西轻轻从约翰的臂弯里抽身站起来。
「你去哪儿，露西？」他问。
「我忘了点洗衣裳的件数。

===Original===

The washerwoman is coming for them early in the morning.”
“Poor Lucy! She don’t realize that this is your only time to relax, John. I could easily have counted the clothes before dinner if she had told me about it,” remarked Nannie when she and John were alone.
After some time Lucy was heard ascending the stairs and John called out, “Why don’t you come in here, Lucy?”
“I am going to bed early,” she answered. “I’ve a lot to do tomorrow. Is there anything I can do for you, Mamma? You know where everything is, don’t you?”
“Why of course I do,” responded her mother, coming out into the hall. “I can look after myself. I don’t want you to ever worry about me. Kiss me good night.” Lucy leaned over the balustrade and kissed her.
“I hope you rest well. You’ve been working too hard. I’m glad I’m here to help you now. I used to think when we were separated how much you needed my care. Poor Mother often spoke of it. You must get strong again.”
“I’ll be up in a little while,” called John.
Lucy undressed herself and lay down. The murmur of the voices of her mother and her husband came to her faintly. She lay and thought.
When John came to bed he supposed her to be asleep, and tiptoed about the room, undressing in silence without a light and getting into bed with the greatest gentleness to avoid waking her. Before reclining on his pillow he leaned over her and saw her eyes wide open.
“Why, Lucy, I thought you had gone to bye-bye,” he said, and, lying down, took her in his arms.
She drew away from him.
“Why, Lucy, what’s the matter?” he asked in a hurt tone.
She made no answer but he could feel that she was silently sobbing. He took her in his arms again. This time she did not resist.
“Tell me what it is, darling,” he pleaded.
“Oh, John, do you want anybody but me?” She wept.
“Of course I don’t,” he returned vehemently. “What made you think that?”
“I don’t know,” she answered evasively, vaguely comforted by his ardent denial.
“Was that what you were crying about?” he asked.
“No.”
“Well, what was it? Won’t you tell me, sweetheart?”
“Oh, John, it wasn’t anything﻿—except I’m﻿—so tired.”
“I know you must be,” he whispered. “It’s been an exciting day for you. But now that Nannie’s here she can take a lot of things off your hands. It will make things easier for you. You poor little girl, you have had to work so hard, and you aren’t strong.”
“I don’t think so, John.”

===Chinese===

洗衣妇一大早就要来收。」
「可怜的露西！」屋里只剩她和约翰两人时，南妮说道，「她不明白，这是你一天里唯一松快的时候。她要是早说一声，饭前我点点衣服就是了。」
过了一阵，听见露西上楼的脚步声，约翰喊道：「怎么不进来坐坐，露西？」
「我想早点睡。」她答道，「明天还有一大堆事呢。妈妈，有什么要我效劳的吗？东西都在哪儿您都知道吧？」
「那还用说。」母亲应着，走到门厅里，「我自己照管得了自己。你可别为我操心。亲亲我道个晚安。」露西俯身越过栏杆吻了她。
「愿您睡个好觉。你太操劳了。如今我在这里能帮帮你，我真高兴。咱们分开的那些日子，我常想你多么需要我的照管。我那可怜的母亲生前也常念叨这个。你可得把身子养结实了。」
「我一会儿就上去。」约翰喊道。
露西解衣躺下。母亲和丈夫的低语隐隐传到她耳边。她躺着，想心事。
约翰上床时只当她睡熟了，蹑手蹑脚地在房里摸黑脱衣，极轻极轻地钻进被窝，生怕惊醒她。躺上枕头前，他俯身看她，却见她眼睛睁得大大的。
「咦，露西，我还当你睡觉觉了呢。」他说着躺下，把她搂进怀里。
她从他怀里挣开。
「怎么了，露西？什么事？」他问，声调里带着委屈。
她不作声，可他感觉得出她在无声地抽泣。他又把她搂进怀里。这一回她没有挣。
「告诉我怎么了，宝贝。」他恳求道。
「唉，约翰，除了我，你还想要别人吗？」她哭着说。
「当然不。」他急切地答道，「你怎么会这么想？」
「不知道。」她含糊地答道，他那番热烈的否认隐隐给了她安慰。
「你就是为这个哭的？」他问。
「不是。」
「那是为了什么？不肯告诉我吗，心肝？」
「唉，约翰，没什么——只是我——太累了。」
「我知道你一定累了。」他悄声说，「这一天够你心绪翻腾的。不过如今南妮来了，好多事她都能替你分担，你日子会松快些。你个可怜的小姑娘，一直这么操劳，身子又单薄。」
「我可不这么想，约翰。」

===Original===

“Don’t think what?”
“That she will make it easier.”
He considered a minute.
“Lucy.”
“Yes?”
“Were you crying because I stayed and talked with Nannie instead of coming upstairs with you?”
“No﻿—not that alone. I﻿—”
He caught her tightly to him.
“You dear, sweet, foolish, jealous little thing,” he whispered, laughing. “The idea! Why you precious darling, I was only trying to be nice to your mother on her first day for your sake. Lucy, don’t you ever, ever dream for a second that anyone could take *your* place for a tiny instant. Why, sweetheart, I *love* you. I only like other people.”
Lucy kissed him.
Soon they were asleep in each other’s arms.

===Chinese===

「不这么想什么？」
「她能让日子松快。」
他想了想。
「露西。」
「嗯？」
「你哭，是因为我留下陪南妮说话，没有跟你一起上楼？」
「不——不光是这个。我——」
他把她紧紧搂住。
「你这个又可爱、又傻、又爱吃醋的小东西。」他笑着悄声说，「瞧你这想法！哎呀，我的心肝宝贝，我不过是看在你的份上，在她来的头一天对你母亲好些罢了。露西，你千万别、千万别有一秒钟想歪了，以为有谁能顶替*你*一星半点儿。哎呀，心肝，我*爱*的是你。对旁人不过是喜欢罢了。」
露西吻了他。
很快，他们便相拥睡去。

## IV / 四
===Original===

Mrs. Merwent’s maiden name was Anna Lockhart. She was born in a southern state, of a family that was considered to be aristocratic and distinguished. None of its members had ever accomplished anything noteworthy, but they had lived a long time in Russellville, there were a large number of them, and they had usually raised sugar and cotton instead of corn and tobacco.
Her earliest memories were of a large, square, white house with a front porch supported by Corinthian pillars, a long driveway lined with great trees on either side, and much display of lavish hospitality.
From early childhood her whims were gratified and it never occurred to her that the numerous things given to her, the clothes, the pony, the negress who nursed her, the rings and brooches, were not a part of her own charm and importance.
Her first pleasure was to attract attention; whether by doing things that earned her the name of “Tomboy,” by being able to show dresses and ribbons finer than her playmates, or by any other device her ingenuity could discover.
She liked negroes because they were obsequious, and she was saucy to white children supposed not to be her social equals whom, in imitation of her elders, she designated as “trash.”
Anna, or Nannie as she was called, delighted in showing her power, and was relentless in exacting notice and recognition of her position. She stuck pins into the arms of Aunt Martha, her nurse, while the old negress was dressing her; she stepped on the bare toes of the small black children on her father’s place, and invented punishments for the dogs, cats, and other domestic animals that happened to be at her mercy.
Especially she enjoyed her tyranny over Troupe, as the large family dog was named. It pleased her to see him grovel before her when she scolded him. She often played a game that she called “circus.” She would tie the dog with a rope that had a loop which she could slip over a stake driven into the ground, and with a buggy whip she would make him run round and round. He would look back at her with pleading eyes, his tail clamped to his body, but she did not pity him. When he was completely exhausted he would lie down, his tongue lolling and the saliva dripping from his mottled black gums. After she untied him he would run about, crazy with joy, barking and licking at her hands and ankles. She paid no attention to these demonstrations.

===Chinese===

默温特太太的娘家姓是安娜·洛克哈特（Anna Lockhart）。她生在南方的一个州，出身于一个被公认为高贵显赫的家庭。这个家的成员谁也没干成过什么了不得的事，但他们在拉塞尔维尔住了很久，人丁兴旺，而且别家种玉米烟草的时候，他们家历来种的是甘蔗和棉花。
她最早的记忆，是一所方方正正的大白房子，门廊由科林斯式廊柱支撑，一条长长的车道两旁古木参天，还有那处处铺张的款客排场。
从孩提时代起，她要什么有什么，从没想过人家给她的这一切——衣裳、小马驹、带她的黑人奶妈、戒指和胸针——不过是她自己魅力与身份的装点，而不是它们本身。
她最大的乐趣就是引人注意：要么靠野小子似的作为落下一个「假小子」的名声，要么靠拿出比玩伴们更漂亮的裙子和缎带，要么靠她的机灵所能想出的任何别的花样。
她喜欢黑人，因为他们卑躬屈膝；对那些被认为门第不如她的白人孩子，她则出言不逊，学着大人的腔调称人家为「穷白佬」。
安娜——或者说南妮（Nannie），大家都这么叫她——最爱卖弄自己的威风，逼着人家垂顾她、承认她的地位，从不留情。奶妈玛莎舅妈替她穿衣时，她拿别针扎老人的胳膊；她踩父亲庄园里黑人小孩的光脚板；落到她手里的狗呀猫呀别的家畜，她都能想出刑罚来摆布。
她尤其喜欢作践特鲁普——那条大看家狗就叫这个名字。她一骂它，它便在她面前匍匐哀告，这最使她快意。她常玩一种自己叫做「马戏」的游戏：用一根带活扣的绳子拴住狗，把活扣套在钉进地里的木桩上，再挥着一根马车鞭子赶它一圈一圈地跑。狗回头望她，眼神哀求，尾巴紧紧夹在两腿间，她毫不可怜它。待它彻底累瘫了，便趴下吐着舌头，斑驳的黑牙床上淌着涎水。她把它解开之后，它欢喜得发了疯似的满处乱窜，汪汪叫着，舔她的手和脚踝。这些殷勤她一概不理。

===Original===

Nannie was called stingy, as she seldom divided her sweetmeats or other good things with her playmates, and would not allow them to touch her toys. But occasionally, when glory could be gotten out of giving, she would bestow some old or broken plaything, always demanding profuse expressions of gratitude, however, from the recipient.
No attempt was made to teach Nannie any useful occupation, and, as she cared neither for stories nor for books, the task of amusing her became no sinecure.
As she grew older she loved to go shopping with her mother in the little town. They always went in the family carriage and Mrs. Lockhart, who considered that she could insult salespeople with impunity, invariably asked for the best in a haughty voice and inquired the price after she had decided on the article.
Nannie’s desire to attract notice increased with the years. She was fond of having her picture taken in fancy dress in imitation of various popular actresses. One, in which she was represented as Iphigenia, was most flattering and was displayed for some months in the windows of the establishment of the local photographer.
She begged a riding horse from her father, and she liked to be seen in elegant riding habits, and at parties in sumptuous gowns too old for her. She adopted a pertness and flippancy of speech that was described as “smart” and assumed a domineering manner toward the servants which was, it is true, less marked as regarded the “house boy” who was a handsome young mulatto.
She also picked out for condescending notice an admiring girl friend at the private school which they both attended and made a chum of her. This girl, Roberta White, was far from pretty, and could be patronized, but was not unintelligent, and possessed considerable personality. Unfortunately Nannie’s first boy admirer soon transferred his callow devotion to “Bob White,” as Nannie had dubbed Roberta. There was a curious scene in which Bob White was forever disowned, and Nannie ever after, in referring to it, spoke of Roberta’s “ingratitude.”
Nannie was eighteen years old when she left school. It was a disastrous year for her father. He had inherited money which he invested and spent with equal display and absence of judgment, but he awoke one day to find that creditors were impervious both to the dignity of the Lockhart name and the impressiveness of the ancestral mansion. Mrs.

===Chinese===

南妮落了个吝啬的名声，因为她极少把糖果点心分给玩伴，也不许人家碰她的玩具。不过偶尔，当施舍能换来风光的时候，她也会赏出一件旧了或破了的玩物——只是必定要受赏的人千恩万谢个够。
没人打算教南妮学什么有用的本事，而她既不爱听故事也不爱读书，哄她这件事便成了苦差。
她大些了，喜欢跟母亲到小镇上去买东西。她们出门向来坐自家的马车。洛克哈特太太自以为辱慢伙计不用付任何代价，总是端足架子用傲慢的嗓音要最好的货，看定了东西才问价钱。
南妮年岁越长，越想出风头。她爱穿上各式戏装，模仿当时走红的女演员去照相。其中一张扮的是伊菲革涅亚，照得格外标致，在当地照相馆的橱窗里一摆就是好几个月。
她缠着父亲讨了一匹鞍马，爱让人看见她一身讲究的骑装，出席舞会时又穿着与年龄不符的华服。她学成了一嘴轻佻尖利的口齿，人家称之为「伶俐」；对仆人则摆出一副颐指气使的派头——不过对那个漂亮的年轻混血「听差」，这一套倒确实收敛几分。
她还在两人同读的私立学校里挑了一个倾慕她的女友，施以居高临下的垂青，跟她结成密友。这姑娘叫罗伯塔·怀特，远远说不上漂亮，尽可以被人俯视，人却不笨，颇有几分个性。不幸的是，南妮的第一个倾慕者很快便把那毛头小子的一片痴心转到了「白鲍勃」身上——这浑名是南妮给罗伯塔起的。于是演出了一场古怪的戏，白鲍勃从此被扫地出门；南妮日后提起此事，总说罗伯塔「忘恩负义」。
南妮十八岁那年离开学校。那一年她父亲遭了大殃。他继承过一笔钱，投资和挥霍得同样阔气、同样没有头脑，可有一天他一觉醒来，发现债主们对洛克哈特这个姓氏的尊严和祖宅的气派竟然不为所动。洛克哈特太太倒是个能干人，拿出许多丈夫所欠缺的实际本事来应付局面。南妮却不能相提并论，她从周围人那里吸进去的，是一种默认她家人天生享有特权的观念。倘若她只归父亲一个人管，多半要等到彻底败落当头一棒，才会懂得钱的分量；所幸洛克哈特太太行使起权威来一如既往地果断，让南妮明白：至少眼下，衣裳要少几件，舞会更要少去几场。
为了不在这场热闹里输给往日让她眼红的白鲍勃，南妮竟用最简省的料子临时赶出一件件极见效果的裙子，那份眼力和天分，让谁都没料到——最没料到的就是她母亲。南妮针线并不好，那些仓促赶制的行头做工从不考究，常常真有散架的危险，可是这儿缀一根缎带、那儿簪一朵花，那份讲究劲儿，拉塞尔维尔虽偏，也认得出叫做「俏」。

===Original===

Lockhart was an efficient person, however, and brought to bear upon the situation many of the practical qualities in which her husband was lacking. The same could not be said of Nannie, who had absorbed from those around her what seemed a tacit recognition of divine right as regarded the members of her family. If she had been subject to her father alone it is probable that only the jolt of an absolute downfall would have aroused her to an appreciation of financial values, but fortunately Mrs. Lockhart exercised her authority as decisively as was her custom and gave Nannie to understand that, for the time being at least, she might enjoy few dresses and fewer parties.
Not to be cut out of the gaieties in which the once envious Bob White was participating, Nannie astonished no one more than her mother by displaying considerable taste and talent in the improvization of very effective frocks with the simplest means. Nannie was not a good seamstress. The hastily devised costumes were never neatly made and were often in actual danger of falling apart, but a ribbon here or a flower there was applied with a discrimination that Russellville was not too provincial to recognize as “chic.”
Though such haphazard dressmaking was her only contribution to the household economy, it had a value that was more than apparent, and Mrs. Lockhart recognized it. She had determined that Nannie should find salvation for the family by attracting a husband whose pretensions to that distinction should rest on a solid financial basis.
---
It seemed almost a divine intervention to insure the Lockharts’ future when Arthur Merwent, a young lawyer from the north, came to the home town.
Young Merwent rented an office and bought some furniture. He purchased steel engravings of famous jurists to decorate his walls, unpacked his law books, and had a sign painted and hung over his door.
Mrs. Lockhart knew, by hearsay at least, that Merwent held expectations of inheriting some money, and, as the young man was attractive and a stranger, it was soon arranged that Arthur should live at the Lockhart home. He insisted on paying for his board and, after some perfunctory objections which embarrassed Nannie and Mr. Lockhart but did not disturb the mother, this was agreed to.
Arthur was agreeable but uncommunicative.

===Chinese===

这种凑合的裁缝活虽是她对家用仅有的贡献，其价值却远不止眼前这点，洛克哈特太太心里有数。她已经拿定主意：南妮要为这一家人找到出路，就得钓一个丈夫，而且这丈夫的派头必须建立在殷实的家底上。
---
北方来的年轻律师亚瑟·默温特（Arthur Merwent）来到这座小城时，简直像上天特意安排来保洛克哈特家前程的。
小默温特租了间事务所，置办了几件家具。他买了几位法学名家的钢版雕印画像挂在墙上，开箱摆出法律书，又请人做了块招牌挂在门楣上。
洛克哈特太太至少听人说过，默温特有望继承一笔钱；这年轻人又有模样又是外乡人，于是很快安排妥当，让亚瑟住到洛克哈特家来。他坚持要付膳宿费，家里人客套推辞了几句——南妮和洛克哈特先生都有些讪讪的，做母亲的却毫不动容——便依了他。
亚瑟和气，却不爱吐露心迹。

===Original===

His reserve pleased Nannie’s father, who was pompous of manner and weak of purpose, but invariably inclined to be enthusiastic about a new acquaintance.
“That young man can keep his mouth shut. He’ll rise,” Mr. Lockhart often said.
From the beginning Nannie assumed a light and jesting attitude toward Arthur. She was saucy and capricious, demanding services and attentions calculated to convince him of her superior birth and position, and suggesting with unconscious skill potentialities that she neither possessed nor appreciated. He said little, as was his wont, but Nannie was clever enough to perceive the favorable impression she was making.
Thrown into a continuous semi-intimacy the two young people reacted as might have been expected and soon the imperturbable Arthur gravely declared himself. Mrs. Lockhart preserved a grim neutrality in the affair. True, his financial prospects were inclined to soften her, but with all that he was a Yankee and there existed a grave doubt as to the aristocracy of his connections. Her husband, who had visited the Merwents and had been considerably impressed by the prosperity evinced in their domestic establishment, was, however, positive in his approbation. This did not alter his wife’s opinion or change her attitude, for she was not accustomed to take his point of view seriously; but when a friend made a self imposed pilgrimage to the Merwent home and returned with enthusiastic corroboration of Mr. Lockhart’s report, Mrs. Lockhart relented and her negative aloofness became encouragement.
Nannie, who shared the common conviction that Arthur was a rising young lawyer and a desirable catch, consented to become engaged.
This did not hinder her from indulging in coquettish tricks of a number and variety that her fiancé found disconcerting.
Finally he brought things to an issue, refusing to be played with longer, and, after exhausting her ingenuity in the endeavor to gain more delay, she fixed the wedding day for a date four months ahead.
Twice in the midst of the work on the extensive trousseau, Nannie and Arthur quarrelled, and on each occasion she returned his ring. But the misunderstandings were adjusted and the wedding morning finally arrived.

===Chinese===

他这份沉静正合南妮父亲的脾胃——此公架子十足，主意全无，对新交却总是照例一阵热心。
「那个年轻人嘴严。会有出息的。」洛克哈特先生常说。
打从一开始，南妮对亚瑟便是一副轻慢打趣的姿态。她任性撒泼，支使他做这做那，要他殷勤侍候，存心叫他领教她出身和地位的高贵；她又以无意识的本领暗示出种种她既不具备也不懂欣赏的柔情来。他照例话不多，可南妮机灵，看得出自己给他留下的印象不坏。
两个年轻人日日厮混在一处半亲半密，彼此生出情意是意料中事，不久，那位不动声色的亚瑟便郑重其事地表白了。洛克哈特太太在这件事上保持着冷冰冰的中立。不错，他的钱财前程让她心软过，可他到底是个北方佬，他家亲戚算不算上等人，还大可怀疑。她丈夫倒是极力赞成——他拜访过默温特家，对他们家宅里显露的殷实颇为叹服。但这改变不了太太的看法，也改变不了她的态度，因为她向来不把丈夫的见识当回事；直到有位朋友自行其是地去默温特家朝圣了一趟，回来把洛克哈特先生的见闻热烈证实了一番，洛克哈特太太才回心转意，那份疏远的冷淡变成了鼓励。
南妮也随大流认定亚瑟是个前程无量的年轻律师，是门好亲事，便应允订了婚。
这并不妨碍她卖弄风情的手段，花样之多，令她的未婚夫无所适从。
末了他索性摊牌，不肯再被人耍着玩；她施尽机谋想再拖一拖，拖不成了，才把婚期定在四个月之后。
赶制大嫁妆的当口，南妮和亚瑟吵了两回，每回都把戒指退还给他。但误会终归化解，成婚的早晨终于到了。

===Original===

Nannie had shown much interest in the more obvious preparations for the ceremony, insisting that her gowns must be of such and such a price, that her bridesmaids outnumber those of her previously married friends, and that the affair as a whole be conducted with an éclat which strained the resources of the Lockharts’ reduced finances to the uttermost.
Nevertheless, on the day before the wedding she showed herself to the household in a state of extraordinary depression, wandering listlessly from room to room, striking discordant notes on the piano, and finally, having fled from Arthur’s presence, she was discovered face downwards on an old horsehair sofa in a violent paroxysm of weeping.
Mrs. Lockhart, who had gone to seek her, was unable to elicit any explanation of her distress and called Merwent. But it was a mistake. Nannie turned on him with a storm of accusations.
“You’ve wrecked my life! I don’t know what’s to become of me!” she wailed.
“It’s not too late yet, Nannie,” Arthur answered. His voice was slightly unsteady and his eyes shone dangerously, but his manner was quiet.
“Oh! Oh! How dare you! I was never so insulted in my life! You don’t care how much disgrace and humiliation you heap on me! It doesn’t make any difference to *you* what they say about me!”
Arthur was left alone. He sat on the sofa Nannie had quitted and held his head in his hands. After a few moments he rose and lighted a cigar. He was smoking when Mrs. Lockhart came in search of him a quarter of an hour later. She brought with her a glass of hot eggnog which she had made to comfort him.
“It’s all the perfectly natural result of her high strung state,” remarked Mrs. Lockhart emphatically. “You must remember this is the last day of her girlhood,” she added in a significant manner.
Arthur drank the eggnog and said nothing.
---
The next day, however, Nannie was radiant. Her mother and her cousin, Mrs. Sheldon, had helped to dress her. The wedding gown bore the mark of an expensive Louisville house, the bouquet was of white orchids, and the diamond pendant which Arthur had given her, though modestly small and fragile, glittered becomingly on her plump throat.
Arthur entered the church gravely, his head bent, and even as Nannie came toward him at the chancel rail he did not lift his eyes. It was only as they stood side by side that he glanced at her face.

===Chinese===

对婚礼上那些看得见的排场，南妮表现了莫大的热心：她的礼服必须是多少多少价钱的，她的女傧相必须比先出阁的姊妹们多，整个场面必须风风光光——这份风光把洛克哈特家紧巴巴的钱囊撑到了极限。
然而婚礼前一日，她却在全家面前露出一副异样的愁容，无精打采地逐屋游荡，在钢琴上弹出几个刺耳的音，最后躲开了亚瑟，被人发现趴在一架旧马鬃沙发上，哭得死去活来。
洛克哈特太太去寻她，问不出她伤心的缘由，便唤了默温特来。可这一步走错了。南妮劈头盖脸朝他发作起来。
「你毁了我的一生！我不知道自己往后会怎么样！」她哭喊道。
「现在还来得及，南妮。」亚瑟答道。他的嗓音略略发颤，眼睛亮得吓人，神色却很平静。
「哦！哦！你好大的胆！我这辈子没受过这样的侮辱！你根本不管你往我头上堆了多少羞辱！人家议论我什么，你满不在乎！」
亚瑟被撇下了。他坐在南妮方才离开的沙发上，双手捧着头。过了一会儿他站起来，点上一支雪茄。一刻钟后洛克哈特太太来找他时，他正抽着。她端来一杯亲手调的热蛋奶酒，给他压惊。
「这全是她那根神经绷得太紧的结果，再自然不过了。」洛克哈特太太斩钉截铁地说。「你得记住，今天是她做姑娘的最后一天。」她又意味深长地补了一句。
亚瑟喝了蛋奶酒，一言不发。
---
第二天，南妮却容光焕发。她母亲和她表嫂谢尔登太太帮她穿戴停当。婚纱出自路易斯维尔一家昂贵的名店，捧花是白兰花的，亚瑟送的那枚钻石坠子虽然小巧单薄，戴在她丰腴的颈项上也正闪闪生辉。
亚瑟低头走进教堂；南妮朝圣坛栏杆这边走来时，他仍旧垂着眼。直到两人并肩站定，他才瞥了一眼她的脸。

===Original===

Every trace of depression had vanished. She held her head high with the slightly insolent air that she had so often been told was aristocratic, and she really looked prettier than he had ever seen her.
The recessional was played. As the bridal couple emerged from the church he turned slightly toward her.
“Well, Nannie,” he whispered, smiling a little.
They were close to the carriage steps. Arthur moved back to assist her. By some chance awkwardness his heel caught in a loop of satin. There was a tearing sound and Nannie flung herself from his grasp.
“I’m sorry,” he murmured.
An impassioned but enigmatic “Ah!” was her only reply, and until the house was reached she sat far away from him on the carriage cushions, replying in monosyllables until Arthur relapsed into moody silence. When they arrived at their destination Nannie left the carriage hurriedly and unassisted.
A supper had been planned but the hour of the departure of the train they were to take prevented their presence. There was, however, no way to escape the brief reception. At this function Nannie, who had looked almost defiantly spirited during the ceremony, assumed a sudden appearance of disconcerting melancholy. The pair stood under an arch of smilax, and Nannie could see herself quite plainly in the long pier glass opposite and appreciated how large and dark her eyes seemed in the shadows that fell on her face.
The affair as a whole was not gay. Mrs. Lockhart shed a few impressive tears, glancing somewhat apprehensively from time to time at her daughter’s unresponsive features. Bob White had come to the reception, being herself engaged. Nannie greeted her with effusive sadness and clasped her in an embrace that was like a despairing renewal of devotion. The two girls talked in low tones, and Arthur was conscious of being ignored. A few moments later Nannie went off to prepare for travel and he was able to excuse himself.
---
It was half past eleven at night. The Pullman was dimly lighted. Merwent had avoided a stateroom and every appearance that might indicate that he and Nannie were a bridal couple, but he felt that the fresh modishness of Nannie’s costume betrayed them. They sat down in a vacant seat while the beds were being made up. Arthur kept an unresponsive profile turned to his wife. He had resolved not to make any more advances.
The two swayed stiffly with the motion of the car.

===Chinese===

愁容消失得无影无踪。她高高地昂着头，带着那副她常听人夸作贵族气的、微微倨傲的神情，看上去当真比他历来所见都要俏丽。
退场曲奏起来了。新人步出教堂时，他微微朝她侧过头。
「好了，南妮。」他悄声说，带着一丝笑。
马车踏级就在跟前。亚瑟退后一步去搀她。不知怎么一个失手，他的鞋跟勾住了她缎子裙裾上的一个环。只听「嗤啦」一声，南妮猛地甩脱了他的手。
「对不起。」他嗫嚅道。
她只回了一声饱含激愤却捉摸不透的「啊！」，此后一路直到家门，她远远缩在座垫那头，回话只肯用一个字，亚瑟只好闷闷地闭了嘴。到了地方，南妮自己匆匆下了车，不要人搀。
本来说好有晚宴，可他们要赶的那班火车开车的钟点不容他们赴席。然而短短的喜宴是躲不掉的。在这个场合，方才在教堂里几乎显得意气昂然、目中无人的南妮，忽然换上一副叫人发窘的愁容。一对新人站在牛眼篱拱门下，南妮正对着对面那面落地穿衣镜，把自己望得清清楚楚，她很得意，脸上罩着阴影，一双眼睛显得又大又黑。
整个喜宴都算不上欢畅。洛克哈特太太挤下了几滴有分量的眼泪，又时不时忧心忡忡地觑着女儿那张毫无反应的脸。白鲍勃自己也订了婚，来吃喜酒了。南妮满腔哀愁地招呼她，把她搂在怀里，那样子像是以绝望的热情重续旧情。两个姑娘低声絮语，亚瑟自觉受到了冷落。过了一会儿，南妮进去换旅行的装束，他才得以脱身。
---
夜里十一点半。普尔曼卧车里的灯昏昏沉沉。默温特特意没要包房，也避开了所有可能招人认出新婚夫妇的排场，可他觉得南妮一身簇新入时的行头，还是把他们出卖了。乘务员铺床的当口，两人在空位上坐下。亚瑟把一张冷淡的侧脸留给妻子。他打定主意不再先开口。
两个人随着车厢的晃动僵硬地摇来晃去。

===Original===

The woodwork creaked. Long shadows moved up and down at the end of the passage. Snoring from a curtained berth was audible.
Nannie touched Arthur’s arm lightly. He looked down at her in surprise. She was regarding him with a new and softened expression.
“Arthur!” Her voice shook slightly.
His face cleared.
“Nannie!” They kissed stealthily.
When the conductor came down the aisle they were sitting consciously far apart and Arthur’s face was flushed.

===Chinese===

木作咯吱作响。长长的影子在过道尽头晃上晃下。帘子围着的铺位里传出鼾声。
南妮轻轻碰了碰亚瑟的胳膊。他诧异地低头看她。她正望着他，带着一种前所未有的温软神情。
「亚瑟！」她的嗓音微微发颤。
他的脸色开朗了。
「南妮！」两人偷偷吻了一下。
列车员走过车厢过道时，两人各怀心事似的坐得远远的，亚瑟满脸通红。

## IX / 九
===Original===

After the scene in which he announced the beginning of his divorce proceedings Arthur never entered his house again. He had established a residence in the state capital but was still compelled, in the course of various suits and details of the legal practice he was disposing of, to make frequent visits to Russellville. On such occasions he slept in his office and ate his meals at the local hotel.
Nannie, who was always panic stricken when alone, less from fear than from lack of resources within herself, went, on her own initiative and despite a grudging welcome, to live with Mrs. Lockhart at “Cousin Minnie’s.”
The divorce was granted in due time and all Russellville was agog with the news. Nannie considered it a matter of propriety that she should stay in semi-retirement for a while, and so went out very little. However, when she did leave the sanctum which “Cousin Minnie” had provided, she always managed to see Professor Walsh, to whom she came to look for advice and help in every situation.
---
It was the same season, the second of Lucy’s attendance at the Art School, that Lucy and John Winter were married. On account of Nannie’s violent reproaches at the time of the divorce Lucy had not communicated with her mother for many months, but when she decided to marry John she immediately wrote to Nannie.
“John is only beginning business and we shall have to be very economical for a while at least,” the letter said. “Miss Storms has asked us to be married at her flat, but we shall have a very simple wedding. I would be glad to have you come here but I suppose you had rather not under the circumstances. We have rented some furnished rooms that will be convenient for light housekeeping but I hope soon we can get out in the suburbs where it will be cheaper and healthier.”
Although Professor Walsh had questioned Nannie closely regarding Lucy and the girl’s attitude toward family affairs, Nannie replied to her daughter’s letter without consulting him.
“I am only your mother,” Nannie wrote, “so of course it would be preposterous to consider *me* in regard to the step you are taking. I have suffered a great deal at the hands of your father but I have not lost my pride and self-respect yet, and if there was anything more you could do to further alienate me, this was it.

===Chinese===

自从宣布开始离婚诉讼的那一幕后，亚瑟再没有踏进过自己的家门。他在州府安顿了下来，但由于要处理各种诉讼和收尾的律师业务，还不得不经常回拉塞尔维尔。每逢这种时候，他睡在事务所，在当地的旅馆用饭。
南妮一向怕独处——与其说是害怕，不如说她自己心里没底——便自作主张，不顾人家的勉强脸色，搬去「明妮表姐」家同洛克哈特太太住在一起。
离婚按时判了下来，全拉塞尔维尔都为这消息议论纷纷。南妮觉得按体统自己该半隐居一阵子，所以很少出门。不过，每逢她走出「明妮表姐」给她备下的那间静室，她总会设法见沃尔什教授——无论什么处境，她都指望他出主意、帮衬。
---
就在同一个季节——露西上美术学校的第二年——露西和约翰·温特结婚了。离婚时南妮那番痛骂，让露西好几个月没跟母亲通信；可她决定嫁给约翰后，立刻给南妮写了信。
「约翰的事业才起步，至少有一阵子我们得非常节省，」信上写道。「斯托姆斯小姐请我们到她的公寓结婚，但婚礼会非常简单。我很乐意你来这儿，不过我想在这种情况下你宁肯不来。我们租了几间带家具的房间，随便做点家常便饭很方便，但我希望不久能搬到郊外去，那边又便宜又卫生。」
沃尔什教授虽然曾仔细盘问过南妮关于露西以及这姑娘对家里事的态度，南妮回女儿的信却没有先请教他。
「我不过是你母亲罢了，」南妮写道，「所以你要迈这一步，还来考虑*我*，自然是不成体统的。我在你父亲手里受够了苦，可我还没有丢掉我的自尊和体面；倘若你还能有什么法子叫我跟你更疏远一层，这一件就是了。

===Original===

You forfeited all right to my affection when you deserted me in my hour of trial in order to pursue your own selfish aims, but this marriage to a man I know nothing about, who may be a nobody from Heaven knows where, is the climax. I think from now on there is not much use in our writing to one another.”
Arthur was still engaged in an important case at the capital and could not be present at the wedding.
“I am sorry I can not be there,” his letter read. “All the advice I can give you, Lucy, is don’t let your mother intrude on your happiness.”
John sent an invitation to his parents, but Dr. Winter, a clergyman, was old and in ill health, and was not able to bear the double strain of the journey and the cold weather. Mrs. Winter, a kindly sentimental lady who would never have thought of leaving her husband, sent tearful regrets, and bemoaned the fact that John and Lucy did not come to the home town and so make it possible for Dr. Winter himself to officiate at the wedding of his only son.
In the end only Jim Sprague and Miss Storms witnessed the quiet affair. These two had liked each other since the day of their first meeting. Miss Storms’ interest in Jim had risen from the fact that he was John’s business associate and intimate. Later she gave him her friendship for his own sake and often referred to him affectionately as “our Jim.” There was an unanalysable and very subtle comprehension between the two, though Miss Storms remained quite as kindly aloof from him in her manner as she did with John and Lucy; and Jim, as in all his relations, was ungiven to self-revealing speech.
“Well, Jim, Lucy has good taste in friends as well as in husbands,” Miss Storms remarked, smiling at him with quizzical complaisance when the wedding ceremony was over. “I hate all the immodest fol-de-lol of church weddings,” she went on, changing the subject, “but it is a shame that anything as pretty as Lucy in those new togs should be wasted on the desert air. For you and I don’t count.”
While she was speaking Lucy came up. Miss Storms took her hand.
“Well, dear?”
Lucy’s eyes swam with tears. She clasped Miss Storms’ hand in both her own and squeezed it impulsively. Miss Storms kissed her forehead.
“I’m jealous, child,” the older woman declared.
“So am I,” John laughed.
“Neither of you need be.” Lucy smiled unsteadily.
“What about you, Jim?” Miss Storms turned to the other guest.

===Chinese===

你在我危难之际撇下我，去追你自己自私的盘算，那时你就把要我疼爱的权利丢尽了；而这门亲事，嫁给我一无所知的男人，说不定是天知道哪里来的无名小卒——更是登峰造极。我想从今往后，咱们通信也没什么意思了。」
亚瑟还在州府办一桩要紧的案子，无法出席婚礼。
「可惜我不能到场，」他的信上写道。「露西，我能给你的忠告只有一句：别让你母亲搅了你的幸福。」
约翰给父母发了请帖，可温特医生是位牧师，年迈多病，经不起旅途和寒冷天气的双重劳顿。温特太太是位心肠软、爱动情的老太太，从未想过撇下丈夫，只得含泪回信致歉，还连连叹惜约翰和露西不回老家来，好让温特医生亲自为独子的婚礼主婚。
到头来，只有吉姆·斯普拉格和斯托姆斯小姐见证了这场简单的婚礼。这两个人从初次见面那天起就彼此投缘。斯托姆斯小姐起初看重吉姆，是因为他是约翰的合伙人兼挚友；后来是为他本人的缘故才交上这个朋友，还常亲昵地叫他「我们的吉姆」。两人之间有一种说不清道不明的默契，尽管斯托姆斯小姐待他的态度同待约翰、露西一样，始终是客客气气的疏淡；而吉姆也同他所有的人际往来一样，从不肯多说自己的心事。
「我说吉姆，露西挑朋友的眼光不亚于挑丈夫，」婚礼结束后，斯托姆斯小姐含着打趣又谦和的笑对他说。「我讨厌教堂婚礼那套放肆的花架子，」她岔开话头，「可是露西穿着那身新衣裳那么漂亮，白白浪费在荒漠的空气里，未免可惜。因为你和我是不算数的。」
她正说着，露西走了过来。斯托姆斯小姐握住她的手。
「怎么样，亲爱的？」
露西眼里噙满泪水。她把斯托姆斯小姐的手紧紧握在自己两只手里，情不自禁地攥了攥。斯托姆斯小姐吻了吻她的额头。
「孩子，我可嫉妒了，」年长的女士宣称。
「我也是，」约翰笑道。
「你们俩都不用。」露西笑着，笑得有些不稳。
「你呢，吉姆？」斯托姆斯小姐转向另一位宾客。

===Original===

“I’ve always been jealous of John,” Jim answered, flushing a little as he spoke.
---
Arthur and Mrs. Low, for reasons best known to themselves, were not married in Chicago as expected, but at the state capital where Arthur’s new practice had begun to flourish. When the notice of the wedding was copied by the *Russellville Weekly Clarion*, another outburst of gossip occurred, but, as neither of them returned to the place after their marriage, it did not disturb them. Arthur made two visits to Chicago, and on one of these, not long after Dimmie’s birth, he spent a night at Rosedene. Here he met Jim Sprague for whom he afterward expressed a strong liking.
Nannie had no means of escape from the public attention attracted to herself and her interests, and the wounds to her vanity were keenly felt. The protest of her self-esteem was her conspicuous appearance in frequented places with Professor Walsh. Again Russellville talked, but not for long. Nannie’s persistent recklessness of opinion in this matter palled. She ceased to be a popular object of criticism. Events settled into the routine that is characteristic of small communities.
The years glided by without any outward change in Nannie’s life. Of Arthur’s doings, she knew nothing except an occasional hint. Mrs. Lockhart had issued an ultimatum on Lucy’s marriage by forbidding any further relation with such an undutiful daughter, and Nannie, overawed by the old lady and more or less dependent on her, dared not disobey.
Professor Walsh was often at the “big house” and, chiefly through his universally acknowledged friendship with the Sheldons, was yearly becoming more and more a power in the community. He paid much special and flattering attention to Mrs. Lockhart, who was rapidly growing deaf and would otherwise have been lonely.
Although the professor’s interest in Nannie was tacitly recognized by the family, Mrs. Lockhart employed all the ingenuity of a jealous and neglected old woman to curb impatience on the part of her niece and nephew, while Professor Walsh, who was already receiving for nothing the benefits that Nannie represented, constantly abetted the old lady’s wiles in so subtle a manner that Nannie hardly realized her own position.
After four years of this regime Mrs.

===Chinese===

「我一向嫉妒约翰。」吉姆答道，说话时脸微微一红。
---
亚瑟和洛太太出于只有他们自己明白的理由，没有如众人所料在芝加哥结婚，而是在亚瑟新开的事务所日渐兴旺的州府成的亲。婚讯被《拉塞尔维尔每周号角报》转载后，又掀起一阵闲话，可他们婚后谁也没再回那地方，也就不受打扰。亚瑟去过芝加哥两回，其中一回——迪米出生后不久——他在罗斯登过了一夜。就在那里他结识了吉姆·斯普拉格，事后对他表示了极大的好感。
南妮却没法躲开引到她自己和她种种利益上的公众目光，虚荣心挨的刀子她感觉得真真切切。她维护自尊的办法，是同沃尔什教授频频露面于人多的场所。拉塞尔维尔又议论开了，但没有议论很久。南妮在这件事上那股不管不顾的劲头叫人腻烦了，她不再是众口交攻的时髦对象。事情渐渐归于小地方特有的那种老一套。
几年悄悄过去，南妮的生活表面上没有一点变化。亚瑟的动静，她除了偶尔一点风声，一无所知。洛克哈特太太就露西的婚事下过最后通牒：不许再同这么个不孝女儿来往。南妮被老太太镇着，又多多少少靠她过活，不敢不从。
沃尔什教授常来「大宅」，主要是仗着大家公认的与谢尔登家的交情，年年在镇上成了越来越有势力的人物。他对洛克哈特太太大献别的殷勤——老太太耳朵很快背了，本会寂寞的。
虽然教授对南妮的那份心思家里人心照不宣，洛克哈特太太却拿出一个失宠吃醋的老太婆的全部心机，遏抑侄女侄婿的不耐；沃尔什教授本来就分文不出地享用着南妮所代表的一切好处，又处处以十分巧妙的手段助长老太太的伎俩，以致南妮几乎弄不清自己的处境。
这样的日子过了四年，洛克哈特

===Original===

Lockhart died, and Nannie was left in “Cousin Minnie’s” house with no one upon whom she could persuade herself she had the semblance of a claim. “Cousin Minnie” herself, on whom seemed to have descended Mrs. Lockhart’s mantle of decision, soon banished any doubts that Nannie may have had regarding her status in the “big house.”
“When are you going to marry Professor Walsh?” she asked Nannie abruptly one day not long after Mrs. Lockhart’s death.
“Why﻿—I never said I was going to marry him,” responded Nannie with a demurely girlish giggle.
“Well, everybody has been expecting you to for the last three or four years, and if you don’t intend to you will please not give people further reason to gossip about you while you are in my house. The best people have dropped you for it and I don’t care to be included.”
“Why, Cousin Minnie﻿—” began Nannie, tearful in an instant.
“Where do you get so many good clothes?” “Cousin Minnie” interrupted.
“Why﻿—why﻿—” Nannie faltered. But “Cousin Minnie” did not remain to discuss the matter.
This, and a number of similar happenings, led Nannie to consider her position seriously, and she made a surreptitious pilgrimage to consult her oracle, Professor Walsh, regarding her future.
“We can not marry for another year, at least, Anna,” said he didactically. Nannie’s eyes opened wide at this.
“Of course, Edward,” she acquiesced dutifully.
“Could you not visit your daughter for a while?” he suggested. “As a rule it is not best to remain too long with one relative.”
Nannie swallowed hard, as the recollection of her letter on the occasion of Lucy’s marriage came over her.
“But, Edward, I’ve told you how matters are between Lucy and me. You know how Mother took her going away and marrying a stranger afterward.”
“Just a minute, Anna. Don’t become so excited. Remember what I have told you so often, that ‘stillness of form and steadiness of feature are signal marks of good breeding.’ I was just about to say that now your mother is dead, it is a good time to write your daughter and renew your old relations with her. She will be most useful to us, and besides, a mother and daughter should not be separated.”
“I’m sure I have no hard feelings toward Lucy,” admitted Nannie, somewhat uneasily.
“Quite so, quite so. A letter properly written would in all probability readjust matters satisfactorily to all.

===Chinese===

太太死了，南妮留在「明妮表姐」家里，再没有一个人能让她说服自己：她对人家还有一分可以称道的权利。「明妮表姐」本人——洛克哈特太太那份杀伐决断的衣钵像是落到了她身上——不久便打消了南妮对自己在「大宅」中地位可能还存有的疑虑。
「你打算什么时候嫁给沃尔什教授？」洛克哈特太太死后不多几天，她劈头就这么问南妮。
「呀——我可从没说过要嫁他呀。」南妮应道，装出一副少女的娇羞咯咯一笑。
「哼，这三四年来人人都等着你嫁他呢。你要是不打算嫁，那么住在我家的时候，就请不要再去给人留下嚼舌根的口实。上等人家都为此不理你了，我不想被捎带进去。」
「哎呀，明妮表姐——」南妮一下子就要哭了。
「你哪来这么多好衣裳？」「明妮表姐」打断她。
「呃——呃——」南妮结结巴巴。可「明妮表姐」没有留下来讨论这个问题。
这一场以及好几桩类似的事，叫南妮认真掂量起自己的处境来，她偷偷跑去请教她的神谕——沃尔什教授——问她前程如何。
「至少还得一年我们才能结婚，安娜。」他一副教训人的口气说。南妮闻言睁大了眼睛。
「当然，爱德华。」她乖乖地依了。
「你何不去你女儿那儿住些日子？」他提议道。「总跟一个亲戚住得太久，总归不妥。」
南妮狠狠咽了口唾沫，露西结婚时她写的那封信的旧事涌上心头。
「可是，爱德华，我跟你说过的，我和露西之间是怎么个情形。你知道母亲后来对她离家嫁给外乡人是怎么个态度。」
「别急，安娜。别这么激动。记住我常跟你说的话：『仪态的沉静、神色的安稳，是良好教养的显著标志。』我正要说，如今你母亲去世了，正是给你女儿写信、同她恢复旧日关系的好时机。她对我们大有用处，再说，母女也不该长久分离。」
「我对露西确实没有恶感。」南妮承认，神色有些不安。
「正是，正是。一封信写得得体，多半能把事情料理得各方都满意。

===Original===

You might advert to the fact that your mother persisted in a hostile attitude and that you were dependent on her. Also you had best adopt a generous tone.”
“Very well, Edward,” agreed Nannie.
“Good. Now that is settled. You had better write at once, my dear. And you might let me see the letter before sending it.”
Nannie wrote that night.
The letter began vaguely. Mrs. Lockhart’s memory was invoked. Forgiveness was offered, though for what was not specified, a reconciliation was taken for granted, and a visit to Lucy proposed in terms that were difficult to ignore or refuse.
Nannie pathetically reminded Lucy that Arthur paid no alimony, and recounted indignantly the heartlessness of the Sheldons. The letter contained no mention of Professor Walsh. “I am thrown on the world,” concluded the epistle. “Your father has deserted me and my own relatives have threatened to put me in the street.”
In a few days came Lucy’s reply. It was an invitation to pass an indefinite time at Rosedene.

===Chinese===

你可以提一提你母亲如何固执地抱着敌对态度，而你如何仰赖于她。还有，信里的口气最好宽厚一些。」
「好的，爱德华。」南妮应道。
「这就对了。事情就这么定了。你最好马上就写，亲爱的。写完不妨先给我过过目再寄。」
南妮当晚就写了。
信的开头含含糊糊。先搬出对洛克哈特太太的追念。继而提出宽恕——至于宽恕什么，却没说明——和解被视为理所当然，又提出要去露西家住上一段没有期限的日子，措辞叫人难以回绝、也不好不理。
南妮可怜巴巴地提醒露西，亚瑟一个子儿赡养费也不出，又愤愤地数落谢尔登家如何凉薄。信中只字未提沃尔什教授。「我被抛到了世上，」这封信收尾道，「你父亲抛弃了我，我自家的亲戚威胁着要把我赶到街上去。」
几天后露西的回信来了。那是一份请帖，请她到罗斯登住上不限期的日子。

## V / 五
===Original===

Lucy was born about a year after the Merwent wedding.
Although everything was normal and the baby a fine healthy child, Nannie persistently vowed that she had gone through an experience never before equalled and that she could never have another child.
Toward the end of their wedding journey, over some slight misunderstanding at a hotel, Nannie had given way to a hysterical fit of passion that amazed and dismayed her husband, but this was nothing compared with the scene when she discovered that she was pregnant. Tears, screams, striking Arthur in the face, with threats of killing herself, him, and the child were only the beginnings of the drama. However, Arthur had by this time begun to perfect his attitude of non-reaction to the stimuli she employed, and went to his office unworried.
Nannie afterward in a thousand ways suggested that the coming of the child was in the nature of a crime and a calamity, and that Arthur was to blame for it. Nannie’s father died a month before her confinement and she even implied that Arthur was responsible for this coincidence. To add to her sense of disappointment and injury Mr. Merwent senior, who had been ill for some time, died also, leaving an involved estate, and the hope that Nannie had secretly treasured of inheriting his money died with him. Arthur’s future, from the Lockhart standpoint, had ceased to be. Mrs. Lockhart, calling to ascertain the truth of the rumor already circulated by gossiping neighbors, met Nannie’s tearful announcement with, “I always thought so!” and when Arthur entered the house a few moments later, she greeted him with marked coldness. After her mother’s departure Nannie turned on him with fresh reproaches.
“Now we are penniless and you don’t seem to care!” she exclaimed.
Arthur did not reply.
“I seem to be the only one who ever thinks about the baby’s future. You haven’t opened your mouth since we got the letter!”
Arthur took out a cigar and lighted it.
“O‑oh! *Why* don’t you say something? I’ll go crazy!” she almost screamed.
“I don’t see just what there is to say,” Arthur answered quietly, and left the room.
Nannie’s bitterness was accentuated by the fact that Arthur could not afford to buy the Lockhart residence which had to be sold to clear her own father’s estate, and she was forced to see the property pass into the hands of “Cousin Minnie Sheldon” whom she cordially hated.

===Chinese===

露西出生在默温特夫妇婚后一年光景。
虽然一切顺遂，婴儿也是个健康漂亮的孩子，南妮却一口咬定，自己经受了一场前所未有的劫难，往后再也不能生育了。
蜜月旅行临近结束时，为着旅馆里一点小小的误会，南妮曾发作过一场歇斯底里的狂怒，惊得她丈夫目瞪口呆；可比起她发现自己怀孕时的那一场，这又算不得什么了。眼泪、尖叫、掌捆亚瑟（Arthur）的脸、扬言要杀了自己、杀了他、杀了孩子——这些还只是这出戏的开场。好在亚瑟这时已开始练就一套以不动制万动的功夫，对她施展的种种刺激一概不作反应，照旧无忧无虑地上他的事务所去。
此后南妮又千方百计地暗示：这个孩子的降生无异于一桩罪孽、一场灾祸，而罪魁祸首就是亚瑟。南妮的父亲在她临产前一个月去世，她甚至影射这桩巧合也得怪亚瑟。雪上加霜的是，缠绵病榻已久的老默温特先生（Mr. Merwent senior）也去世了，留下一笔乱作一团的家产——南妮暗自怀着的继承他钱财的希望，也随他一同埋进了土里。在洛克哈特家（Lockhart）的眼里，亚瑟的前程从此告吹了。洛克哈特太太（Mrs. Lockhart）上门来打听邻里闲话早已传开的那个消息是真是假，迎头听见南妮哭哭啼啼的宣告，只回了句：「我早料到了！」片刻后亚瑟进屋，她招呼他时的那份冷淡再明白不过。母亲一走，南妮又冲他发作起新的责难。
「这下我们一贫如洗了，你倒好像满不在乎！」她嚷道。
亚瑟不作声。
「这孩子的将来，好像只有我一个人惦记。信来了之后你连嘴都没张过一回！」
亚瑟掏出一支雪茄，点上了。
「哦——！你怎么就*不*说句话呀？我要疯了！」她几乎是尖叫了。
「我看不出这有什么可说的。」亚瑟平静地答了一句，走出房去。
更让南妮苦涩的是，洛克哈特家的宅子必须卖掉来清偿她父亲的遗产债务，亚瑟又拿不出钱来买下它，她只得眼睁睁看着这份产业落到「表亲明妮·谢尔登（Minnie Sheldon）」手里——一个她打心眼儿里憎恨的人。

===Original===

The spirit of rivalry between Nannie and her cousin, the fruit of a childhood antipathy, had reached its climax in a contest for the affections of the well-to-do young business man who afterward became Minnie’s husband, and when he and his wife took formal possession of Nannie’s ancestral home she felt it as the cruel affirmation of her first defeat.
Mrs. Lockhart, who on previous occasions had not hesitated to express her own disapproval of “Cousin Minnie,” after feeble and unconvincing protestations to the effect that she did not wish to inconvenience her prosperous relatives, accepted a grudging offer which allowed her to remain in the old place on the bounty of its new owner.
Little Lucy was weaned soon after she was born, for Nannie declared that she could not nurse a baby. By great good fortune, however, sterilized cow’s milk agreed with the child and she thrived, thanks to the devoted care of old Martha who came to live with Nannie and Arthur and “bring up” their baby. Nannie continually quarreled with the old negress but Aunt Martha stayed on, partly from a habit of allegiance to the family and partly from real devotion to little Lucy.
Nannie gave scant attention to her baby until other people began to notice and praise the child, when she promptly asserted her proprietorship, pointing out with great pride the little thing’s remarkable feats and insisting that Lucy be given to her whenever visitors were present. Nevertheless, the child preferred Aunt Martha and even Arthur, as the latter often held his daughter during the evenings and sang lullabies to her. Nannie resented this deeply, and it made her secretly furious to see Lucy toddle toward him as he came into the room. She often said things to drive Arthur out of the house so that he need not divide the child’s affections.
Lucy did not begin to go to school until she was eight years old. Even then her mother objected to the separation, and in the little girl’s absence wandered restlessly about the house. Lucy, on her return, was covered with kisses. Acquaintances remarked on the unusual affection between the two, although Nannie spoke crossly and often cruelly to the child when they were alone, and not infrequently struck her. In attitude she placed herself on an equality with Lucy and at times depended on the little girl’s judgment and ideas.

===Chinese===

南妮和她这位表亲之间的争强斗胜，本是孩提时代结下的宿怨，后来在一场争夺战中达到顶点——争夺的是那位后来成了明妮丈夫的殷实青年商人的心。待到他和妻子正式住进南妮娘家的祖宅，她觉得这正是对她初次失利最残忍的确认。
洛克哈特太太——她从前倒是从不迟疑地表露过自己对「表亲明妮」的不以为然——这回在心虚气短、全无说服力地表白了一番不愿打搅阔亲戚之后，便接受了一项勉勉强强的条件，靠着新主人的恩典继续住在老宅里。
小露西出生后不久就断了奶，因为南妮宣称自己奶不了孩子。所幸天可怜见，消毒牛奶合这孩子的肠胃，她越长越壮实——这全亏了老玛莎尽心尽力的照料。玛莎大婶（Aunt Martha）搬来同南妮和亚瑟一起住，替他们「带」孩子。南妮不断同这位老黑人妇人拌嘴，可玛莎大婶还是留了下来，一半是出于对这个家族世代效忠的习惯，一半是真心疼爱小露西。
起初南妮很少理会自己的婴儿，等到旁人开始留意并夸赞这孩子了，她立刻摆出主人的架势，得意洋洋地指给人看小家伙的种种了不起的本事，并且坚持：只要有客人在场，露西就得抱到她怀里来。可孩子偏喜欢玛莎大婶，连亚瑟也比她亲——晚间他常抱着女儿，给她唱摇篮曲。南妮对此怀恨在心，每见亚瑟一进屋、露西就摇摇晃晃朝他扑过去，她心里就暗暗冒火。她常常说些话把亚瑟撵出家门，免得他分走孩子的爱。
露西直到八岁才上学。即便到那时，她母亲还嫌这一别难捱，小姑娘不在家的时候，就在屋里坐立不安地转来转去。露西一回来，就被亲个满脸。熟人们都称道这母女俩感情深得少见——尽管母女独处时，南妮对孩子说话又凶又常常狠毒，动手打她也是常有的事。她摆出一副同露西平起平坐的姿态，有时还要仰仗小姑娘的判断和主意。

===Original===

Nannie could never help Lucy with her lessons at night, saying that the figures in the book made her head ache, and if Arthur attempted to offer any assistance a situation was usually precipitated that resulted in driving him from the house. Left alone with the little girl, Nannie played and sang, or produced candy from some hiding place, while she persuaded the child to talk to her about teachers and schoolmates. She enjoyed giving her piano lessons because Arthur knew nothing of music. However, she never wanted Lucy to try to sing and always insisted that her daughter had no voice for singing.
Nannie, however, resented Lucy’s growing self-sufficiency as the girl became graver of manner and expressed herself positively on all sorts of subjects.
Arthur’s law practice did not increase and, as there were no more expectations from his father’s estate, it was decided to discharge Aunt Martha. After the old woman had gone Nannie made a few halfhearted experiments at cooking, but soon relegated this undertaking to her daughter, who at sixteen was virtually the head of the domestic establishment.
But, though Lucy was responsible under this arrangement for the selection and quantity of food bought for the family, Mrs. Merwent reserved for herself the prerogative of giving orders to the groceryman. The young solicitor, who always made a punctual appearance, had soft brown eyes and a beautiful complexion. Nannie never opened the back door to him without first looking into the mirror.
“Are you sure your eggs are fresh today?” she would ask coquettishly. She tapped him on the arm with the pencil she held as she objected to the exorbitant price of bacon.
Lucy, who was annoyed by his habit of staring, expressed her dislike.
“Well, *you* don’t have to give the orders to him!” was her mother’s pettish answer.
Lucy worked too hard and, as a result, became ill. Arthur was away at the time. Nannie called the family physician and annoyed the girl with useless attentions. The doctor telegraphed Merwent that his daughter’s condition was serious. Arthur returned on the first train and, arriving at the house, found Nannie walking the floor with senseless gestures. He went into the sick room and seated himself beside Lucy’s bed. His quiet, emotionless manner seemed to drive Nannie to distraction.

===Chinese===

晚上南妮从来没法辅导露西做功课，说是课本上的数字叫她头疼；而亚瑟若想帮上一把，多半会闹出一场事端，最后以他被撵出家门收场。
单独同小姑娘在一起时，南妮就又弹又唱，或是从哪个藏东西的地方摸出糖来，哄着孩子跟她讲老师同学的事。她乐意亲自教露西弹钢琴，因为亚瑟对音乐一窍不通。但她从不许露西试着唱歌，一口咬定女儿没有唱歌的嗓子。
然而随着露西举止越来越持重、对种种事情都敢于直陈己见，南妮又怨恨起女儿日渐的自立自主来。
亚瑟的律师业务不见起色，父亲的遗产那边也不再有什么指望，家里便决定辞退玛莎大婶。老太太走后，南妮三心二意地试着做过几回饭，很快就把这摊子事推给了女儿——十六岁的露西，实际上已经是一家内务的主心骨。
不过，虽说在这个安排下，家里买什么吃的、买多少都归露西操心，默温特太太却给自己保留了向食品杂货铺发号施令的特权。那年轻伙计总是踩着钟点到，一双温和的棕色眼睛，气色好得很。南妮每次给他开后门之前，总要先照一照镜子。
「你敢担保今天的鸡蛋新鲜吗？」她会娇滴滴地问。她一边抱怨咸肉贵得离谱，一边用手里的铅笔轻轻敲他的胳膊。
露西烦他那个爱盯人瞧的毛病，把厌恶说了出来。
「哼，又不是*你*非要向他订货！」母亲赌气地回她。
露西操劳过度，终于病倒了。其时亚瑟正出门在外。南妮请来家庭医生，又用种种无用的殷勤烦扰着病人。医生给默温特拍去电报，说他女儿病情严重。亚瑟搭最早一班车赶回，一进家门，只见南妮在屋里团团打转，比划着不知所云的手势。他走进病房，在露西床边坐下。他那安静、不动声色的样子，几乎要把南妮逼疯。

===Original===

Her chief resentment, however, seemed to arise from the fact that he did not comfort her but only concerned himself with Lucy.
“Are you going to eat your dinner, or sit there all night?” she asked, her voice trembling with vindictiveness.
Arthur went into the dining room without answering. Nannie shut the door between them. She would not eat because she did not want to leave Arthur and Lucy alone together, and the doctor was obliged to order her to begin her meal. When she did go, however, her appetite was as hearty as usual.
After a few critical days Lucy’s condition improved and on the fourth morning, when the crisis was passed, she smiled at Arthur. Nannie, in the room at the time, bent over the bed.
“Why don’t you smile at Nannie?” she asked accusingly, and Lucy smiled at her mother too.
Arthur, as was his wont when he saw the approach of a useless scene, left the room.
During the convalescence Nannie invented a thousand meaningless attentions with which she endeavored to fix upon herself the regard of the invalid, but as Lucy grew stronger and began to walk about Nannie forgot to flutter around her and their life together resumed its former course.

===Chinese===

不过她最恨的，似乎是他竟不来安慰她，只把心思放在露西身上。
「你是吃饭呢，还是要在那儿坐一整夜？」她问，声音里颤着怨毒。
亚瑟一言不发地走进餐厅。南妮把两人之间的门关上了。她不肯吃饭，因为不想留亚瑟和露西单独在一起，医生只得吩咐她开始用餐。不过等她真吃起来时，胃口倒还和往常一样好。
捱过几天险期，露西的病情好转了。第四天早晨，危险期一过，她朝亚瑟笑了。南妮当时也在房里，俯身到床前。
「你怎么不朝南妮笑呀？」她带着责问的口气说。露西便也朝母亲笑了。
亚瑟一见又要上演一场无谓的戏，便照他的老习惯，离开了房间。
养病期间，南妮变着法儿造出千百样毫无意义的殷勤，想赢得病人的心；可等露西身子硬朗些、能下地走动了，南妮便忘了围着她打转，母女俩的日子又照旧轨运转下去。

## VI / 六
===Original===

Even after reaching the age when young girls begin to notice the opposite sex, Lucy preferred girls to men. She also delighted in caring for younger children and babies. Nannie laughed at Lucy for not having beaux.
“I had dozens at your age,” she would say.
The most common cause of misunderstanding between the mother and daughter was Nannie’s trick of regarding every remark about other people, or about human nature in general, as a covert slur on herself. Each mention of an ignoble quality brought forth, “That’s just like me,” from Nannie, while any reference to an admirable characteristic was greeted with, “I haven’t got that.”
“I wish Mamma wasn’t that way,” Lucy confided to her father one night, after a number of irritating experiences with this mania. “It makes things so unpleasant.” The evening had ended in a quarrel and Nannie had gone upstairs to cry.
“Your mother has every defect of character that can be mentioned,” Arthur replied, “so I suppose she shouldn’t be blamed for being sensitive.”
Lucy was surprised by the bitter feeling in the tone of her usually silent and impassive father, and remembered his remark.
Nannie spent a great deal of time in beauty culture. She tried all sorts of exercises and devices for preserving her figure and warding off wrinkles. Every recipe she saw in the women’s columns of the newspapers she tested carefully. Each day a large part of the morning was devoted to a faithful observance of these rites, and she never neglected to take her “beauty nap” during the afternoon. Often she would gaze with envy at Lucy’s charming color and clear eyes.
“I don’t want to grow old and ugly, Lucy,” she would say. “I’m so afraid of growing old! Do you think I am beginning to show my age?”
“Why, Mamma, what nonsense! You look like a girl,” Lucy would reply, for Nannie’s efforts were not without a certain kind of effect.
“Do you really think so?” she would ask happily. “Don’t tell anybody I use rouge, will you?”
“I won’t,” Lucy always promised.
After taking up her residence with “Cousin Minnie Sheldon” Mrs. Lockhart seldom visited her daughter.
One summer afternoon, however, a carriage stopped before the Merwents’ gate.
“Why, I believe that’s Mamma coming here,” Nannie whispered excitedly, as the carriage door was opened. “There’s a young man with her!” she added. “Who in the world can it be? You stay here to receive them, Lucy.

===Chinese===

露西到了少女们开始留意异性的年纪，仍旧更喜欢同姑娘们来往，不喜欢男人。她还喜欢照看小孩子和婴儿。南妮则笑话露西没有男朋友。
「我在你这么大的时候，追求者有一打呢。」她总这么说。
母女之间最常起的误会，是南妮有个毛病：凡是谈到别人的长短，或是泛论人性，她都当作是含沙射影地糟践自己。提到哪样下作的品性，南妮必定接一句「这不就是说我嘛」；提到哪样可贵的品质，她则回一句「我可没有这个」。
「真希望妈妈不是这样。」一天晚上，被这个毛病气了一晚上之后，露西向父亲吐露，「这样家里多难过啊。」那晚以一场争吵收场，南妮上楼哭去了。
「你母亲身上集齐了人说得出的一切性格缺陷，」亚瑟答道，「这么想来，她多心也怪不得她。」
父亲平日沉默寡言、不动声色，话里这一股怨气让露西吃了一惊，把这句话记住了。
南妮在美容养颜上花去大量工夫。她试用各种体操和法子来保身材、防皱纹。妇女栏里见到的每一个方子她都仔细试过。每天上午的大部分时间她都虔诚地奉行这些仪轨，下午的「美容觉」也从不耽误。她还常带着艳羡端详露西那动人的气色和清亮的眼睛。
「我可不想又老又丑，露西，」她总说，「我真是怕老！你看我是不是开始显老了？」
「哎呀，妈妈，胡说什么！你看着跟大姑娘似的。」露西总这样回答，因为南妮的功夫也确实没有白费。

Tell Mamma I’ll be down in a minute,” and she ran upstairs to change her dress and improve her complexion.

The young man remained in the background, ill at ease.
“This is Kingsley Dodd,” Mrs. Lockhart announced, waving him forward. “He saw you at your father’s office and has given me no peace till I brought him over to call on you.”
Young Dodd, red to his hair, bowed and shook hands awkwardly.
“Where’s Nannie?” Mrs. Lockhart asked, entering the parlor and seating herself. “Sit down, Kingsley,” she commanded her follower.
“Mamma will be down in a minute,” explained Lucy.
Nannie delayed many minutes, and Lucy became more and more self-conscious under the influence of her grandmother’s continued scrutiny and the confusion of the new acquaintance, answering briefly his timid observations and Mrs. Lockhart’s peremptory questions.
At last Nannie appeared on the stairs in her best afternoon frock.
“Why, Mamma,” she exclaimed, “how nice of you! I’m so glad to see you.”
“You kept us waiting long enough if you *are* glad.”
“I was in my bath,” lied Nannie, “and couldn’t come sooner.”
Lucy looked at her mother.
“Well, I’m glad you still take baths,” remarked Mrs. Lockhart as Nannie kissed her.
Nannie laughed.

“Yes indeed!” Nannie smiled her sweetest and shook hands with Kingsley. “We’re delighted to know you, too,” she declared as she beamed on the youth.
“Nannie, I want to see that old dresser your father gave you. We’re furnishing a guest room in antiques and maybe it will just fit in. If it does, I’ll give you another,” said Mrs. Lockhart after a brief conversation.

Mrs. Lockhart frowned at her meaningly.

“Young Kingsley is greatly taken with Lucy,” explained Mrs. Lockhart, coming to the point at once as soon as they were out of hearing of Lucy and her guest. “You know all Judge Dodd’s money will go to Kingsley. It’s Lucy’s great chance.

Nannie lost her temper.

Lucy’s gaze did not waver. “Yes, I shall,” she said steadily.
Nannie burst into tears. Lucy hesitated a moment before she continued.

Nannie made no reply and went on sobbing. Looking rather miserable, Lucy left her and went to the kitchen to prepare supper.

The day after Kingsley Dodd’s presentation Nannie paid a visit to her mother.
“Lucy, your grandmother wants you to go over and see her in the morning,” Nannie announced on her return.
“All right, Mamma,” Lucy acceded without hesitation.
The following morning Lucy made her appearance at the Sheldon home with her customary punctuality.
“Well, well, it’s a long time since my grandbaby came to see me,” began Mrs. Lockhart kindly as Lucy entered the library where she sat tatting.
“It’s been a long time since I was asked,” Lucy answered simply.
“Yes, of course,” agreed her grandmother hastily. “I haven’t a home of my own, as you know, Lucy, and can’t always do as I would like to.”

金斯利·多德引见之后的第二天，南妮去了她母亲家。
「露西，你外祖母要你明天上午过去见她。」南妮回来后宣布。
「好的，妈妈。」露西毫不迟疑地应了。
第二天上午，露西照她一贯的守时，出现在谢尔登家门。
「哎哟，我的外孙女儿可是好久没来看我喽。」露西走进书房时，坐在里面织花边的洛克哈特太太和气地开腔了。
「也好久没人请我来了。」露西简单地回答。
「是，那是自然，」外祖母赶忙附和，「露西，你知道，我自己没有一个家，想做什么也未必做得了。」

===Original===

Lucy did not reply to this but continued to regard her grandmother expectantly.
“You remember what I said yesterday?” resumed Mrs. Lockhart briskly. “Well, we’ve begun to make the arrangements for a little dance in your honor. It’s high time you were introduced to society.”
“I don’t know whom you want to introduce me to, Grandmamma. Everybody in town knows me already.”
“Oh, you know what I mean, Lucy. You ought to take your place as a young lady, and this is the nicest way to do it.”
“Thank you, Grandmamma, but I’d rather not have the dance.”
“Stuff and nonsense! I never saw a young girl yet who didn’t love parties and attentions. We’ll consider it all settled then.”
“No, we won’t, Grandmamma. I am not going to have a party of any kind.”
“Lucy, you’re crazy, my child. Why people will think you’re queer. I’ve already told several people of our plans.”
“I don’t dance, Grandmamma.”
“What? Well, Nannie must have paid lots of attention to your education! I’ll have a little talk with her. The idea of a young lady who can’t dance! Well, we’ll have a little bridge party then.”
“I don’t play bridge, either.”
“Don’t play bridge!” Mrs. Lockhart eyed her granddaughter quizzically. “Then we’ll have just an old fashioned party,” she said at length.
“Thank you, Grandmamma, but I don’t want the party,” reiterated Lucy.
“I really believe you don’t,” admitted Mrs. Lockhart with a grim smile, “but we’ll have it anyway. What night next week shall we decide on?”
“I don’t want to seem ungrateful, Grandmamma, but I’m not coming to any party.”
Mrs. Lockhart stared at Lucy only to encounter a gaze as firm as her own.
“Very well, Lucy,” she said at last in an icy tone. “I had intended to do a great many things for you, but if you want to throw away your chances like this I can do nothing. You evidently don’t want either friends or relations. You must excuse me now as I’m going out.” And Mrs. Lockhart rose and left the room.
Lucy made her way through the hall alone and went into the street.

Thursday evening came and Kingsley Dodd appeared very promptly. The day had been rainy and the remaining clouds were tinged crimson by the setting sun. The windows of the Merwents’ house were open and as the visitor went up the walk he saw Lucy lighting the gas jets in the parlor.

星期四晚上到了，金斯利·多德如约而至，来得非常准时。白天下过雨，残云被落日染成绯红。默温特家的窗子开着，客人走上小径时，看见露西正在客厅里点煤气灯。

===Original===

She was the only one ready to receive him, for Nannie had not yet completed her careful toilette and Arthur was away from home, being engaged on a legal case out of town.
Nannie soon descended the stairs, however, and the three sat down to supper. During the meal she and young Dodd became such friends that it was with an obvious effort that she excused herself, when they had risen from the table, and returned to her bedroom, leaving the young people together.
“We’re going to have an all-day picnic out at Dad’s country place next Saturday,” Dodd observed, after some ordinary conversation, “and I want you to come. Your grandmother and Colonel and Mrs. Mainter and Dad and Aunt Sally are going to be the chaperones, and we’ll have a great time. I’ll come around with a single buggy and my bays and drive you out. The rest are going in surreys and on horseback. We better start early so as to﻿—”
“Thank you, Mr. Dodd,” Lucy stopped him, “but I shan’t be able to go.”
“Why, have you got something on for Saturday?” he asked. “Well, I’ll have the thing put off till next week.”
“No, it’s not another engagement but I can’t go because﻿—”
“Because you don’t like picnics,” he laughed. “All right. We’ll get up something at the house here in town. What shall it be?”
Lucy opened her lips to speak, but hesitated. At the same instant a gust of wind blew through the room and one of the window curtains fluttered out and threatened to overturn some ornaments on a nearby table. She hastened to secure the drapery.
“Let me fix it!” he offered, starting toward her. Lucy turned to face him as he reached her side.
“It’s fixed, thank you,” she began, then hesitated, flushing painfully. “Mr. Dodd,” she met his eyes steadily, “I know you mean to be nice in giving me the picnic, and coming here to see me, and all that. We are glad to have you and I hope we shall always be friends, but Grandmamma is trying to throw us at each other’s heads, and I won’t have it!”
Kingsley stared at her a moment. His glance fell.
“I couldn’t help it,” she resumed, finally. “I had to say it. I hope we will be friends, but if you are angry I can’t help that either.”
“I guess I’d better go.” Kingsley’s face was a dull brick red. Lucy stood in the same place while he took his hat from the hall tree, and she did not move until the front door had closed behind him.
“Lucy! Lucy!”

===Chinese===

只有她一个人准备迎客——南妮那套仔细的梳妆还没收拾停当，亚瑟出远门办案去了，不在家。
不过南妮很快就下了楼，三人入席用晚饭。饭桌上她同小多德一见如故，及至离席之后，她竟像是很费了些劲才告了退，回自己卧室去，好把两个年轻人留在一起。
「下星期六，我们要在我爸的乡下宅子办一整天的野餐会，」寒暄了几句之后，多德说，「我想请你去。你外祖母、梅因特上校夫妇、我爸和萨莉姑妈都来充当监护，咱们一定痛快玩一场。我赶着一匹马的轻车、带着我那对枣红马来接你。其余的人坐大马车或骑马去。咱们最好早点动身，好——」
「谢谢你，多德先生，」露西打断他，「可我去不了。」
「哎呀，星期六你有安排？那好办，我把日子推到下星期。」
「不，不是另有安排，是我去不了，因为——」
「因为你不爱野餐，」他笑道。「行啊。那咱们就在城里的宅子里安排点什么。安排什么好呢？」
露西张了张嘴，又迟疑了。恰在此时，一阵风灌进屋来，一幅窗帘飘了起来，眼看要碰翻旁边桌上摆的几件摆设。她急忙过去按住帘子。
「我来！」他自告奋勇，朝她走去。他走到她跟前时，露西转过身来面对着他。
「弄好了，谢谢你。」她开口道，随即又迟疑起来，脸涨得通红。「多德先生，」她坦然迎上他的目光，「我知道，你请我野餐、上门来看我，都是一片好意。我们欢迎你来，也希望咱们永远是朋友。可外祖母正拼命把咱们往一处撮合，我不答应！」
金斯利愣愣地看了她一会儿，垂下了目光。
「我控制不了，」她终于接着说，「这话我不得不说。我希望咱们做朋友，可你要是恼了，我也没办法。」
「我看我该走了。」金斯利的脸红成了猪肝色。他到门厅衣帽架上取帽子时，露西原地站着没动，直到前门在他身后关上，她才挪步。
「露西！露西！」

===Original===

Nannie called almost instantly, as she leaned over the stair rail in the upper hall, “Are you and Mr. Dodd going out?”
“He’s gone,” Lucy announced, coming out of the parlor and looking up into her mother’s anxious face.
“Why, what in the world’s the matter?” Nannie asked agitatedly, descending the stairs as she spoke.
“Nothing’s the matter, Mamma, except that I don’t intend to let you and Grandmamma dispose of me as though I were an animal.”
“Did you say such an awful thing to Kingsley?” gasped Nannie, wringing her hands.
“Not exactly,” replied Lucy, almost smiling in spite of herself. “I told him we were being thrown at each other and I wouldn’t permit it.”
“Why, how could you say such a thing as that!” she protested. “Was he mad?” Then, before Lucy could answer, “Why couldn’t you have waited till you saw whether you liked him or not?”
“I don’t need to wait, Mamma.”
“But, Lucy, there are other things to be considered.”
“No, there aren’t, Mamma.”
Nannie’s tone changed.
“Well, I think you might consider me a little at any rate!”
Lucy eyed her mother squarely.
“I don’t, Mamma﻿—not in this matter. I’m willing to work for you.”
“You are certainly an ungrateful child, Lucy!” Lucy winced. “Nobody knows what I sacrificed when I married your father. He has done nothing and nobody pays any attention to him. His father died with everything in a muddle and he’s never made anything for himself. All the society or notice I get is through my own people, and now when they are disposed to take you up, and give us a chance to be somebody, you won’t even look at one of their friends! I should think you would be glad of the chance to help your family. You would if you had any feelings at all!”
Lucy was white. Without answering she walked past her mother and went upstairs to her room.

===Chinese===

南妮几乎立刻就在楼上门厅里倚着楼梯栏杆喊了起来，「你和多德先生要出门吗？」
「他走了。」露西从客厅出来，仰头望着母亲那焦急的脸，宣告道。
「哎呀，到底出什么事了？」南妮激动地问，一面说一面下楼。
「没出什么事，妈妈，无非是我不打算让你和外祖母把我当牲口一样发落。」
「你对金斯利说了那么难听的话？」南妮绞着双手，倒抽一口气。
「也不算难听，」露西答道，几乎忍不住要笑。「我告诉他，有人在把咱们往一处撮合，而我不容许这样。」
「哎呀，这种话你怎么说得出囗！」她抗议道。「他恼了吗？」不等露西回答，她又问：「你干吗不等一等，看看自己喜欢不喜欢他再说呢？」
「我不需要等，妈妈。」
「可是，露西，还有别的要考虑呢。」
「没有别的，妈妈。」
南妮变了口气。
「哼，我看你怎么也该顾念我一点吧！」
露西正眼望着母亲。
「我不——在这件事上，妈妈。为您出力干活，我愿意。」
「露西，你可真是个没良心的孩子！」露西浑身一震。「谁也不知道我嫁你父亲牺牲了什么。他一事无成，也没人把他放在眼里。他父亲死的时候留下一团乱麻，他自己又从没挣出个名堂。我这点社交、这点体面，全是从我娘家得来的；如今他们有心抬举你，给咱们一个出人头地的机会，你却连他们的一个朋友都不屑一顾！我以为你该乐得有这个机会帮衬家里。你要是还有半点心肝，早该愿意了！」
露西脸色煞白。她一言不发，从母亲身边走过，上楼回自己房间去了。

## VII / 七
===Original===

Late on the night of Kingsley Dodd’s visit Arthur returned from his trip, and the next morning he had only time for a hasty greeting to his family before leaving to keep an early appointment with the out-of-town client who engaged his services at the moment. An hour after his departure Lucy appeared in the hall with her hat on and her gloves and sunshade in her hand.
“Where are you going, Lucy?” Nannie demanded.
“I’m going to Papa’s office,” replied Lucy as she passed out the door.
When Lucy reached her father’s place of business she found Mr. Merwent alone. As his daughter entered he was reading a letter. He put it into a drawer in his desk and turned the lock.
“Well, Lucy?” He leaned back in his chair and smiled his slow smile as he spoke.
She gazed around on the dingy furniture, the old books, the discolored steel engravings, the dusty floor, and other signals of her father’s rundown law practice. Her heart almost failed her, but the memory of the preceding day rushed back upon her.
“Papa,” she began, “I want to earn my own living.”
Her father scrutinized her face kindly.
“I don’t blame you, speaking in general,” he observed, “but why have you so suddenly decided it?”
“Mamma and Grandmamma are trying to marry me to Kingsley Dodd in spite of myself, and Mamma thinks I am ungrateful because I object. I don’t think I ought to be dependent on anyone any longer.”
“You seem to have all the self-respect in the family, Lucy,” Mr. Merwent commented, “but what can you do?”
“I can learn book binding,” she responded without hesitation. “The pay for fine tooled hand binding is good. Mamie Willis, who used to be in my class at high school, has been to Chicago Art School to learn it. I think I should like to go there. I want to go right away.”
“Let me think, Daughter,” he said meditatively. “I have a friend I should like to consult about it. It is very possible that it may be the best thing. We’ll talk it over again tomorrow. Come down here about this time and we’ll decide.”
Lucy kissed her father, amazed at his complaisance.
“I wonder who the friend he spoke of is,” she said to herself as she went back to the house. “Papa’ll probably have to borrow money of him to send me away.”
---
The following day Mrs. Merwent preserved an air of gentle sadness and grief, replying softly to her daughter’s remarks but avoiding any reference to what had happened.

===Chinese===

金斯利·多德（Kingsley Dodd）来访的那个深夜，亚瑟出差回来了。第二天一早，他只来得及同家人匆匆打个照面，便出门赴一个早约——眼下聘他办事的是位外地委托人。
他走后一个钟头，露西就戴着帽子、手里拿着手套和阳伞出现在门厅里。
「你去哪儿，露西？」南妮问。
「我去爸爸的事务所。」露西边答边出了门。
露西到父亲办公的地方时，默温特先生正独自一人。女儿进来时他在读一封信。他把信放进书桌抽屉，转上了锁。
「怎么样，露西？」他仰在椅背上，说话时脸上慢慢漾开他惯常的慢悠悠的笑。
她环顾着昏暗的家具、旧书、变色的钢版蚀刻画、蒙尘的地板，以及父亲那门惨淡律师业的其他种种迹象。她的心几乎要软下来，可前一天的记忆又猛地涌上心头。
「爸爸，」她开口道，「我想自食其力。」
父亲慈和地端详着她的脸。
「就事论事，我不怪你，」他说，「可你怎么这么突然就下了决心？」
「妈妈和外祖母（Grandmamma）非要把我嫁给金斯利·多德，由不得我。我一反对，妈妈就说我忘恩负义。我觉得我不该再依赖任何人了。」
「这一家人的自尊心好像全长在你身上了，露西。」默温特先生议论道，「可你能做什么呢？」
「我可以学书籍装帧。」她毫不犹豫地答道，「精工手工烫印装帧的工钱很高。我中学时同班的玛米·威利斯（Mamie Willis）就上芝加哥艺术学校学过这个。我想我也愿意去。我想马上就去。」
「让我想想，孩子。」他若有所思地说，「我有位朋友，这事我想先同他商量商量。很可能这就是最好的出路。咱们明天再细谈。明天这个钟点你再来，咱们把它定下来。」
露西吻了父亲，对他这份好说话又惊又喜。
「他说的那位朋友究竟是谁呢？」她往家走时暗自琢磨，「八成爸爸得跟他借钱才送得走我。」
---
第二天，默温特太太一直端着一副温婉哀伤的神情，女儿说什么她都轻声应着，只是绝口不提已经发生的事。

===Original===

When Lucy went out a little before the hour appointed for the conference at the office, Nannie did not ask her errand, but returned the girl’s kiss and volunteered a statement that she would not be gone long, with a look of patient melancholy.
“Well, it is all arranged,” were Arthur’s first words, as he greeted Lucy. “You are to go to Chicago where I have planned for you to stay with the relative of a friend. We agree﻿—that is, I agree that you couldn’t do better than go to the Art School, as it is a recognized institution and the courses are reliable.”
“Oh, Papa, thank you so much!”
“The thanks are due to someone else, Lucy, and I hope some day you can thank the friend who has made it possible.”
“Who is it, Papa? Is it someone I know?”
Mr. Merwent looked at her a moment before replying.
“It’s a woman friend, Lucy, Mrs. Ellen Low.”
He continued to regard his daughter. Lucy’s face turned pink.
“I didn’t know you knew her, Papa,” she said.
Mr. Merwent, though he did not take his eyes from the young girl’s, seemed, in his turn, somewhat embarrassed.
“Yes, I’ve known her quite a while. When her husband died I was retained with Mr. Blair to settle up the estate,” he answered.
“Oh!” Lucy relapsed into thoughtful silence.
She knew Mrs. Low by sight and in spite of herself rather liked the pleasant homely face with its strongly marked features, though she had, almost against her will, absorbed some of the prejudiced tone in which she had heard her mother and grandmother make occasional references to the woman.
Mrs. Low was the widow of a handsome man, who had been very fond of the ladies, and she had not lived happily with him. She appeared, in a whimsical way, to hold men in little awe. The unpopularity of her outspoken manner, which voiced a point of view that Russellville found unbecoming in a lady, had sent her to seek a more congenial atmosphere with relatives in Chicago. However, she continued to spend several months of each year in her native town.
“I expect you have accepted the family’s hostility to Mrs. Low, Lucy,” Arthur went on after a minute.
“I’ve hardly ever heard Mamma speak of her,” Lucy returned, her glance drooping before the bitter, slightly amused expression that crept into her father’s face.
“Mrs. Low is too big a woman for her environment. She’s got too much self-respect to be understood by the people in this place.

===Chinese===

到了约定去事务所面谈的钟点之前，露西出门时，南妮没有问她去干什么，只是回了女儿一个吻，还主动说她不会去多久，一脸坚忍的忧郁。
「好了，都安排妥当了。」亚瑟迎接露西时的头一句话便是。「你要去芝加哥，我已打点好让你住在一位朋友的亲戚家里。我们一致认为——确切说，是我认为，上艺术学校是再好不过的了，那是所正规院校，课程也靠得住。」
「啊，爸爸，太谢谢您了！」
「该谢的不是我，露西。但愿有一天你能当面谢谢那位成全这事的朋友。」
「是谁呀，爸爸？是我认识的人吗？」
默温特先生看了她一会儿，才回答。
「是一位女性朋友，露西——埃伦·洛太太（Mrs. Ellen Low）。」
他仍旧端详着女儿。露西的脸红了。
「我不知道您认识她，爸爸。」她说。
默温特先生虽然目光没有离开女儿的眼睛，这会儿倒像是有些局促。
「嗯，我认识她有些年头了。她丈夫去世时，我和布莱尔先生（Mr. Blair）受托清理遗产。」他答道。
「哦！」露西陷入沉思的沉默。
洛太太她见过，而且不由得颇有好感——喜欢那张爽朗家常、轮廓分明的脸；可她也几乎不由自主地沾染了些成见，那是听母亲和外祖母偶尔提起这女人时的口气。
洛太太是个俊美男人的遗孀——那男人生前极好亲近女色，她跟他过得并不快活。她似乎带着几分古怪，对男人不怎么敬畏。她心直口快，发表的那套见解拉塞尔维尔（Russellville）人认为有失淑女身份，招来的物议逼得她去芝加哥投亲，寻个气味相投的环境。不过她每年仍要在故乡住上几个月。
「我看你也随了家里人那份对洛太太的敌意，露西。」过了一分钟亚瑟接着说。
「我几乎没听妈妈说起过她。」露西答道，目光低垂，不敢迎视父亲脸上悄悄浮起的那丝辛酸而微含讥诮的神情。
「洛太太这样的人，她那个环境容不下。她自尊心太强，这地方的人理解不了她。

===Original===

It’s because she’s too bighearted to be prudent, she’s let herself be unpleasantly criticised!” Mr. Merwent’s manner was warm.
Lucy, surprised at her father’s outburst, stared at him in silence, her lips slightly parted. He rose from his chair and walked over to the open window. He stood there for a moment with his back turned, and when he finally reseated himself he appeared as self-contained and emotionless as usual.
“Are﻿—are the people you want me to stay with Mrs. Low’s friends?” Lucy paused, once more embarrassed by her father’s calm scrutiny.
“Her cousin, Miss Storms,” he explained. “She is very well known in Chicago and could do a lot for you, we think.” This time he used the plural pronoun without hesitation. “You haven’t told your mother anything about this yet?”
“No, Papa, I’m afraid Mamma won’t like it.”
“So am I, Daughter. We’ll have to talk it over with her tonight.”
Lucy looked troubled.
“I’m afraid she won’t consent.”
“Yes, she will. You go on back now, and we’ll see after supper.”
Lucy was very thoughtful as she walked home. She had been much astonished to learn that Mrs. Low and her father were acquainted.
“Lucy is going to Chicago to school,” Mr. Merwent announced that night without preamble, toward the end of the evening meal.
Nannie looked up from her plate with a startled expression.
“What in the world are you talking about, Arthur?”
“About Lucy going away to school, as my words implied,” returned Mr. Merwent.
“But who said Lucy was going away?” insisted Nannie.
“I did,” responded her husband shortly.
“Lucy, what is all this about?” asked Nannie, appealing to her daughter.
“It’s about Lucy going away to school. Do you think you can manage to understand or shall I repeat it a few more times?” interrupted Arthur almost menacingly.
Nannie studied the face of her long silent husband and read in it something that experience taught her to be the signal of an occasion when tears, arguments, and tantrums would avail nothing. She rose suddenly and left the table and not long after they heard the front door close as she went out.
“A council of war,” remarked Arthur with a wry smile.
Lucy shut herself in her room and cried.
About nine o’clock Nannie returned from the “big house” as the Sheldon home was called, where she had received neither comfort nor suggestion.
“Don’t ask me to take over the management of your husband too, Nannie!” Mrs.
