就因为她心胸太宽，不知避嫌，才由着人家说三道四！」默温特先生动了感情。
露西没料到父亲会这般动容，默然盯着他，嘴唇微微张开。他从椅子上起身，走到敞开的窗前，背对着人站了片刻；等重新坐下时，又是一副一如既往的内敛、无动于衷的样子。
「那——那您要我跟他们同住的人，是洛太太的朋友吗？」露西顿了顿，又被父亲平静的注视弄得发窘。
「是她的表亲，斯托姆斯小姐（Miss Storms）。」他解释道，「她在芝加哥很有名，我们想，她能帮你不少忙。」这一回，他毫不迟疑地用了「我们」这个字眼。「这事你还没跟你母亲提过吧？」
「没有，爸爸。我怕妈妈不会乐意。」
「我也怕，孩子。今晚只好同她摊开了谈。」
露西面有难色。
「怕她不会答应。」
「会答应的。你现在先回去，晚饭后再说。」
露西往家走，一路心事重重。得知洛太太和父亲相识，她吃惊不小。
「露西要去芝加哥上学。」那天晚上，饭快吃完时，默温特先生开门见山地宣布。
南妮从盘子上抬起头来，一脸惊愕。
「你到底在说什么呀，亚瑟？」
「说我说的露西要出门上学这件事。」默温特先生答道。
「可谁说露西要出门了？」南妮追问。
「我说的。」丈夫简短地应了一声。
「露西，这都是怎么回事？」南妮转而求告女儿。
「就是露西要出门上学这件事。你能不能设法听明白，还是需要我再重复几遍？」亚瑟打断她，口气近乎威吓。
南妮端详着长久沉默的丈夫的脸，从中读出了某种东西——经验告诉她，那标志着一种场合：眼泪、争辩、撒泼全都不管用。她突然起身离席，不多时，前门一响，她出去了。
「开军事会议去了。」亚瑟咧嘴苦笑道。
露西把自己关在房里哭。
九点钟光景，南妮从谢尔登家的「大宅」回来——那地方既没给她安慰，也没给她主意。
「可别叫我把你丈夫也接手管起来，南妮！」洛克哈特太太

===Original===

Lockhart had exclaimed with asperity. “Any effort to help you is wasted because you make no attempt to cooperate.” And Nannie had left in tears.
When it became apparent that both Arthur and her daughter must be taken at their word, curiosity got the better of the secret resolve Nannie had made to show no interest in the matter of Lucy’s future.
“Of course it’s none of my business, Lucy, but I should think you and your father would at least tell me where you are going to stay when you get to Chicago. You don’t know a soul there and you’ve never been away from home in your life.”
“I told you I was going to stay with Miss Storms,” replied Lucy, repeating information she had previously offered, to which Nannie, being in a temper at the time, had refused to listen. “She is the friend of somebody Papa knows,” Lucy hesitated and flushed, “and he wrote and asked her if she’d take me.”
“I don’t know what your father is thinking about to let you go off like this to people he knows nothing of. She may be as common as dirt for all we know. I’d like to find out who the mutual friend is. I notice he don’t mention his name,” Nannie finished with a sneering intonation.
Lucy, her cheeks still scarlet, glanced away but said nothing.
---
During the days in which Lucy was preparing for departure Nannie relapsed into tearful silence and repelled her daughter’s advances and demonstrations until the girl, still not much more than a child in experience, was almost ill and about to relinquish her plan.
At this point a note came from Mrs. Lockhart which spurred Nannie to more decisive speech.
“I want to tell you something,” she announced, calling Lucy to her. “You had better think carefully before you leave home for if you go you can’t come back here! It will be final.”
“But, Mamma, Papa﻿—” Lucy began.
“This is my final decision,” interrupted Nannie in a manner almost ludicrously like Mrs. Lockhart’s.
“Very well, Mamma,” replied Lucy, hardly able to keep back the tears, and choking with a sense of injustice.
Arthur came in at this juncture.
“What’s this, Anna?” he inquired peremptorily.
Nannie was silent and avoided his gaze. He turned to Lucy who told him what had taken place.
“My child shall come to my home as often and as long as she pleases,” he declared. “Lucy, come upstairs. I want to talk with you.”
The father and daughter left the room together.

===Chinese===

厉声嚷道，「帮你也是白帮，因为你自己一点也不肯配合。」南妮是含着泪走的。
等到看明白亚瑟和女儿都是要说到做到的了，南妮暗下的决心——对露西的前途摆出不闻不问的架势——终究抵不过好奇心。
「当然，这不关我的事，露西。可我想，你和你父亲总该告诉我，你到了芝加哥住哪儿吧。那儿你一个人也不认识，你又这辈子没出过门。」
「我说过我要住在斯托姆斯小姐家。」露西答道，把先前说过、而南妮当时正在气头上不肯听的话又说了一遍，「她是爸爸认识的什么人的朋友。」露西迟疑着，脸红了，「爸爸写了信，问她肯不肯收留我。」
「我真不明白你父亲是怎么想的，放你这样跑到素不相识的人家里去。鬼知道她下贱成什么样。我倒想打听打听那位双方共同的朋友是谁。我注意到他可没提人家的大名。」南妮末了带着讥诮的腔调说。
露西双颊仍旧通红，把目光挪开，没有作声。
---
露西打点行装的那些天里，南妮一味含泪缄默，女儿一凑近、一示好就被她推开，闹得这经历上还近乎孩子气的姑娘几乎病倒，差点要放弃自己的打算。
正在这时，洛克哈特太太来了张字条，催得南妮说出了更决绝的话。
「我有话要告诉你。」她把露西叫到跟前宣布道，「离家之前你最好想清楚——你要是走了，就再也回不了这个家！那可是最后的了断。」
「可是妈妈，爸爸——」露西刚开口。
「这是我最后的决定。」南妮打断她，那做派简直滑稽地像足了洛克哈特太太。
「好吧，妈妈。」露西答道，几乎止不住泪，满腔的委屈堵在喉头。
恰在这时亚瑟进来了。
「这是怎么回事，安娜（Anna）？」他厉声问。
南妮不作声，躲开他的目光。他转向露西，露西把方才的事说了。
「我的孩子愿意回我的家多少回、住多久，随她的便。」他宣布道，「露西，上楼来，我有话同你讲。」
父女俩一同出了屋子。

===Original===

Aunt Martha had been called in to help with the arrangements for Lucy’s departure. Nannie heard the old negress’s voice, then the voices of Arthur and Lucy, and the sound of a trunk being moved. Shut in her own room she was conscious of the feet that hurried past her door and the general bustle of packing for a journey. When there was no one about she stepped into the hall and listened. She convinced herself that Lucy would not really take this step but that the appearance of unusual preparation was arranged to deceive and punish her.
After a time, the father and daughter went out, Lucy locking her door. Aunt Martha went home at the same time. Nannie, from a window, watched them until they were out of sight and, notwithstanding her determination to discredit all evidences of Lucy’s decision, began to feel very forlorn.
She prepared herself some luncheon, eating as heartily as usual, but she was convinced that she was heartbroken. She was indeed bothered by the uncertainty and, most of all, by the fact that her daughter and husband were together.
Late in the afternoon Lucy and her father returned with a number of packages.
“Where did he get the money to buy all those things? I wonder what they are?” Nannie said to herself.
She could hear the murmur of their voices in Lucy’s room as they opened the packages and arranged the contents of the trunk and valises. When Lucy descended to the kitchen to prepare supper Nannie tiptoed to the open door of the bedroom and peered in at the still scattered belongings of her daughter. She saw a pair of new shoes and an unfamiliar hat which were a concrete affirmation of all that she had tried to deny.
“Lucy leaves in the morning on the nine fifteen,” volunteered Arthur when the three were seated at their evening meal.
Nannie was speechless. She gave him one scathing look and fled to her room.
Lucy followed her and knocked but Nannie would not open the door. She could be heard sobbing. What disturbed the girl was that the supper she carried up on a tray and left outside her mother’s door was not eaten. At bed time Nannie had not appeared. Lucy kissed her father good night, but when she lay down she could not sleep.
---
When Arthur and Lucy were eating breakfast the next morning an expressman came for the trunk.

===Chinese===

家里把玛莎大婶请来帮忙张罗露西动身的事。南妮听见老黑人妇人的声音，接着是亚瑟和露西的声音，还有挪动衣箱的声响。她把自己关在房里，听着门外匆匆来去的脚步，和收拾行装的一片忙乱。四下无人时，她溜到门厅里侧耳听。她说服了自己：露西并不真要走这一步，这番异乎寻常的打点，不过是摆出来哄她、罚她的。
过了一阵，父女俩出门去了，露西锁上了房门。玛莎大婶也同时回家去了。南妮守在窗口，望着他们走得不见了影，尽管打定主意不把露西成行的种种迹象当真，心里却渐渐涌起一阵说不出的凄凉。
她自己弄了点午饭，吃得照旧津津有味，心里却认定自己已是肝肠寸断。悬而未决的局面确实让她烦心，最烦的是，女儿和丈夫竟站在一边。
傍晚时分，露西和父亲抱着大包小包回来了。
「他哪儿来的钱买这么多东西？不知道都是些什么？」南妮暗自嘀咕。
他们开包裹、往衣箱和旅行袋里归置东西，南妮隔着露西的房门听得见叽叽咕咕的说话声。露西下楼进厨房预备晚饭去了，南妮便蹑手蹑脚凑到那敞开的卧室门口，窥看女儿摊了一摊的东西。她看见一双新鞋子和一顶没见过的新帽子——那是对她一直不肯承认的一切最实在的确认。
「露西明早走，坐九点一刻的车。」三人坐下来吃晚饭时，亚瑟主动说道。
南妮哑口无言。她狠狠剜了他一眼，逃回自己房里去了。
露西跟过去敲门，南妮就是不开。屋里传出她的呜咽。让姑娘不安的是，她用托盘端上楼、搁在母亲门外的晚饭，原封没动。
到了就寝时分南妮还没露面。露西吻过父亲道了晚安，躺下后却怎么也睡不着。
---
第二天早晨，亚瑟和露西正吃早饭，捷运行的人来取衣箱了。

===Original===

Nannie had not yet descended to the dining room and still refused to open her door though Lucy had been to her several times and asked for admittance. When Mrs. Merwent saw the baggage carried away, however, her tragic resolution was broken, and, after hesitating at the head of the stairs, she composed her face to what seemed an appropriate expression of her state of mind and went down.
It was a beautiful late summer morning. The lawn was visible through the half open front door. The grass was parched, and the vines on the porch were beginning to turn yellow. There was a tinge of autumn crispness in the air, but the sunshine that flooded the hall was golden.
Lucy came out of the dining room and laid her gloves, coat, and a little hand bag on the hat stand. She had on the new hat, which was very becoming, and wore a new blue serge frock. She was excited by the unusual prospect of a journey and looked exceptionally pretty.
“Lucy!” Nannie began in an ominous voice. The girl, a little startled, turned to face her mother.
Mrs. Merwent wore a soiled lace negligee. Her hair hung down her back, loose and uncombed. Her habitual precautions to ward off wrinkles and retain her good looks had been neglected. She seemed much older than on the previous day when Lucy had seen her carefully dressed, rouged and with well ordered hair.
“You wicked, selfish, cruel girl!” continued Nannie in high pitched strident tones. “You mean, hard hearted, wretched beast!” She suddenly advanced toward Lucy who retreated in frightened astonishment.
Arthur appeared in the hall doorway.
“That is enough!” he said sternly.
Nannie suddenly lost all self control.
“Get out of my house!” she screamed. “You stinking﻿—!” and a stream of awful invectives that seemed from a brothel poured from her lips. She picked up Lucy’s belongings from the hat stand and threw them against the front door where they fell on the threshold.
“Anna!” Arthur spoke calmly, but he only succeeded in evoking another paroxysm of screams and vile words directed at himself.
“Put your things on, Lucy,” he said, and in a moment the two fairly fled into the street, leaving Nannie in a state of hysteria bordering on madness.
“I can’t go on this way, Papa!” exclaimed Lucy, looking back at the house after they had walked half a block.
“All right. Do as you wish,” he responded without emotion.

===Chinese===

南妮还没下餐厅来，任凭露西去敲了几回门求进，她就是不开。可等默温特太太眼看着行李被搬走，她那悲壮的决心便垮了。她在楼梯口踌躇了一下，把脸色调整到一副同她心境相称的表情，下楼来了。
这是夏末一个晴美的早晨。半开的前门外望得见草坪。草晒得焦黄，廊下的藤蔓也开始转黄了。空气里已有一丝秋天的爽脆，可漫进门厅的阳光是金灿灿的。
露西从餐厅出来，把手套、外套和一只小手包放在衣帽架上。她戴着那顶新帽子——戴着很衬她——身上是新的蓝𪰗叽连衣裙。难得出门旅行，她心里兴奋，人显得格外标致。
「露西！」南妮开口了，声音不祥。姑娘微微一惊，转过脸对着母亲。
默温特太太穿着一件脏了的蕾丝晨衣，头发披散在背上，蓬乱着没有梳。她平日照例防范皱纹、保养容颜的那套功夫全抛下了。比起昨天——那时露西见她衣装齐整、涂着胭脂、头发一丝不乱——她仿佛老了好多岁。
「你这个恶毒、自私、狠心的丫头！」南妮继续嚷道，尖利的嗓门拔得老高，「你这个卑鄙、没心肝的下贱货！」她突然朝露西逼过来，露西惊惶地直往后退。
亚瑟出现在门厅口。
「够了！」他严厉地说。
南妮顿时全然失去了自持。
「滚出我的房子！」她尖叫着，「你这个臭——！」接着一串不堪入耳、仿佛出自窑子里的咒骂从她嘴里倾泻出来。她抓起衣帽架上露西的东西，朝前门掷去，散落在门槛上。
「安娜！」亚瑟说话仍旧平静，却只换来冲着他本人的又一阵尖叫和秽语。
「把东西穿戴好，露西。」他说。不多时，父女俩几乎是逃到了街上，撇下南妮在那儿歇斯底里，几乎发狂。
「我这样子没法走啊，爸爸！」走出半条街，露西回头望着那房子，喊道。
「好吧，随你的便。」他不动声色地答道。

===Original===

“Yes, it’s better that I should go,” she decided as if to herself, and they did not speak again until they reached the station.
As they stood waiting for the train Lucy studied Arthur’s set face furtively.
“I don’t want my going to make more trouble between you and Mamma,” she ventured at last, timidly.
Arthur’s eyes sought hers slowly.
“Don’t worry,” he replied heavily in his deep voice. “What you saw today is only a fair sample of what I’ve enjoyed for years. In the beginning I used to hold a quarter of an hour’s argument to get two minutes’ peace. Then I quit that and she tried to punish me with long periods of curdled gloom. Finally I took to staying away from the house in self defense. Long before you grew up things were as bad as they could be, so don’t blame yourself, Daughter. You have had nothing to do with it.”
Lucy was surprised by this unprecedentedly long speech.
“Don’t you think that maybe when I’m gone you and Mamma﻿—” she did not know how to proceed.
“I wouldn’t ask her for a drink of water if I were dying,” Arthur answered. Lucy had never heard such suppressed hatred and vehemence in his tone. “There’s the train,” he added in his ordinary manner, and they moved toward the platform.
“Here are your tickets, and here is your money,” explained Arthur as they entered the Pullman. “Miss Storms will meet you. She will wear a bunch of white carnations. Here is a white carnation for you to wear. Miss Storms knows all about your plans.” He removed a flower that was in his button hole and Lucy fastened it on her jacket. They kissed. The train began to move.
“Goodbye, Daughter. Write often. I will try to get up there to see you soon,” he called.
The journey began.
---
It was late at night and rain was falling when Lucy arrived in Chicago. Leaving the train she was bewildered by the people, noises, and bustle about her. As she stood looking around the thronged platform misted by the radiance of many lights, a very tall woman, dressed in a grey linen tailored suit with a grey hat, and holding some white carnations in her hand, came up and spoke.
“I think you are Lucy.”
“Are you Miss Storms?”
“I am. Where are your baggage checks, dear?”
Lucy handed over her single check and pointed out her smaller baggage which Miss Storms entrusted to a leather coated chauffeur.

===Chinese===

「嗯，还是走吧，走了的好。」她像是自言自语地拿定了主意。到了车站，两人没再说话。
站着候车时，露西偷偷端详亚瑟那张绷紧的脸。
「我不愿意我这一走，又叫您和妈妈多生嫌隙。」她终于怯生生地试着说。
亚瑟慢慢把目光移到她脸上。
「别担心。」他用低沉的嗓音沉重地答道，「你今天见到的，不过是我这些年享受的家常便饭。起初，我常得吵上一刻钟，换两分钟的清净。后来我不吵了，她就改用长时间的阴沉脸色来罚我。到最后，我只好躲出去自卫。远在你长大之前，事情就已经坏到头了，所以别怪自己，孩子。这一切与你毫不相干。」
露西没听过他说这么长的话，吃惊不小。
「您不觉得，也许等我走了，您和妈妈会——」她不知道该怎么说下去。
「我就是快渴死了，也不会向她讨一口水喝。」亚瑟答道。露西从没听过他话音里有这样压抑的恨意和激烈。「车来了。」他又用平常的口气补了一句，两人朝站台走去。
「这是你的车票，这是你的钱。」进普尔曼车厢时亚瑟交代道，「斯托姆斯小姐会来接你。她会戴一束白色康乃馨。这里也有一朵白色康乃馨给你戴着。斯托姆斯小姐对你的安排全都清楚。」他从自己扣眼里取下一朵花，露西把它别在外衣上。他们吻别。火车开动了。
「再见，孩子。常来信。我争取早点儿去看你。」他喊道。
旅程开始了。
---
露西抵达芝加哥时已是深夜，下着雨。下了车，她被四周的人声、嘈杂和忙乱弄得晕头转向。她站在那儿环顾这灯火辉映、人影攒动的站台，一位极高的女人朝她走来，开了口——她穿一身灰色亚麻的定做套裙，戴灰色帽子，手里拿着几枝白色康乃馨。
「我想你就是露西吧。」
「您是斯托姆斯小姐？」
「是我。你的行李牌在哪儿，亲爱的？」
露西交出仅有的一张行李牌，指了指自己那些小件行李，斯托姆斯小姐把它们托付给一个穿皮外套的司机。

===Original===

In a few minutes Lucy found herself in a luxurious limousine gliding through the crowded streets of the city.
---
A few evenings later, at a small dinner to which Miss Storms had invited some young people of her acquaintance, Lucy found herself between a red haired girl and a fresh faced young man, both from the Art School.
“Lucy, this is Nora Stimpson,” began Miss Storms, and Lucy bowed to the red haired girl. “Your right hand partner is John Winter,” continued Miss Storms.
Lucy, following Miss Storms’ direction, looked into the smiling blue eyes of John. He rose and reached out his hand to take hers.
Lucy’s romance had begun.

===Chinese===

几分钟后，露西已经坐在一辆豪华的轿车里，滑行在城中拥挤的街道上。
---
几天后的一个晚上，斯托姆斯小姐请了几个相识的年轻人来吃一顿小便宴，露西的座位在一头红发的姑娘和一位面色鲜润的青年男子之间——两人都是艺术学校的。
「露西，这是诺拉·斯廷普森（Nora Stimpson）。」斯托姆斯小姐开口介绍，露西朝红发姑娘点头致意。「你右手边的搭档是约翰·温特（John Winter）。」斯托姆斯小姐接着说。
露西顺着斯托姆斯小姐的示意，望进约翰那双含笑的蓝眼睛里。他站起身，伸出手来握住了她的手。
露西的罗曼史开始了。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

After Lucy’s departure the Merwent home went from bad to worse. In times past her presence had often sufficed to prevent the more sordid expressions of the disgust and contempt which Arthur felt for Nannie, and of Nannie’s feeling of fear and distrust toward him. He now avoided being at home alone with his wife until he became almost a stranger in the house.
The scandal loving people of Russellville, like the inhabitants of all small towns when similar opportunity is afforded, began to talk, and Arthur’s name was linked with that of Mrs. Low. This, in time, came to Nannie’s ears.
Arthur’s law practice, now that he was put to extra expense for Lucy’s maintenance and education, barely supported the family, and Nannie began to feel the pinch of poverty more keenly, especially in the matter of her wardrobe. Mrs. Lockhart was bitter toward her daughter and made no effort toward consolation. In short, Nannie felt herself hardly used by the world.
She exerted all her ingenuity, however, in making an appearance that belied her pocket book, and achieved a certain amount of success.
“You could turn a good many pennies dressmaking, if you only had horse sense, Anna. Minnie could get you work among her friends. She could put it to them that it was a kind of favor, and if you’d keep your mouth shut nobody would be the worse for it,” remarked Mrs. Lockhart one day, eyeing one of Nannie’s costumes with reluctant approval.
But Nannie was very much hurt by such a suggestion. She had no desire to use her arts to teach other and more prosperous women to dress becomingly.
It was evident, nevertheless, that something must be done to supplement Arthur’s earnings. She still attended social events at the “big house” as often as she could squeeze an invitation from “Cousin Minnie,” and it was to “Cousin Minnie’s” influence, rather languidly exerted in her behalf, that she applied for a solution of her problem.
It came in the shape of a position in a small private school in Russellville.
The school, which had existed precariously for several generations, was usually referred to as “The Academy.” A short while previously it had appeared to be almost moribund, but it had lately been taken over by one Mr. Walsh, called “Professor Walsh,” though he held no degree or official position; and it had, for the time being at least, awakened to unwonted patronage.

===Chinese===

露西走后，默温特家的光景每况愈下。从前，有她在场，往往就足以拦住两口子更难看的场面——亚瑟对南妮的厌恶轻蔑，南妮对他的畏惧提防，都不好意思发作。如今他干脆避免单独同妻子在家，渐渐弄到自己在这宅子里几乎成了外人。
拉塞尔维尔那班爱嚼舌根的人，同一切小镇居民一样，一有类似的机会便开了腔，把亚瑟的名字同洛太太连在了一起。这话迟早传到了南妮耳朵里。
亚瑟如今既要额外负担露西的赡养和学费，他那点律师收入便只够勉强撑住这个家。南妮开始更真切地尝到穷的滋味，尤其在穿戴一项上。洛克哈特太太对女儿没有好气，更不打算安慰她。总之，南妮觉得自己叫全世界亏待了。
不过她仍施尽机巧，撑出一副与钱囊不相称的体面门面，倒也有几分成功。
「安娜，你要是有点起码的见识，做做裁缝也能挣不少钱。明妮能在她那些朋友里替你揽活。她可以对人说是赏你脸面，只要你嘴严，谁也不吃亏。」一天，洛克哈特太太打量着南妮一身行头，老大不情愿地露出赞许之色，说道。
可这活子深深刺伤了南妮。她可不想把自己那套本事拿去教别的更有钱的女人穿得俏丽。
然而，要贴补亚瑟的进项，总得想个法子，这是明摆着的。她仍旧每逢能从「明妮表姐」那儿磨来请帖，就去「大宅」应酬；她便去求「明妮表姐」替她那桩难处拿个主意——表姐为她出力向来是懒洋洋的。
办法来了：拉塞尔维尔一家小私立学校给她谋了个职位。
这所学校摇摇欲坠地传了几代，通称「学馆」。不久前它眼看就要咽气，近来却被一位沃尔什（Walsh）先生接了手——人家都叫他「沃尔什教授」，其实他既没有学位也没有官职——于是它至少一时之间又焕发出少有的兴旺。

===Original===

Though Professor Walsh was from a nearby town, no one seemed to know much of his antecedents. He was a large slow-spoken man with a low-pitched pleasant voice. He had fat features and was rather pale. His hair, parted in the middle, was wetly and thinly combed over a glistening scalp. He had thick curveless lips. His eyes were a nondescript blue and he wore nose glasses which glittered inscrutably.
He had an air of decorum and responsibility, and his smile, which displayed double rows of perfect teeth, was full of warmth and condescension. He was peculiarly apt as a listener, showed a subdued but flattering appreciation of the broad talk of men, and was earnestly sympathetic, though somewhat noncommittal, when he gave ear to the ladies.
His personality was such that he impressed one, in his genial restraint, as being responsible for the group.
He was enough of an enigma to overawe people and acquired the reputation of being learned. The teachers in the school were afraid of him. By patronizing some and snubbing others, and by frequent vague allusions to distinguished acquaintances and remote but exalted relations, the Professor forced the first families of Russellville, so styled by themselves, into receiving him. At the institution over which he presided Nannie gave piano lessons to the younger pupils. She rather enjoyed than disliked her task, for her position allowed her to remind Arthur that he could not support her, and besides Professor Walsh soon became interested in her and was, as she told herself, exceedingly kind and sympathetic.
She also found it pleasant to meet the grown boys, or “young gentlemen,” as they were called, who attended some of the higher classes. Professor Walsh left her little opportunity to talk with these youths but their presence lent a piquancy to his attentions.
She was rather afraid of him, but the envy with which the teachers looked on such signal favor as she received from this august person reconciled her to those things in his makeup which she found incomprehensible. She was further led to encourage his attentions by the fact that there was no one to contest with him the role of exclusive admirer.
Professor Walsh, being of ambiguous antecedents, realized fully the advantages of a connection with some family which exemplified the southern tradition.

===Chinese===

沃尔什教授虽是邻镇人，身世却没人说得上来。他是个大块头，说话慢条斯理，嗓音低沉悦耳。他五官肥厚，面色苍白。头发从中间分开，稀稀地油光光覆在亮晶晶的头皮上。他嘴唇厚而无形。眼睛是不深不浅的蓝色，鼻梁上架一副夹鼻眼镜，闪着叫人捉摸不透的光。
他一派端庄可靠的神气，笑起来露出一口整整齐齐的牙齿，笑里满是温暖和垂青。他天生是个好听众：男人们高谈阔论，他听得克制冷却倍觉受用；女士们向他倾吐，他一片恳切的同情——只是不置可否。
他那份人格力量，加上和气的克制，叫人觉得这一伙人该由他当家。
他这人够神秘，镇得住人，博得了有学问的名声。学校里的教师都怕他。他时而抬举这个、时而训斥那个，又时不时含糊地提起显赫的熟人、偏远而高贵的亲戚，硬是让拉塞尔维尔那几户自封的「高门大族」不得不接待他。在他主持的这所学馆里，南妮给年幼的学生上钢琴课。这份差事她喜欢多于讨厌，因为这个身份让她可以时时提醒亚瑟养不起她；再说沃尔什教授不久便对她上了心，用她自己的话说，又体贴又知冷知热。
她也乐意遇见那些大男孩——上高级班的称作「年轻君子」的那些人。沃尔什教授很少给她同这些少年攀谈的机会，但有他们在场，他的殷勤便多了几分刺激的味道。
她有点怕他，可教师们眼见她蒙受这位威严人物如此的恩宠、个个艳羡，这份艳羡也就把她对他性情中那些她弄不懂的地方抵消了。再者，再没有第二个人来同他争夺她独一无二的仰慕者这个角色，这也促使她乐于领受他的殷勤。
沃尔什教授身世暧昧，深知攀上一户体现南方门第传统的人家有多少好处。

===Original===

He easily perceived that Nannie Merwent was susceptible to influence, and he hoped through her to cement an alliance with the Sheldons, the most prosperous and dictatory citizens of Russellville.
Professor Walsh’s plans were not laid in a day; but, without her realizing it, he considered each circumstance of Nannie’s life as she presented it to him, and decided on its possible relation to his own future.
At one of the few receptions which Arthur had felt bound to attend in Russellville, he met Professor Walsh.
“I am honoured to know you, Mr. Merwent,” remarked the educator affably, when they were introduced. “I have the deepest admiration for your profession. A﻿—ah﻿—distant connection of mine﻿—though our names are not spelt the same﻿—James K. Walshe of Chicago. You may have heard of him?”
“Every lawyer knows the reputation of your relative, Mr. Walsh,” replied Arthur. “In criminal cases he has few equals.”
“In case you ever go to Chicago I shall be most happy to give you a letter of introduction to him,” offered the Professor.
“Thank you,” returned Arthur, “I hardly think﻿—” Then he appeared to reconsider. “It is true that I shall probably go to Chicago in the near future, and I should be very glad to go to see your relative.”
“Well, sir, you shalt have the letter.” The Professor compressed his lips, put the tips of his fingers together and regarded Arthur curiously. “Without fail. I will send it to your office tomorrow,” he added.
“Thank you,” repeated Arthur. And the two separated.
---
Nannie, on one of the rare occasions when Arthur was at home, sneeringly alluded to his attentions to Mrs. Low.
“You will kindly not interfere with the conduct of my personal affairs while you are so careless of your own,” he requested in a tone that closed the conversation.
A few days later the gossips of Russellville were informing their friends that Mrs. Low had gone to Chicago for an indefinite stay. Nannie at once wrote furiously to Lucy accusing her of plotting with deliberate intent to wreck her mother’s life.
Nannie’s accusations were so obviously ill founded that the only recourse left for Lucy’s wounded pride was silence, and she made no reply to a succession of violently worded letters.
Arthur appreciated in part the difficult situation in which his daughter was placed, and did his best to prevent her being drawn into this conflict. It was not long after Mrs.

===Chinese===

他一眼便看出南妮·默温特容易受人摆布，指望通过她同谢尔登家结成同盟——那是拉塞尔维尔最殷实、最说一不二的人家。
沃尔什教授的盘算不是一天画成的；南妮把自己的境遇一桩桩说给他听，他一一掂量（她自己浑然不觉），掂量它们同他自己的前程可能有多大干系。
亚瑟在拉塞尔维尔觉得不得不出席的宴会屈指可数，在其中一次上，他遇见了沃尔什教授。
「得识尊驾，深感荣幸，默温特先生，」介绍过后，这位教书先生和蔼地说。「我对您的行业怀着最深的敬意。我有一位远亲——虽然我们的姓拼法不同——芝加哥的詹姆斯·K·沃尔什（James K. Walshe）。您或许听说过？」
「当律师的谁不知道您这位亲戚的大名，沃尔什先生，」亚瑟答道。「办刑事案件，没几个人比得上他。」
「您哪天去芝加哥，我乐意给您写封引荐信给他。」教授主动提出。
「谢谢，」亚瑟答道，「我想我还不至于——」随即他像是转了个念头。「其实我不久大概真要去芝加哥，去拜望您那位亲戚，我是很高兴的。」
「好，先生，这信您一定有。」教授抿紧嘴唇，指尖对着指尖，好奇地打量亚瑟。「误不了事。明天我打发人送到您事务所。」他补充道。
「谢谢。」亚瑟又说了一遍。两人便分开了。
---
亚瑟难得在家的一回，南妮冷嘲热讽地提起他对洛太太的殷勤。
「你既把自己的行止这么不当回事，就请不要来过问我的私事怎么办。」他一句话封死了这场谈话。
几天之后，拉塞尔维尔的闲话婆们纷纷告知亲友：洛太太到芝加哥去了，归期不定。南妮当即怒气冲冲地写信给露西，指控她存心设局，存心毁掉母亲的一生。
南妮的指控实在无凭无据，露西受了伤的自尊无处申诉，唯有沉默；接二连三措辞激烈的来信，她一概不复。
亚瑟多少体谅女儿的难处，尽力不让她被卷进这场冲突。洛太太到芝加哥后不久，露西便得知父亲把大部分时间都花在州府，在那里结交了几处重要的法律同行。这样一来，南妮几乎完全被撇下独自一人，便越来越依赖那位好心的教授——他对她的事务始终保持着一种有分寸却从不间断的关切。
他有个习惯：隔着闪闪发光的眼镜谜一样地端详她，叫她始终有点怕他；可他又总能唬得住旁人，她谨慎的本能催她去倚靠他的沉稳镇定，她的虚荣心又更进一步怂恿她去把他征服。南妮并非不知道镇上已经开始议论她同他的来往，但她怕独处的恐慌压过了她谨慎的老习惯。
一天傍晚离校之后，教授差人送来一张便条，说他听到一些关于亚瑟行止的传闻，认为她应当知道，请她夜里到学馆来一趟。
南妮穿戴好，毫不迟疑地出了门，却特意拣僻静街道走，一面暗暗祷告「明妮表姐」和别的亲戚千万别听说这趟造访。
学馆是一所老式的大红砖楼，添建了好几进，还有一侧翼，翼楼开一扇便门，通一条人迹罕至的小巷，教授的办公室便设在那一进里。
南妮走上小径时，看得见他俯身伏在窗子同灯光之间书桌上的巨大身影。她在侧门按铃，他亲自来开门。不知怎的，她说不清为什么，起初在他面前竟感到一阵异样的不自在；待他俯身执住她的手——友好而随意——她的心猛地一跳，狐疑地抬眼望他。可是他安抚地拍拍她的肩，又低声把新近听来的几桩关于亚瑟的闲话讲给她听，她便完全放下心来。她认定他仰慕她仰慕得了不得，并在心里打定主意，要拿他这份痴情当武器，抵挡「明妮表姐」没完没了的白眼。

===Original===

Low’s arrival in Chicago that Lucy learned that her father was spending most of his time in the state capital, where he was making important legal affiliations. In this way Nannie, left almost entirely to herself, began to depend more and more on the benevolent Professor, who showed a reserved but unfailing interest in her affairs.
He had a way of regarding her enigmatically through his twinkling glasses that made her always a little afraid of him, but he was also always able to impress other people, and her cautious instincts prompted her to lean toward his poise and aplomb while her vanity still further urged her to his conquest. Nannie was not unaware of the fact that the town had begun to comment on her association with him but her terror of being alone was greater than her habit of caution.
One evening after leaving school, a note from the Professor was delivered to her, in which he suggested that she come to the school building that night, as he had heard some rumors of Arthur’s doings which he thought she should know.
Nannie dressed herself and went out without hesitation, but she was careful to find her way along obscure streets and hoped fervently that neither “Cousin Minnie” nor her other relatives would hear of the visit.
The Academy was a large old fashioned red brick building with many additions and one wing which had a private entrance in a little-frequented lane where the Professor’s office was situated.
As Nannie came up the walk she could see his large shadow as he bent over his desk between the window and the light. She rang the bell at the side door and he himself admitted her. For some reason which she could not explain she felt at first an unusual discomfort in his presence, and, when he leaned forward and took her hand in a kind but casual manner, her heart gave a sudden leap and she glanced up at him suspiciously. However, he patted her shoulder comfortingly, and, as he repeated to her in a low tone some incidents of gossip about Arthur which had lately come to his ears, she was completely reassured. She was certain that he admired her tremendously, and she was determining in her heart that she would use his devotion as a weapon of defense against the continual disapprobation of “Cousin Minnie.”

===Chinese===

沃尔什教授絮絮地宽慰了她许久，待她瞥见他书架上的钟，不由吃了一惊——竟由着自己待到了拉塞尔维尔要骂伤风败俗的钟点。沃尔什教授提出送她回家，她极力推辞。要是被人撞见这个时辰他陪她走在一起，她就当不成受害的一方了——鉴于亚瑟的冷落，她正一心要世人承认她这个身份。
南妮同教授道过晚安，走到街上，真是她最可怕的一刻：大门口迎面撞上一个陌生男人，那人带着沉静的决心走上前来，问她是不是默温特太太。
「是——是的。」南妮迟疑地嗫嚅，恐惧扼住了喉咙，尽管她想不出接下来会发生什么。
「对，这位就是默温特太太。」一个干脆的男声应道。布莱尔先生——亚瑟在好几桩案子里共过事的同行——从学馆墙根的浓影里跨出来，身后跟着亚瑟本人和又一个陌生男人。
南妮虽说严格讲没做什么亏心事，也几乎瘫倒，简直不知道自己在做什么，便应承配合亚瑟为离婚所做的种种安排。
这场谈话，亚瑟的同伴们个个竖着耳朵细听。其间她只软弱地异议过一句；亚瑟便冷冷宣告，他可以改按更重的罪名起诉，决不怕闹得满城风雨，还可以拿今夜这件事做证据——那两个她不认识的男人都是体面的证人。
南妮降服了。
---
然而默温特太太沉思了几天，便说服自己：丈夫绝迈不出他所盘算的那一步。
因此，亚瑟正式提出离婚诉讼之后的那场大闹，比起她婚后隔三差五赏给丈夫的许多场大闹——多数还是由鸡毛蒜皮引起——并不见得更凶。
待她发觉暴怒、辱骂和讥讽都撼不动亚瑟的平静，她当真怕了，开始歇斯底里地哭起来。
亚瑟望着她，依旧无动于衷。
她使出最后一招，凄凄切切地提起两人共同的生活。

===Original===

Professor Walsh talked so long and so comfortingly that Nannie was startled when she looked at a clock on his book shelf and realized that she had allowed herself to remain until an hour which Russellville would consider scandalous. When Professor Walsh offered to escort her home she protested vehemently. If he were seen walking with her at such a time it might rob her of the position of an injured party which was what she desired the world to permit her in view of Arthur’s neglect.
It was a terrible moment for Nannie when she had told the Professor good night and walked into the street, only to find herself confronted at the very gate by a strange man who stepped up to her with an air of quiet determination and inquired if she were Mrs. Merwent.
“Y﻿—yes,” stammered Nannie doubtfully, terror clutching her throat, though she was unable to conceive of what was about to happen.
“Why yes, this is Mrs. Merwent,” affirmed a brisk masculine voice as, from the deep shadow thrown by the Academy walls, Mr. Blair, Arthur’s colleague in several law cases, stepped out, followed by Arthur himself and another strange man.
Nannie, though technically guiltless of wrongdoing, was almost in a state of collapse and, scarcely knowing what she did, agreed to cooperate in the arrangements Arthur desired for a divorce.
Only once during the conversation, which was carefully listened to by all of Arthur’s companions, did she weakly demur; whereupon he coldly declared he would bring suit on a graver charge, would not shrink from publicity and would use the incident of the night to prove his case, as the two men unknown to her were reputable witnesses.
Nannie was conquered.
---
After a few days of reflection, however, Mrs. Merwent persuaded herself that her husband could never bring himself to the point of taking the step he contemplated.
The scene which followed the actual filing of Arthur’s suit for divorce, therefore, was no worse than numerous other scenes to which she had treated her husband at frequent intervals throughout their married life, many of them precipitated by trifles.
When she perceived that rage, abuse, and sarcasms all failed to affect Arthur’s calm, she grew really frightened and began to weep hysterically.
Arthur gazed at her still unmoved.
As a last resort she referred pathetically to their life together.

===Original===

“You can’t mean that you will carry this thing through, Arthur!” she moaned. “For Lucy’s sake you can’t do such awful things.”
His sudden cold fury was terrible.
“For Lucy’s sake,” he repeated slowly and gratingly, “after you have driven her out of the house and poisoned her youth! Yes, it would be highly appropriate for us to stay together for Lucy’s sake.”
“Arthur, I can’t believe that you hate me that much!” Nannie pleaded. “You don’t hate me, do you?”
“I can’t put my feelings into words,” he answered mercilessly. “You poison the air. I wonder the flowers don’t die as you pass by.” His tone was coldly implacable and he did not raise his voice.
She gazed at him an instant with silent and helpless animosity.
“You cruel monster!” she shrieked, springing suddenly from her chair.
Arthur turned on his heel and went out.
Nannie’s first act following this interview with her husband was to see Professor Walsh. Since Lucy’s rebellion she had grown afraid of Mrs. Lockhart’s comments on family difficulties. Nannie felt that the Professor’s accidental role in her embarrassment constituted a sort of obligation on his part.
Their meetings of late had been slightly constrained. But once in his private office he listened gravely and kindly to her agitated story.
Without much having been said on either side Professor Walsh had understood for some time from Mr. Sheldon that the family would not look unkindly on the prospect of someone eventually taking Nannie off their hands.
“My advice is that you do not contest the suit,” he said, when she had finished, “but I should try to get him to agree to something for your support if I were you, Anna. This could be done amicably and out of court. It is no more than right that he should do something for you financially.”
Nannie had not noticed that he called her by her given name and broke in with, “But, Professor Walsh﻿—”
“Call me Edward, Anna,” he interrupted, taking her hand.
She tried to withdraw her hand but he retained it.
“We are such good friends,” he continued. “Aren’t we, Anna?”
“Yes,” she admitted uncomfortably.
“Well, it is agreed then. I should refuse to talk about it to others if I were you. And anything new that may come up you will let me know at once, won’t you, dear?”
“Yes﻿—Edward,” answered Nannie, hesitating slightly, somewhat bewildered by the new aspect which her affairs had taken.

===Chinese===

「你总不会真要把这事做到底吧，亚瑟！」她哀声道。「看在露西的份上，你不能做这么狠心的事。」
他陡然爆出的冷冷的狂怒，可怕极了。
「看在露西的份上，」他一字一顿、声音刺耳地重复道，「在你把她赶出家门、毒害了她的青春之后！是啊，咱们『看在露西的份上』继续守在一起，那可再合适不过了。」
「亚瑟，我不信你恨我恨到这个地步！」南妮哀求道。「你不恨我的，对不对？」
「我的感受没法用言语表达，」他毫不留情地答道。「你毒化空气。我奇怪你走过的地方，花怎么不都枯死。」他的语气冷得斩钉截铁，嗓门却没有抬高。
她默默望了他一瞬，眼里是无计可施的怨毒。
「你这狠心的恶魔！」她尖叫着，猛地从椅子上跳起来。
亚瑟转身径直出去了。
这场夫妻对话之后，南妮头一件事便是去见沃尔什教授。自打露西反抗以来，她怕听洛克哈特太太对家务难处的评点了。南妮觉得，教授在她这场难堪里偶然扮演的角色，等于是他欠了她一分情。
近来两人的会面多少有些拘谨。可是一进他的私人办公室，他便恳切而和善地听她激动地讲述原委。
双方都没有多说，沃尔什教授其实早从谢尔登先生那里明白：将来真有人把南妮接手过去，这一家人只会乐见其成。
「我的忠告是：不要争讼，」她讲完之后他说，「不过若是我，总得设法叫他答应一笔赡养。这事可以好说好商量，庭外解决。他在钱上对你有所表示，也不为过分。」
南妮没有留意他已改口叫她的闺名，径直插嘴道：「可是，沃尔什教授——」
「叫我爱德华，安娜。」他打断她，执起她的手。
她想抽回手，他却不放。
「咱们是这么好的朋友了，」他继续道，「是不是，安娜？」
「是。」她不自在地承认。
「好，那就说定了。我要是你，就不跟旁人再提这事。往后有什么新的动静，你立刻告诉我，好不好，亲爱的？」
「好——爱德华。」南妮答道，微微踌躇，她的事务呈现的这副新面目叫她有些发懵。

## X / 十
===Original===

Nearly a week had passed since Mrs. Merwent’s arrival at Rosedene and Jim had not visited the Winter home. This was an unprecedented length of time for him to stay away.
“How is Mr. Sprague, your partner?” Nannie queried one evening at dinner.
“Oh, he’s all right,” responded John, and then added, “Why he’s not been out since the day you came! I’ll ask him out to dinner tomorrow night. He’s no extra trouble.”
Lucy was silent.
“I’d like to get acquainted with him,” pursued Mrs. Merwent. “Of course if it’s too much work for Lucy﻿—”
“Why no,” answered John. “Lucy never has anything extra for Jim. Do you, Lucy? I want you to know him, Nannie. You’ll like him fine.”
“Lucy hasn’t said anything,” persisted Nannie. “I think she’d rather not have anybody.”
“No, it’s all right,” agreed Lucy. “Jim’s no extra trouble, ever.”
The next morning John told Jim.
“Nannie and Lucy want you for dinner,” John said. “You didn’t use to have to be asked. Why didn’t you come out before?”
“I’ve been going over the books,” explained Jim. “We must raise our prices on those small houses. We don’t make anything on them at present figures.”
That evening they took the train out to Rosedene together.
When they arrived Lucy was in the kitchen and Mrs. Merwent met them at the door.
“Are you tired?” she murmured solicitously as she kissed John’s cheek, then, smiling and extending her hand to Jim, “How do you do, Mr. Sprague? You’re quite a stranger. Lucy thought you had deserted us.”
“No, I’ve been busy,” he replied, taking her hand and smiling in turn.
When the three entered the dining room, Mrs. Merwent picked up two boutonnieres from the table and pinned first one on Jim’s coat lapel, and then the other on John’s.
“Thank you, Mrs. Merwent,” said Jim.
John patted her shoulder.
“Why, how sweet of you, Nannie!” he exclaimed.
At this juncture Dimmie rushed in and, throwing himself headlong into Jim’s outstretched arms, yelled delightedly, “Uncle Jim, Uncle Jim, the cat’s got kittens!”
“Why how boisterous you are, Jimmie.” Mrs. Merwent spoke reprovingly. (She never called him “Dimmie.”)
“Yes,” said John. “You make too much noise, Dimmie.”
“He’s all right. I’m used to him,” put in Jim. He realized as he spoke that never before had he interfered in the child’s training.
“Well, if you like it.” Mrs. Merwent smiled. “I’m afraid you spoil him, Mr. Sprague,” she added.

===Chinese===

默温特太太来罗斯登快一个星期了，吉姆还没上温特家来过。这么久不露面，在他可是破天荒的事。
「你那位合伙人斯普拉格先生怎么样啦？」一天晚饭时南妮问。
「哦，他挺好的。」约翰应道，接着又补充，「说起来，打您来的那天起他就没来过！明晚我请他来吃晚饭吧。多他一个不费事。」
露西没言语。
「我倒想同他认识认识。」默温特太太接着说，「当然，要是露西嫌添麻烦——」
「哪会呢。」约翰答道，「吉姆来，露西从来不多费一点事。是不是，露西？我要您认识认识他，南妮。您准会喜欢他。」
「露西可一句话没说。」南妮不依，「我看她宁愿谁也别来。」
「不，没关系。」露西附和道，「吉姆从来不费事。」
第二天早上，约翰告诉了吉姆。
「南妮和露西请你来吃晚饭。」约翰说，「从前哪用得着请。你先前怎么不出来？」
「我在过账。」吉姆解释道，「那些小房子必须提价了。照现在的价钱，我们一点赚头都没有。」
当晚，两人一道搭火车去罗斯登。
到的时候露西在厨房，默温特太太到门口迎接。
「累了吧？」她关切地低声问着，吻了吻约翰的面颊，随即又含笑向吉姆伸出手去，「你好，斯普拉格先生？你可真成了稀客。露西还当你把我们撇下了呢。」
「没有，我忙。」他握住她的手，也笑了笑。
三人进了餐厅，默温特太太从桌上拿起两朵钮孔花，先别在吉姆的衣领翻边上，又把另一朵别在约翰的。
「谢谢您，默温特太太。」吉姆说。
约翰拍拍她的肩。
「哎呀，您真好，南妮！」他喜滋滋地说。
正这时，迪米冲了进来，一头扎进吉姆张开的怀抱，欢天喜地地嚷：「吉姆叔叔，吉姆叔叔，猫生小猫啦！」
「瞧你多野呀，吉米。」默温特太太用责备的口气说。（她从来不叫他「迪米」。）
「就是。」约翰说，「你太吵了，迪米。」
「他挺好，我惯了。」吉姆插话。说出口他才意识到，自己还从没插手过这孩子的管教。
「好吧，只要你乐意。」默温特太太笑了，「只怕你把他惯坏了，斯普拉格先生。」她补了一句。

===Original===

Jim lifted Dimmie to his shoulder and went off to inspect the kittens. On the way they passed through the kitchen.
“Hello, Lucy.” Jim held out his hand. “Heard you thought I’d deserted you.”
“I can’t shake hands with you. My hands are all over flour,” she explained. “You know I never thought any such thing!”
“Yes, I know,” he answered.
Dimmie grew impatient.
“It’ll get dark and you can’t see the kittens,” he complained.
After Jim and Dimmie had left the dining room, Nannie, who was standing by the table, began to look about as if in search of something.
“Why, Lucy must have forgotten to order it,” she observed, as if to herself. Then she turned to her son-in-law, placing her hand on his arm.
“John,” she asked, smiling, “would you do Nannie a favor?”
“Sure,” he consented. “What is it?”
“I wish you would get me a little fruit to keep in my room. I like to eat a little before going to bed.”
“Of course. I’ll have some sent around in the morning.”
“I meant tonight﻿—if you didn’t mind.”
“All right. Jim and I’ll go and get it after dinner.”
“You cruel boy! You want to advertise my weakness to the world. I didn’t want anybody to know.”
“Good. I’ll just pop out and get it right away. How’s that?”
“You’re a dear boy. I’m afraid Lucy wouldn’t approve of spoiling me this way.” Mrs. Merwent walked into the hall where John followed her.
“Well, we won’t tell anybody,” declared John. He took his hat from the rack and went out the front door, closing it gently after him.
Jim and Dimmie came back from the kitten inspecting expedition and, entering the living room, found Mrs. Merwent alone.
“Mother was asking for you a minute ago, Jimmie,” she told the little boy.
Dimmie ran to the kitchen.
Jim sat down by the fire place.
“Everybody has left me to amuse myself, Mr. Sprague.” Nannie looked up at him challengingly from the depths of the Morris chair in which she was reclining.
“Why where’s John?” he asked.
“He’s gone out some place for a few minutes. I don’t know where,” she said. “Wouldn’t you like to smoke?” She rose. “I’ll get some of John’s cigarettes.”
“No, thank you. I don’t smoke cigarettes. I’ll fill my pipe if you don’t mind.”
“I smoke cigarettes sometimes, when I’m quite alone,” she confided, laughing slightly.
“Yes?”
“I suppose you don’t approve of ladies smoking, do you?” she insisted.
“If they want to.”
“Does Lucy smoke?”
“No.”
Mrs. Merwent laughed again.

===Chinese===

吉姆把迪米扛上肩头，去看小猫了。路上经过厨房。
「你好，露西。」吉姆伸出手，「听说你以为我把你撇下了。」
「没法跟你握手，我手上全是面粉。」她解释道，「你知道我从来没那么想过！」
「嗯，我知道。」他答道。
迪米等得不耐烦了。
「天要黑了，就看不清小猫了。」他抱怨道。
吉姆和迪米出了餐厅后，站在桌旁的南妮开始四下张望，像在找什么。
「哎呀，露西准是忘了订了。」她像自言自语地说。然后她转向女婿，一只手搭上他的胳膊。
「约翰，」她笑吟吟地问，「你肯帮南妮一个忙吗？」
「当然。」他应承道，「什么事？」
「我想请你给我弄点水果，搁在我房里。我喜欢睡前吃一点。」
「没问题。明早我让人送些过来。」
「我是说今晚——你要是不嫌麻烦。」
「行。晚饭后我和吉姆去买。」
「你这个狠心的孩子！你是想把我的嘴馋昭告天下呀。我本来谁也不想叫知道的。」
「好，那我现在就溜出去一趟，马上买来。怎么样？」
「真是个乖孩子。只是露西怕不会赞成这样宠我。」默温特太太走进门厅，约翰跟在后面。
「放心，咱们谁也不告诉。」约翰说。他从架上取了帽子，出了前门，轻轻带上门。
吉姆和迪米看完小猫回来，进了客厅，只见默温特太太独自一人。
「妈妈方才正找你呢，吉米。」她对小家伙说。
迪米跑向厨房。
吉姆在壁炉边坐下。
「人人都把我撇下，让我自己找乐子，斯普拉格先生。」南妮从她半躺着的莫里斯扶手椅（Morris chair）深处，挑逗地仰望着他。
「咦，约翰呢？」他问。
「他出去几分钟，到个什么地方去了。我不知道。」她说，「想抽支烟吗？」她站起身，「我去拿点约翰的香烟。」
「不用了，谢谢。我不抽香烟。您不介意的话，我装我的烟斗。」
「我有时候也抽香烟，一个人独处的时候。」她略带笑意地吐露道。
「是吗？」
「我想你是不赞成女士抽烟的，对吧？」她追问。
「她们想抽就抽呗。」
「露西抽烟吗？」
「不抽。」
默温特太太又笑了。

===Original===

“I didn’t know,” she declared.
Jim gazed at her steadily. The front door clicked and she started.
“Was that John?” she asked.
Jim rose and glanced through the window.
“No,” he responded. “It was Dimmie. He threw his ball into the street. Did you want anything, Mrs. Merwent?”
“Oh, no,” she assured him. “I just wondered. He said he was coming right back. Well, we don’t need a chaperone, do we?”
“Hardly,” replied Jim.
Mrs. Merwent dropped a marquise ring she had been pulling on and off her finger. Jim picked it up and handed it to her.
“Thank you. I shouldn’t be wearing rings with this old gown. I just put it on from force of habit.”
“Which?” asked Jim, smiling.
“The ring, of course, you sarcastic thing,” she retorted, striking his knee with the lace handkerchief in her hand.
“The dress is charming, Mrs. Merwent.”
“Oh, thank you. You’re going to be nice after all. Well, I was quite prepared to find you so. Lucy and John can’t say enough good things about you.”
“I’m much obliged to them,” he remarked, smiling again. “I can say the same of them.”
“How lovely! I do think real friendship is the grandest thing﻿—and so rare.”
“No doubt about that, Mrs. Merwent.” Jim smiled once more.
“Now I believe you’re making fun of me, Mr. Sprague.”
“I can assure you I’m not, Mrs. Merwent.”
“I envy them, you know. I’ve been so lonely since my﻿—trouble.”
“I can quite imagine,” said Jim sympathetically. “But now you are with your children and﻿—”
“Yes,” she interrupted hastily, “and isn’t John just the dearest fellow! I do so regret our misunderstanding. Though I was not to blame for it,” she added.
“John’s a good sort,” Jim agreed.
“And Lucy,” Mrs. Merwent now smiled. “You haven’t said anything about Lucy.”
“We weren’t talking about her.”
“Well, she’s a dear girl, although﻿—why I’ve burnt my slipper!” she broke off. “Do you know, Mr. Sprague, that I have the hardest time to get shoes narrow enough. It seems that most women, especially here in the North, have big feet. Lucy takes after her father. He was a Northern man.”
Jim seemed amused, and Mrs. Merwent concluded hastily.
“But here I am chattering on about *me*,” she said, “and you haven’t told me anything about yourself.”
“There isn’t much to tell, Mrs. Merwent.”
“Well, if you could hear Lucy and John talk about you you wouldn’t be so modest.”
“Neither of them has much critical acumen in matters of friendship.”

===Chinese===

「我还不知道呢。」她说。
吉姆定定地望着她。前门咔哒一响，她一惊。
「是约翰吗？」她问。
吉姆起身朝窗外瞥了一眼。
「不是。」他答道，「是迪米。他把球扔到街上去了。您要什么吗，默温特太太？」
「哦，不。」她让他放心，「我不过问问。他说马上回来的。嗐，咱们用不着陪护人，对吧？」
「用不着。」吉姆答道。
默温特太太把指头上摘下戴上的那枚船形戒指弄掉了。吉姆拾起来递还给她。
「谢谢。穿这件旧袍子本不该戴戒指的。不过是习惯使然，顺手就戴上了。」
「哪个的习惯？」吉姆笑着问。
「当然是戒指，你这个挖苦人的家伙。」她反唇相讥，用手里的蕾丝手帕拍了拍他的膝盖。
「这裙子可真好看，默温特太太。」
「哦，谢谢。你到底还是招人喜欢的。嗐，我早就料到你是这样。露西和约翰说起你来，好话简直说个没完。」
「多承他们抬爱。」他又笑着应道，「他们俩我也可以照这样说。」
「多美啊！我真觉得真正的友谊是天底下最了不起的东西——又那么难得。」
「这倒没错，默温特太太。」吉姆又笑了笑。
「这会儿我信你是在打趣我了，斯普拉格先生。」
「我向您保证真没有，默温特太太。」
「你知道，我真羡慕他们。自从我那场——变故之后，我一直好孤独。」
「这我完全想象得出。」吉姆同情地说，「可如今您同孩子们在一起，而且——」
「是啊。」她急忙插嘴，「约翰这人多招人疼啊！我们那场误会，我真懊悔极了。虽说那不怪我。」她补了一句。
「约翰是个好人。」吉姆表示同意。
「还有露西。」默温特太太这才笑了，「你还没说说露西呢。」
「我们方才谈的不是她。」
「嗯，她是个可爱的姑娘，虽然——呀，我把拖鞋烤糊了！」她话锋一转，「斯普拉格先生，你可知道，我想买到够窄的鞋子有多难。好像大多数女人，尤其是你们北方这儿的，脚都大。露西随她父亲。她父亲是个北方人。」
吉姆像是被逗乐了，默温特太太赶紧收住话头。
「瞧我，光顾着絮叨*我*自己了。」她说，「你还没跟我说说你自己呢。」
「没什么可说的，默温特太太。」
「哼，你要是听得见露西和约翰怎么夸你，就不会这么谦虚了。」
「他们俩在交朋友这件事上，眼光都不怎么挑剔。」

===Original===

“But you are their only intimate friend.”
Jim laughed.
“That’s just it,” he asserted.
“You cynical thing!” she reproved banteringly. “I know you men with no illusions. I declare I’m afraid of you.”
“Not dangerous﻿—believe me, Mrs. Merwent.”
“No. You despise us women too much to take us seriously.”
“Some of the biggest individuals I have met have been women,” Jim answered gravely.
“Lucy, for instance.”
“Yes, Lucy,” agreed Jim.
“Well, I’m not much like her. Do you think so?”
“Our acquaintance is pretty brief to justify a fundamental judgment of that sort.”
“Oh, I’m not at all intellectual or deep. I can’t talk about Bergson and books like *Jean Christophe* that Lucy reads. I’m afraid you won’t like poor little me much.”
“There are lots of people I don’t like who talk about philosophy and musical novels.” His tone was pleasant.
“Well, I have a feeling already that you don’t like me very well.”
“I don’t think I could help liking Lucy’s mother,” Jim answered without conviction.
“Thanks! I don’t know that I care to be liked because I’m Lucy’s mother,” she replied, laughing nervously. “Your liking seems to be confined to Lucy and things belonging to her. That’s not very complimentary to the rest of us.”
Jim, who had been staring at the empty grate, glanced up and met Mrs. Merwent’s eyes. Her gaze dropped.
“Yes, I do like Lucy, and the people she likes,” he acknowledged frankly, “and if anybody liked me on the same recommendation I should feel honored. She is a person any man or woman must feel it a great privilege to know.”
Mrs. Merwent arched her brows.
“Of course I think just as you do about Lucy, Mr. Sprague, but if I were John I might” (she smiled again) “object the tiniest bit to your﻿—enthusiasm.”
“Object?” Jim looked blank.
“Maybe that is the wrong word.” Nannie’s smile grew more meaning. “It isn’t every man who enjoys having his wife admired *too* much.”
“If John objects he only needs to say so. I don’t think you quite understand the atmosphere of this household yet, Mrs. Merwent.”
“This household?”
“Your daughter then.”
“Well, I certainly think I ought to know her.”
“You ought, but the most charitable thing is to believe you don’t.” There was decided asperity in Jim’s tone.
Mrs. Merwent’s manner changed. She rose haughtily.
“What do you mean by that, Mr. Sprague!” she exclaimed.
Jim rose also, and the two scrutinized each other in silence for a moment.

===Chinese===

「可你是他们唯一的知交呀。」
吉姆笑了。
「这就对了。」他断言。
「你这个愤世的家伙！」她半嗔半笑地训道，「你们这种没有幻想的男人我见得多了。说实话，我怕你。」
「不危险——相信我，默温特太太。」
「是不危险。你们太瞧不起我们女人了，根本不会拿我们当真。」
「我平生所遇最了不起的人物里，就有几位是女性。」吉姆正色答道。
「比如露西。」
「不错，露西。」吉姆同意。
「嗐，我可不大像她。你说呢？」
「我们认识得还浅，够不上下这种根本性的判断。」
「哦，我一点也算不上有学问、有深度。柏格森（Bergson）也好，露西读的《约翰·克利斯朵夫》（*Jean Christophe*）那类书也好，我都谈不来。只怕你不会多喜欢可怜的小小的我呢。」
「高谈哲学和音乐小说的人里头，我不喜欢的可多着呢。」他的口气很和悦。
「嗯，我已经觉出来了，你不大喜欢我。」
「露西的母亲，我想我不喜欢也难。」吉姆答道，语气并不怎么由衷。
「多谢！可我不知道自己乐不乐意只因为是露西的母亲才被人喜欢。」她答道，笑得有些发慌，「你的喜欢好像只限于露西，和属于露西的东西。对咱们其余这些人，这可算不上恭维。」
吉姆本来盯着空空的炉膛出神，这时抬眼正碰上默温特太太的目光。她的视线垂了下去。
「是的，我喜欢露西，也喜欢她喜欢的人。」他坦然承认，「倘若有人因为同样的缘由而喜欢我，我会引以为荣。她这样的人，无论男女，能认识她都是莫大的荣幸。」
默温特太太挑起了眉毛。
「关于露西，我的想法当然同你一模一样，斯普拉格先生。不过我要是约翰，说不定会」（她又笑了）「对你这份——热情，有那么一丁点儿意见。」
「意见？」吉姆一脸茫然。
「也许这个词用得不对。」南妮的笑意变得意味深长，「可不是个个男人都喜欢自己的妻子被人夸得*太*厉害。」
「约翰要有意见，只消说一声就是。我想您还不大明白这个家里的空气，默温特太太。」
「这个家里的空气？」
「那就说，令嫒的空气。」
「哼，我想我总该是认识她的。」
「您本该认识。不过往好里想，只能算您不认识。」吉姆的话音里透出明显的不客气。
默温特太太变了脸色，傲然起身。
「你这话什么意思，斯普拉格先生！」她喝道。
吉姆也站起来，两人默默对视了片刻。

===Original===

“No offense intended,” he protested, turning and knocking his pipe out on the mantel shelf.
Nannie was still injured.
“I don’t see how such innocent remarks can be so misinterpreted,” she insisted, crumpling her handkerchief.
Jim did not answer at once. When he spoke his manner was authoritative.
“Don’t you think it would be wise to drop this topic, Mrs. Merwent?”
She preserved her air of dignity, but her uneasiness was obvious.
“I don’t understand you, Mr. Sprague. I certainly will not be stopped from doing my duty in protecting Lucy no matter if you do misunderstand me.”
“Protecting Lucy?” Jim’s eyes hardened.
Mrs. Merwent twisted her handkerchief nervously.
“Do you think Lucy needs to be protected from me, Mrs. Merwent?” he went on inexorably.
Lucy’s voice was heard calling Dimmie. Nannie backed toward the hallway.
Jim heard Lucy too.
“I think Lucy needs protecting from her own ignorance and inexperience,” explained Nannie defensively. “I’ve suffered enough from public opinion even though I was innocent, Mr. Sprague, and I can’t be expected to welcome the same thing for her.”
Jim seemed nonplussed.
“I confess you have the best of me, Mrs. Merwent.”
Nannie was mollified by seeing how disturbed he looked.
“I knew you would consider Lucy’s welfare just as I do.” Then she seemed to dismiss the discussion. “There she is now. She does spoil Jimmie so!”
“It has begun to rain, Dimmie. You mustn’t stay out in the rain. Where’s Papa?” Lucy was heard saying.
“Dear me! It *is* raining. I left a coat suit airing in the window. I’m packing away my winter things. You will excuse me while I go and take it in, Mr. Sprague?” Mrs. Merwent was smiling now, though she continued to regard Jim with apprehension.
Jim turned to face her suddenly.
“Hold on, Mrs. Merwent!” he began in an odd tone, his voice not quite under his control.
Nannie continued to smile impersonally but in spite of herself she hesitated.
“You will please tell me now just what you mean about Lucy and myself,” he demanded rather than asked.
“Maybe Uncle Jim knows where Papa is.” Lucy came toward the living room door.
Nannie raised her brows again and nodded at Jim significantly.
“Ahem﻿—I really don’t think we had better﻿—” she murmured.
Jim shrugged his shoulders with a baffled air and began to refill his pipe. At the same moment Lucy appeared in the doorway behind her mother.
“Where’s John?”

===Chinese===

「无意冒犯。」他分辩道，转过身把烟斗在壁炉架上磕了磕。
南妮还端着受辱的架势。
「我不明白，这样清清白白的话，怎么会被歪曲成这样。」她揉着手帕，不依不饶。
吉姆没有立刻回答。再开口时，他的神态带着不容置疑的威严。
「换个话题，是不是更明智些，默温特太太？」
她维持着尊严的神态，不安却显而易见。
「我不明白你的意思，斯普拉格先生。哪怕你误会我，也休想拦住我尽本分保护露西。」
「保护露西？」吉姆的眼睛冷了下来。
默温特太太紧张地绞着手帕。
「您是觉得露西需要提防我这个人吗，默温特太太？」他不依不饶地问下去。
外面传来露西唤迪米的声音。南妮朝门厅退去。
吉姆也听见了。
「我是觉得露西需要保护，免得她因为自己的无知和没阅历吃亏。」南妮辩解道，「我是清白无辜的，可舆论的苦头我已经吃够了，斯普拉格先生，总不能指望我眼看着她再吃一遍吧。」
吉姆像是被问住了。
「我承认您把我难住了，默温特太太。」
见他这般心绪不宁，南妮的气平了些。
「我就知道，露西的福祉你是同我一样上心的。」随即她像是把这番争论撇开了，「她来了。她可真宠吉米！」
「下起雨来了，迪米。可不能淋在外头。爸爸呢？」只听露西在说。
「哎呀，真下雨了。我还在窗口晾着一套衣裳呢。我正把冬衣收起来。失陪一下，我去把它收进来，斯普拉格先生？」默温特太太此刻又挂上了笑，尽管望着吉姆的眼神仍带着不安。
吉姆猛地转过脸来对着她。
「慢着，默温特太太！」他开口了，声调古怪，嗓音也不大听使唤。
南妮依旧挂着那种无关痛痒的笑，却不由自主地迟疑了一下。
「请您这就告诉我，您说露西和我之间的话，究竟是什么意思。」这已不是询问，而是要求。
「吉姆叔叔兴许知道爸爸在哪儿。」露西朝客厅门口走来。
南妮又扬起眉毛，意味深长地朝吉姆点头。
「咳——我看咱们最好还是别——」她低声道。
吉姆一脸受挫，耸了耸肩，开始重新装烟斗。正在这时，露西出现在母亲身后的门洞里。
「约翰呢？」

===Original===

Lucy asked, entering the room with Dimmie and glancing about inquiringly.
“Why he went out for a few minutes. I must run upstairs and take my clothes out of the rain,” Mrs. Merwent murmured, brushing past her daughter into the hallway.
“Whatever made John run away just when dinner was ready? And it’s begun to rain too!” Lucy walked over to the fireplace and placed a screen in front of it.
“Tell me a story, Uncle Jim,” Dimmie begged, pulling Jim’s coat tails and indicating the Morris chair which Nannie had quitted.
“It’s too near dinner time,” Lucy remonstrated, noticing how abstracted Jim appeared. She looked at the clock on the writing desk. “Is it as late as that?” she asked in surprise.
Jim took out his watch.
“Seven fifteen,” he informed her, replacing his timepiece, and walking over to the darkened window he remained, with his back to her, gazing into the faintly lit street.
She scrutinised his half averted face.
“Are you bothered about something, Jim?”
He started slightly.
“Why, no, not particularly. I was figuring out what would be the cheapest material we could put into that new row of houses,” he lied. “I’ve got to send in the final estimates tomorrow.” He moved away from the window and seated himself in the Morris chair with Dimmie in his lap.
“I can’t imagine what keeps John,” Lucy remarked again.
Jim slid Dimmie to the floor.
“Don’t you want me to go and hunt him up?”
“Why﻿—no‑o. I﻿—” Lucy demurred. “I don’t think﻿—”
Just then there was the sound of a key turning in a latch and Dimmie darted forward into the hall shouting, “There’s Papa! There’s Papa!”
John left a parcel on the stand with his hat and overcoat and entered the living room.
“Why didn’t you tell me that you intended going out, John? I wouldn’t have taken dinner up. I’m afraid it is spoiled,” Lucy said good naturedly as she came forward to meet him.
“Oh, you know I﻿—well﻿—” John explained running his fingers through his hair with an embarrassed gesture.
“What in the world did you have to buy that couldn’t wait until after dinner?” Lucy walked into the hall and picked up the package which lay beside his hat.
“Why, it’s some fruit. I thought it might be nice to have some,” he said lamely, following her to the hat stand.
“But there’s plenty of fruit in the house.”
Nannie appeared on the stairs.
“Why, where have you been, John? We were all looking for you,” she called rather uneasily.

===Chinese===

露西问着，领迪米进了屋，四下环顾。
「他出去几分钟。我得赶紧上楼把衣裳收进来，别淋着雨。」默温特太太低声说着，擦过女儿身边进了门厅。
「约翰也真是，晚饭刚做好怎么就跑出去了？偏偏还下起雨来！」露西走到壁炉前，在跟前立了扇屏风。
「给我讲个故事吧，吉姆叔叔。」迪米拽着吉姆的衣角，指指南妮空出来的那把莫里斯扶手椅。
「快到吃饭时间了。」露西劝道，一面留意到吉姆神情恍惚。她看了看写字台上的钟。「都这么晚了吗？」她惊讶地问。
吉姆掏出怀表。
「七点一刻。」他告诉她，收起怀表，走到暗下来的窗前，背对着她，望着街上隐约的灯光出神。
她端详着他半侧着的脸。
「你有心事吧，吉姆？」
他微微一怔。
「啊，没有，没什么特别的。我在算新那一排房子用什么料子最省。」他撒了个谎，「明天就得交最终预算了。」他离开窗边，坐进莫里斯扶手椅，把迪米抱在膝上。
「真想不出约翰怎么还不回来。」露西又说。
吉姆把迪米放下地。
「要不要我出去找找他？」
「这个——不用、不用。我——」露西迟疑着，「我不觉得——」
恰在此时，钥匙转动锁孔一响，迪米抢先冲进门厅，嚷着：「爸爸回来了！爸爸回来了！」
约翰把一个包裹连同帽子大衣撂在架子上，走进客厅。
「你要出门怎么不告诉我一声，约翰？我就不上菜了。只怕菜都放坏了。」露西迎上去，好脾气地说。
「哦，你知道我——这个——」约翰解释着，尴尬地用手指拢了拢头发。
「到底是什么东西，等不到饭后非得去买？」露西走进门厅，拿起搁在他帽子旁边的包裹。
「喳，是点水果。我想家里有点水果也好。」他跟到衣帽架前，吞吞吐吐地说。
「可家里水果多着呢。」
南妮出现在楼梯上。
「哎呀，你去哪儿了，约翰？我们都在找你呢。」她的呼唤带着几分不安。

## XI / 十一
===Original===

John and Nannie entered the dining room together. Jim followed with Lucy and Dimmie.
“Did Nannie give you those flowers, Uncle Jim?” Dimmie asked as they went through the hall. He reached for the boutonniere which Jim was wearing.
“Yes. Do you want some?” Jim answered, taking the sprig of blossoms from his button hole and halting to decorate Dimmie’s blouse with it.
In the dining room there was a little confusion as John pulled out a chair for Nannie and she seated herself with a rustle. She began to serve the soup. Jim observed Lucy stealthily.
When the plates were distributed Nannie noticed Dimmie’s adornment.
“Why, you’ve lost your flowers, Mr. Sprague!” she exclaimed, without looking at Jim.
“I gave them to Dimmie,” he answered quickly.
“Oh,” said Nannie.
John regarded Jim with an expression of surprise but lowered his eyes as Jim met his gaze.
“I don’t believe Mr. Sprague likes flowers, does he, Lucy?” inquired Mrs. Merwent sweetly.
“Of course he does, Mamma,” Lucy declared patiently. There was a brief silence.
“Now we’ll have one of the good old evenings!” cried John, rubbing his hands. “By the way, you two are too formal, Nannie. Why don’t you call each other by your names?”
“Mr. Sprague doesn’t approve of getting acquainted too rapidly,” retorted Nannie.
“I see by the paper that some rich guy has loaned a lot of Corots to the Field Museum. That ought to interest you, John,” remarked Jim, changing the subject.
“Do you know, I was thinking of Corot today,” responded John enthusiastically. “His art is a fairy art. He gives you the poetry of Nature without being irritatingly subjective.”
“I just love Corot,” put in Mrs. Merwent ecstatically.
“Don’t you!” pursued John eagerly. “I tell you, Nannie, that lots of greatly admired things make me sick, jammed full of Christian sentiment, or reminding one of literary illustrations. Why can’t artists stick to their purpose! What was that you said the other evening, Jim, about Botticelli’s *Spring*﻿—that it was both pagan and subtle, wasn’t it?”
“Forgotten,” said Jim, who was helping Dimmie to potatoes.
“That was it.” Lucy smiled approvingly. “Won’t you have another croquette, Mamma?”
“I believe I will, thank you,” assented Mrs. Merwent, even as she spoke accepting the helping. “There! There! That’s more than plenty, Lucy. You’ll have Mr. Sprague thinking I’m a gourmand.”

===Chinese===

约翰和南妮一同走进饭厅。吉姆跟着露西和迪米。
「这些花是南妮给你的吗，吉姆叔叔？」穿堂时迪米问，伸手去够吉姆扣眼里的襟花。
「是呀。你也想要？」吉姆答道，把那小簇花从扣眼里取下，停下来替迪米别在上衣上。
饭厅里乱了一小阵：约翰为南妮拉开椅子，她窸窸窣窣地坐下了。她开始盛汤。吉姆悄悄打量露西。
汤盘分下去后，南妮注意到迪米的点缀。
「哎呀，你的花不见啦，斯普拉格先生！」她喊道，看也不看吉姆。
「我给迪米了。」他赶紧回答。
「哦。」南妮说。
约翰惊奇地望着吉姆，可吉姆的目光一迎上来，他就垂下了眼。
「我看斯普拉格先生不喜欢花吧，是不是，露西？」默温特太太甜甜地问。
「他当然喜欢，妈妈。」露西耐心地声明。片刻沉默。
「今晚咱们可要好好过一个老式的快活晚上！」约翰搓着手嚷道。「对了，你们两位也太见外了，南妮。你们干吗不互相称呼名字？」
「斯普拉格先生不赞成这么快就熟络。」南妮反唇道。
「报上说有个阔佬把好多幅柯罗借给菲尔德博物馆了。约翰，这个你该感兴趣。」吉姆岔开话题说。
「你们知道吗，我今天正好在想柯罗，」约翰热烈地应道，「他的艺术是仙境的艺术。他给你自然之诗，却不拿叫人腻味的主观来烦你。」
「我真爱柯罗。」默温特太太心醉神迷地插话。
「可不是嘛！」约翰热切地追下去。「南妮，我告诉你，好多备受吹捧的东西叫我恶心，塞满了基督教的滥情，或者活像书里的插图。艺术家干吗不守住本分！吉姆，前几天晚上你说波提切利的《春》怎么来看着——既是异教的，又精妙，是不是？」
「忘了。」吉姆说，正帮着迪米夹土豆。
「就是那话。」露西赞许地笑了。「再来一块可乐饼吧，妈妈？」
「好吧，谢谢你。」默温特太太应道，话音未落那一份已经接了过去。「哎！哎！够多啦，露西。你要让斯普拉格先生当我是个馋痨鬼了。」

===Original===

“Good stuff, Lucy,” declared Jim, taking a second croquette himself. “Wouldn’t blame your mother if she were.”
“But really, Mr. Sprague, as a rule﻿—”
She was interrupted by John.
“Jim, why on earth can’t you call Nannie by her name! One would think she was a perfect stranger.”
“She is, nearly,” said Lucy, smiling again.
“Well, we’re all starting over now,” returned John in a tone of mild reprimand, “and she won’t be in the future, so let’s not be so formal.”
An uncomfortable pause followed.
“You’ve dropped your flower, John.” As Nannie spoke at last, she picked up the boutonniere from the table and replaced it in the lapel of his coat. “What was that you were saying about art?”
“Oh! Well you know, Nannie, I was just going to say that I don’t have any more use for these new movements in art than for the moralizing and story telling things of the last century. These cubist and futuristic cranks forget the same thing the others do. That is that the artist’s purpose is to create beauty. Why can’t they give us the beauty they see and let us judge of it, instead of trying to tell us something about it we aren’t interested in. Now take Inness, for instance﻿—”
“I just love him! Aren’t his things fine!” Nannie chimed in.
John rumpled his hair.
“Yes, an Inness,” he repeated. “Doesn’t it give you enough? Can’t you look at it and hear the wind in the trees? It isn’t only atmosphere, it’s the beauty of nature in its simplicity, and that’s what we want in art﻿—simplicity.” He looked around the table.
“You’ve thought a lot about such things,” exclaimed Mrs. Merwent admiringly.
“It isn’t so much an intellectual conception as a feeling for the true thing that counts in art, Nannie,” replied John.
“That’s what I mean,” she explained.
“Your dinner’ll get cold, dear,” interrupted Lucy.
John began to eat again and silence reigned for some moments.
Lucy turned to Jim.
“The croquettes aren’t all gone yet. Have another?” she invited.
“No, thanks.”
“I’ll take one, Lucy, if you don’t mind,” interposed Nannie. Lucy passed the dish hastily.
“Why of course, Mamma.”
Silence descended again on the group.
“We seem to be a quiet family,” Mrs. Merwent observed after a few moments.
John looked up from his plate.
“What’s the matter with you, Jim? And you too, Lucy?” he inquired, glancing from one of them to the other. “It seems as though a funeral had struck the place.

===Chinese===

「手艺好，露西。」吉姆宣称，自己也拿了一块可乐饼。「你母亲真是馋痨鬼我也不怪她。」
「可是说真的，斯普拉格先生，照例——」
约翰打断了她。
「吉姆，你究竟是怎么回事，连南妮的名字都不肯叫！人家还当她是个十足的外人呢。」
「她也差不多就是。」露西说着又笑了。
「好了，如今咱们都从头开始，」约翰用温和责备的口吻回敬，「往后她不再是外人了，所以别这么客套。」
接着是一阵不好受的冷场。
「你的花掉了，约翰。」南妮终于开口，一面从桌上拾起那朵襟花，替他别回上衣翻领上。「你刚才说到艺术，说到哪儿了？」
「哦！是这样，南妮，我正想说：如今艺术里这些新流派，同上个世纪那些说教、讲故事的货色一样，我全都用不着。那些立体派、未来派的怪物，忘掉的东西同那帮人一样，就是：艺术家的天职是创造美。他们干吗不把自己看见的美给我们看，让我们自己评判，反倒非要给我们讲一套我们毫不感兴趣的东西？就拿英尼斯来说——」
「我真爱他！他的画多好呀！」南妮插进来。
约翰挠了挠头发。
「是啊，一幅英尼斯，」他重复道。「看着还不够吗？你看着它，不就能听见树梢的风声吗？那不光是气氛，是自然在质朴中的美，而艺术里我们要的正是这个——质朴。」他环视餐桌。
「这些事你想得真多。」默温特太太赞叹道。
「南妮，艺术里要紧的与其说是智识上的概念，不如说是对真东西的直感。」约翰答道。
「我说的就是这个意思。」她解释道。
「饭菜要凉了，亲爱的。」露西打断他。
约翰重新吃起来，席间静了好一会儿。
露西转向吉姆。
「可乐饼还没吃完呢。再来一块？」她让道。
「不了，谢谢。」
「露西，你不介意的话，我倒要一块。」南妮插嘴。露西赶紧把盘子递过去。
「当然不介意，妈妈。」
沉默又罩了下来。
「咱们这一家子可真够安静的。」过了片刻，默温特太太议论道。
约翰从盘子上抬起头来。
「你们俩怎么了，吉姆？还有你，露西？」他从这一个望到那一个。「这地方像是叫丧事罩住了。

===Original===

Neither of you have a word to say tonight.”
“Seems to me I’m talking as much as usual,” said Jim defensively, laughing a little. “The trouble is that you’ve quit talking. We always listen to you.”
“Rot!” retorted John. “You haven’t said half a dozen words.”
“I thought you all discussed the most abstruse things,” put in Nannie.
“We do,” avowed John. “I can’t understand what’s come over them.”
“Do talk about something profound, Mr. Sprague. I love to listen even if I can’t join in the discussion,” she urged.
“You’re really mistaken, Mrs. Merwent. I talk very little,” Jim protested. “John’s joking.”
“I suppose it depends on the company you’re in,” she parried.
“I talked a lot to you before dinner.” Jim turned his eyes on her.
Nannie avoided his gaze.
“My friend Professor Walsh, who is head of the school at home, has a better opinion of me than some other people,” she declared after a pause, ignoring Jim’s remark and speaking to the table at large.
“What nonsense, Mamma!” Lucy interrupted gently. “Whoever made any comparison!”
“Comparisons can be made by inference,” Mrs. Merwent insisted with dignity. “Professor Walsh knows all kinds of erudite things and *he* never considers it any condescension to talk to poor little me!” she finished.
“Neither do we! What’s got into you, Nannie?” John exclaimed, slightly irritated, but laughing.
“I know *you* don’t, John.” Nannie smiled at him.
Lucy rose.
“Help me bring in the dessert, Jim,” she invited.
“Lucy does need a servant,” Nannie declared when she and John were alone together.
---
After dinner the men remained in their places to smoke as was their custom, and a little later they all drew their chairs back and the conversation became more animated as John related at length some of his experiences at the art school. Then Lucy began to clear the table.
Jim glanced once or twice at Mrs. Merwent who was listening to John and asking questions about various girls mentioned by him in the course of his narrative.
“Let me help you with the dishes, Lucy,” Jim lowered his voice slightly.
“Why of course, Jim. I thought you had forgotten your job,” Lucy agreed, at the same time regarding her mother, who seemed much amused by something John had just said and entirely oblivious to her daughter.
Jim removed his coat and he and Lucy carried the dishes to the kitchen, Dimmie assisting with invaluable efforts and advice.

===Chinese===

你们俩今晚一句囫囵话都没有。」
「我觉得我说得不比平时少呀，」吉姆略带辩解地说，笑了一笑。「毛病出在你不多说了。我们向来都是听你说的。」
「胡说！」约翰反驳。「你连六句话都没说。」
「我还以为你们尽谈些最深奥的题目呢。」南妮插话。
「我们就是谈的。」约翰宣称。「我不明白他们今晚中了什么邪。」
「那就谈点高深的吧，斯普拉格先生。就算我插不上嘴，我也爱听。」她怂恿道。
「您实在误会了，默温特太太。我话很少的，」吉姆分辩，「约翰说着玩呢。」
「我想这得看同什么人做伴吧。」她挡了回去。
「饭前我同您说得不少。」吉姆把眼睛转向她。
南妮避开了他的目光。
「我的朋友沃尔什教授——我们那儿那所学校的校长——对我的评价，可比另外某些人高。」停了停，她宣布，不理吉姆的话茬，冲着满桌人说。
「胡说什么呀，妈妈！」露西轻声打断。「谁拿你比来着！」
「比较也可以不言之而喻嘛。」默温特太太端着架子坚持道。「沃尔什教授懂得种种高深的学问，*他*同可怜的小女子我说话，可从来不觉得是屈尊！」她说完道。
「我们也不觉得呀！你这是怎么了，南妮？」约翰嚷道，有点恼，但还是笑着。
「我知道*你*不觉得，约翰。」南妮朝他嫣然一笑。
露西站起身。
「吉姆，来帮我端甜点吧。」她邀道。
「露西真是缺个下人。」只剩她同约翰两人时，南妮下了断语。
---
饭后，两个男人照他们的老习惯留在座上抽烟，过了一会儿大家都把椅子往后挪，谈话重新热闹起来——约翰细细讲了些他在美术学校的经历。随后露西开始收拾桌子。
吉姆朝默温特太太瞟了一两眼，她正听约翰讲，就他叙述中提到的几个姑娘问长问短。
「露西，碗我来帮你洗。」吉姆把嗓门略略压低。
「那敢情好，吉姆。我还当你忘了你的差事呢。」露西应道，同时望着母亲——母亲像是被约翰刚说的一句什么话逗得大乐，全然没把女儿放在心上。
吉姆脱下外衣，同露西一道把碗盏端进厨房，迪米也来效力，出的力和出的主意都无比宝贵。

===Original===

While the dish washing was in progress Mrs. Merwent appeared in the kitchen doorway, and surveyed the scene of activity: Jim, coatless and aproned, Lucy with her sleeves rolled up, and Dimmie in general administrative charge.
“Why, Lucy, I didn’t know you were washing the dishes! Do let me help!” Nannie begged rather weakly.
Lucy replied pleasantly.
“No, thank you, Mamma. We’ll be through in a minute.”
Nannie went back to the dining room.
“They don’t seem to need us out there,” she informed John. “Do tell me some more about that Miss Stimpson at the Art School﻿—the one with the red hair. I think she’s so interesting.”
Soon Lucy and Jim returned to the dining room. Dimmie was hanging to his mother’s skirt and rubbing his eyes. She looked down at the child.
“I think I know a little boy who wants to go to sleepy town.” She smiled at him and took his hand. “Kiss Papa and Uncle Jim,” she continued, leading him up to John.
“And Nannie! Would you forget poor Nannie?” complained Mrs. Merwent, presenting her cheek which Dimmie dutifully kissed.
“Good night, Dimmie,” Jim called after the child.
“You’re quite domestic, Mr. Sprague,” Nannie commented after Lucy and Dimmie had gone upstairs.
“Lucy turns old Jim into a regular hired girl when he’s here.” John grinned at his friend.
Jim was lighting his pipe and did not show that he had heard Nannie’s remark. When he spoke it was to John.
Dimmie fell asleep in the midst of the first bedtime story, and Lucy soon left him. When she came into the dining room her mother was moving about as though searching for something.
“What is it, Mamma?” inquired Lucy.
“Why a piece of sheet music I put here on the bookcase doesn’t seem to be here. Jimmie must have moved it.”
“Let me help you find it,” offered John, jumping to his feet.
“Perhaps it’s on the piano where you were practicing this afternoon,” suggested Lucy.
Mrs. Merwent disappeared into the living room, followed by John, and a moment later the strains of Massenet’s “Ouvrez tes Yeux” floated in to the dining room.
“Sit down, Jim,” Lucy bade Sprague, who was standing with his hands in his coat pockets.
He seated himself a little distance from her.
“Well, Jim, Mamma and I are not much alike, are we?” Lucy remarked, smiling with a tired expression.
“Wait till I pinch myself,” Jim answered.
There was a brief pause.

===Chinese===

洗碗正洗着，默温特太太出现在厨房门口，把这一片忙碌打量了一番：吉姆脱了外衣系着围裙，露西挽着袖子，迪米总揽全局。
「哎呀露西，我不知道你们在洗碗呢！快让我来搭把手！」南妮有气无力地恳求。
露西愉快地答道：「不用了，谢谢妈妈。马上就好。」
南妮回了饭厅。
「外头看来用不着咱们，」她告诉约翰。「再给我讲讲美术学校那位斯廷普森小姐吧——红头发的那位。我觉得她真有意思。」
不多时露西和吉姆回到饭厅。迪米吊在母亲裙边揉眼睛。她低头看孩子。
「我看有个小家伙要去瞌睡国喽。」她朝他笑笑，牵起他的手。「亲亲爸爸和吉姆叔叔，」她继续说，把他领到约翰跟前。
「还有南妮呢！就把可怜的南妮忘啦？」默温特太太抱怨着，把脸颊送过去，迪米乖乖亲了一下。
「晚安，迪米。」吉姆冲孩子背后喊。
「你可真会持家，斯普拉格先生。」露西和迪米上楼后，南妮评点道。
「露西把老吉姆使唤成正经的丫头婆子了，他一来了就是。」约翰冲朋友咧嘴一笑。
吉姆正点烟斗，没理会南妮的话茬。他开口时是对着约翰的。
迪米听着听着头一个睡前故事就睡着了，露西很快离开了他。她走进饭厅时，母亲正四处转悠，像在找什么。
「找什么呀，妈妈？」露西问。
「喏，我搁在书柜上的一份乐谱好像不见了。准是吉米给挪走了。」
「我来帮你找。」约翰一跃而起。
「兴许在钢琴上，你下午练琴那会儿。」露西提醒。
默温特太太走进客厅，约翰跟上，片刻之后，马斯涅的《睁开你的眼睛》的曲调飘进饭厅。
「坐吧，吉姆。」露西招呼斯普拉格——他正把手插在外衣口袋里站着。
他在离她稍远的地方坐下了。
「我说吉姆，我和妈妈长得不太像，是吧？」露西说，带着倦容笑了笑。
「等我掐自己一把再说。」吉姆答道。
短暂的停顿。

===Original===

“We’ll just have to do the best we can with things,” Lucy said, rising and averting her face as she spoke. She walked over to the mantel and arranged some flowers in a vase.
“I’m outclassed,” Jim admitted.
“Maybe it’s not for always.” She spoke consolingly, looking at the floor and continuing to smile determinedly. “When people see things clearly there’s always a way, so let’s not get panicky.”
“The trouble is we don’t all see through things.” Jim shrugged his shoulders uncomfortably.
The music ceased abruptly but almost immediately began again and Nannie sang, “Vous Dansez, Marquise.”
“Give John a little time, Jim. He’ll see more than the rest of us before long,” Lucy went on, as though defending her husband from an unspoken accusation.
Jim smoked fiercely.
“I hope so,” he said at last, “but, as a rule, meeting a problem on the installment plan is pretty poor spiritual economy.”
“Lucy,” he resumed after a pause, “your mother and I had a talk before dinner.”
“I thought so.”
“Well, unless I’m greatly mistaken﻿—” Jim hesitated.
The music had stopped.
“Lucy, why don’t you and Jim come in and listen?” John stood in the doorway.
Mrs. Merwent’s laugh tinkled from the other room.
“John, you dear silly boy!” she called. “You should allow people to enjoy themselves in their own way.”
“I was just going back to town,” announced Jim, standing up. Lucy stared at him with a surprised expression.
At this juncture Nannie appeared in the doorway behind John. Both entered the dining room.
“Why what do you mean, Jim?” John inquired.
“I’ve got to get to the office early tomorrow.”
“I thought Mr. Sprague *always* spent the night,” remarked Nannie. “Lucy showed me his room.”
“He does,” declared John, and turned to Jim. “You’ll have plenty of time in the morning. We’ll take the six forty.”
“I must look over the specifications for those houses, and they’re in my room.”
“Well, if you must, you must,” John agreed, “but why didn’t you say so before?”
“Goodbye, Mrs. Merwent,” Jim said, ignoring John’s question. “Good night, Lucy.” They shook hands.
“I’ll go to the station with you,” John offered.
“No,” declined Jim. “No need. I’ve just time to catch the nine five. Good night.” He went into the hall and seized his hat from the rack.
“Good night,” called John. “See you in the morning.”
Dimmie, awakened by the noise in the hall, cried out, “Mamma!”
Lucy went to him.

「咱们只能尽力把日子过好。」露西说着站起来，把脸偏了过去。她走到壁炉边，把瓶里的花插弄了弄。
「我不是对手。」吉姆认了。
「也许不是永远这样。」她宽慰道，眼睛望着地板，仍旧强笑着。「人把事情看清楚了，总会有办法的，所以咱们先别慌。」
「毛病是咱们看事没看透。」吉姆不自在地耸耸肩。
琴声戛然而止，随即又响起来，南妮唱起了《您跳舞吧，侯爵夫人》。
「吉姆，给约翰一点时间。用不了多久，他会比咱们谁都看得清楚。」露西继续说，像是在替丈夫辩护一句没说出口的指控。
吉姆狠狠抽着烟。
「但愿如此，」他终于说，「不过照例说，把难题分期付款地应付，精神上未免太不经济了。」
「露西，」停了停他又开口，「晚饭前你母亲同我谈了一阵。」
「我猜也是。」
「呃，除非我大大地看错了——」吉姆迟疑了。
琴声停了。
「露西，你和吉姆干吗不进来听听？」约翰站在门口。
默温特太太的笑声从那边的屋里叮当传来。
「约翰，你这个傻宝贝孩子！」她喊道。「该让人家用自己的法子自己乐去嘛。」
「我这就回城里去。」吉姆宣布着站起来。露西惊讶地瞪着他。
正在这时，南妮出现在约翰背后的门口。两人都进了饭厅。
「呃，你这是什么意思，吉姆？」约翰问。
「我明早得一早到事务所。」
「我还以为斯普拉格先生*向来*住这儿呢，」南妮说，「露西带我看过他的房间。」
「他是住这儿的。」约翰宣称，转向吉姆。「你早上时间宽裕得很。咱们搭六点四十那班。」
「我得翻一翻那几所房子的规格书，都在我屋里放着呢。」
「好吧，非走不可那就走，」约翰依了，「可你干吗不早说？」
「再见，默温特太太。」吉姆说，不理约翰的问话。「晚安，露西。」两人握了手。
「我送你去车站。」约翰提出。
「不，」吉姆谢绝，「不用。我刚好赶得上九点五分那班。晚安。」他走进门厅，从衣帽架上抓起帽子。
「晚安，」约翰喊道，「明儿见。」
迪米被门厅里的响动惊醒，喊了一声：「妈妈！」
露西到他那边去了。

===Original===

An hour later she heard John and her mother tiptoeing up the stairs, trying to avoid disturbing her.
---
John had breakfast early the succeeding morning as he wished to reach the office simultaneously with Jim. When Mrs. Merwent came downstairs it was already close to the lunch hour and she asked to have her breakfast in the kitchen so that she might talk to Lucy who was at work there.
“Your friend, Mr. Sprague, doesn’t like me,” Mrs. Merwent began as she buttered her toast contemplatively.
“Why, Mamma, I don’t see what makes you say that.” Lucy measured some flour.
“Do you mean you didn’t notice what he said after dinner about parents being in secret competition with their children?”
“Yes, I heard that, but he was speaking generally. He was probably not thinking about you at all.”
“No, he doesn’t consider me intellectual enough to be worth thinking about. Professor Walsh is a great deal better educated than Mr. Sprague, so I guess I can console myself with the fact that he *does* find me worth talking to.”
Lucy referred again to her recipe book.
“Really, Mamma, you have no reason to feel this way. I’m sure Jim was as attentive to you as you let him be.”
“Well, it was mere politeness. He wasn’t at all interested in anything I said.”
“I’m sure I don’t know how you know. He listened to everything you said to him. Of course he doesn’t know any of the people you talked about, and he isn’t good at small talk, and besides you talked almost entirely to John, but I thought he was very nice.”
“Very patronizing, you mean, Lucy. I can see very plainly that he has a low opinion of women﻿—except you, of course. He seemed anxious enough to talk to you.”
“Mamma, I wish you wouldn’t speak that way.”
“Speak what way?”
Lucy paused in her task and gazed steadily at her mother.
“You know perfectly well, Mamma. The reason he talked to me was that you wished it, and besides I ask you not to talk about Jim in that tone.”
“Well, anyone could see that he was crazy to be with you,” Nannie began angrily.
“Mamma!” Lucy was indignant.
Her mother’s eyes filled with tears.
“Why, Lucy, I don’t see how you can twist my words so. I didn’t mean there was anything between you.”
Lucy gasped.
“Who said you did?” she ejaculated, articulating with difficulty. “I only meant that you were unjust to Jim.”
Mrs. Merwent continued to regard her daughter reproachfully.
“You don’t know him, Mamma.

===Chinese===

一个钟头后，她听见约翰同母亲踮着脚上楼，生怕搅扰她。
---
第二天早晨约翰提早吃了早饭，他想同吉姆同时到事务所。默温特太太下楼时已临近午饭钟点，她要人把早饭送到厨房，好同正在那儿忙活的露西说话。
「你那位朋友斯普拉格先生不喜欢我。」默温特太太一边若有所思地往吐司上抹黄油，一边开腔。
「哎呀妈妈，我不明白你怎么说这话。」露西量着面粉。
「你是说你没留意他饭后说的那话——做父母的同儿女暗地里较劲？」
「听是听见了，可他讲的是泛论，八成压根儿没想到你。」
「不，他嫌我学问不够，不值得想。沃尔什教授受的教育比斯普拉格先生强得多，我想我可以拿这个自宽自解：教授*倒*觉得我值得交谈。」
露西又低头看她的食谱。
「真的，妈妈，你没有理由这样想。吉姆待你有多殷勤，还不是随你给几分。」
「哼，那不过是客气。我说什么他都毫无兴趣。」
「我倒不知道你怎么看出来的。你跟他说的话他句句都听了。他当然不认识你讲的那班人，又不善应酬闲谈，再说你差不多全是同约翰说话，可我觉得他挺好的。」
「你是说他挺居高临下，露西。我看得明明白白，他瞧不起女人——当然你除外。他同你说话的那副急切劲儿还不够瞧的吗。」
「妈妈，我希望你别这么说话。」
「怎么说？」
露西停下手里的活，直直地望着母亲。
「您心里明白得很，妈妈。他同我说话，是因为您愿意他这样。再说，请您不要用那种口气谈论吉姆。」
「哼，谁都看得出他巴不得同你在一起。」南妮怒冲冲地开口。
「妈妈！」露西动了气。
母亲的眼里涌上泪水。
「哎呀露西，我不明白你怎么能把我的话拧成这样。我可没说你们俩有什么。」
露西倒抽一口气。
「谁说你说了？」她冲口而出，字都咬不清。「我只是说你冤枉了吉姆。」
默温特太太仍旧用责备的眼光望着女儿。
「你不了解他，妈妈。

===Original===

He’s the very best kind of a man,” Lucy went on more gently.
“Yes! I suppose I am incapable of appreciating him. Well, *you* seem to at any rate,” Nannie taunted.
Lucy paused ominously.
“Well, Mamma, if you don’t like him, I can’t help it,” she resumed at last in an odd strained voice. “He’s our friend, but I’m not responsible for him or his opinions. You’ll just have to like him or dislike him for yourself.”
“I never said I disliked him,” retorted Nannie. “I said he disliked me. Anyone would think to hear you talk that he was﻿—”
“Suppose we don’t discuss it any longer, Mamma,” Lucy interrupted shortly.
“Oh, very well. If I had known you couldn’t bear to have the slightest hint of criticism of him I’d have kept still.”
Lucy said no more.

他是顶好的一种男人。」露西放缓了声气，接着说。
「是呀！想必我是没眼光欣赏他。哼，反正*你*是有的。」南妮讥诮道。

「好了妈妈，你要是不喜欢他，我也没办法，」她终于重新开口，声音古怪而紧绷。「他是我们的朋友，可他也好、他的见解也好，我都担待不起。您喜欢他也好，不喜欢也好，只得您自己去拿主意。」
「我可从没说我不喜欢他，」南妮反驳，「我说的是他不喜欢我。听你这口气，人家还当他是我什么人——」
