「我只看了标题，没读内文。」
这顿饭剩下的时光，谈话有气无力，约翰几乎不说话，露西更是一言不发。
凯蒂端上甜点时，南妮悄声对约翰说：「布丁是我做的。」
「好得很。」约翰悄声回。
离席后，默温特太太同约翰径直进了客厅，不开灯，低声交谈。
「你注意到没有，露西连对斯普拉格先生都那样古怪？」南妮说话时朝门口瞟了一眼。
「谁摊上他都得古怪。」约翰恼火地回。
「可不是嘛，亲爱的约翰，你对他多能忍。可露西是病了，这没跑。我是说，脑筋病了。哦约翰，我多想帮你走出这个可怕的境况！」
他握了握她的手。
「南妮，天知道，你每喘一口气都在帮我。」他动情地断言。
饭厅里，露西和吉姆默默对坐。吉姆一反常态，没有抽烟。
「露西，你生我的气了吗？」他终于问。
「没有，吉姆。」回答是这两个字。
「到底怎么了？」
露西的眼里涌上泪，她摇了摇头。
「吉米，我说不出口。」停了停，她低声说。
吉姆望着她苍白的脸和发抖的嘴唇，眼神深沉。
「露西，我什么都肯做，什么都肯——只要能帮你。」
「谢谢你，吉姆。我知道你肯。」
「那就让我试试？」他开腔。
她摇头。
「不，吉姆。没用的。谁也帮不了谁。我——我——」她的眼里又噙满泪。
「露西，」吉姆的声音绷得紧紧的，「你要是知道我多么——看重你的情谊——多么——」
「别说了——吉姆——」她压着嗓子说。
两人许久无话。客厅里约翰和南妮的低语不断。
「我走了。」吉姆起身伸出手。
露西把手放上去。他握得比以往任何时候都更热、更久。
「露西，你要是需要我——就是说，如果我——」他的声音发颤，目光却执拗地寻着她的。「如果你需要我。」他重复，顿住了。
「谢谢你，吉姆。」
「替我向迪米道晚安。」

===Original===

He went out without saying good night to John and Nannie. Lucy did not go to the door with him.
“Excuse me,” Mrs. Merwent called from the living room after a time. “I’m coming in.” She did not show herself immediately.
Lucy made no reply, and Nannie approached the door and looked in.
“Why, where is Mr. Sprague?” she inquired.
“He has gone home.” Lucy’s expression was hostile. “And if he were here, you need not insult us both by warning us that you were coming.”
“Why, Lucy, I only thought﻿—”
“You only thought something vile, as usual,” Lucy interrupted.
“What right have you got to say such a thing as that to me?”
“You have given me the right to say far worse things to you. You are not in a position to defend yourself from anything I care to say.” Lucy’s tone was hard. Nannie could not meet her daughter’s eyes.
“What do you mean by that?” demanded Mrs. Merwent angrily.
“You know very well what I mean,” declared Lucy evenly, rising and starting toward the hall.
John appeared on the threshold.
“Where’s Jim?” he questioned.
“He’s gone home,” Nannie informed him.
“Well, what did he sneak out without saying a word for?”
Lucy’s eyes glinted, but she only said, “You’d better ask him. I don’t know.”
Dimmie ran in from the kitchen.
“Mamma! Mrs. Hamilton wants﻿—” he began.
“Come to bed. You can tell me upstairs.” Lucy caught the child’s hand.
The two went out.
“John, what in the world do you suppose is the matter with Lucy? She treats me terribly.”
“I’m damned if I know!” exclaimed John fiercely. “I’m sick of it.”
“I don’t know what’s to become of us if she keeps on like this,” Nannie continued.
“Well, I know what will become of *me* if it goes on much longer!” His tone was eloquent.
“Now, John,” Mrs. Merwent placed her hand on the sleeve of his coat. “For my sake, John. I have suffered so much for Lucy already.” Tears seemed near.
“I’ll sit tight and do the best I can, Nannie, but as much as you’ve done for me, there are some things I can’t bear.”
Nannie sighed.
“I’ve noticed that every time she sees Mr. Sprague she’s worse,” she remarked significantly a moment later.
She and John stood looking into each other’s faces.
“Poor John.” She took one of his hands in both hers.
He returned appreciatively the pressure she gave it, then moved away.
“Are you going to bed so early?” she interrogated uneasily.
“No. I’m going to take a walk,” he answered.

===Chinese===

他没有同约翰和南妮道晚安就走了。露西也没有送到门口。
「失陪了，」过了一阵，默温特太太从客厅喊，「我进来了啊。」她却没有立刻露面。
露西不答。南妮走到门口张望。
「咦，斯普拉格先生呢？」她问。
「回家了。」露西一脸敌意。「再说他要是在这儿，也用不着你来『警告』我们要进来了——那是对我们两个人的侮辱。」
「哎呀露西，我只是想——」
「你想的又是些脏事，同往常一样。」露西打断她。
「你有什么权利对我说这种话？」
「你给我的权利，够我说你更难听的话。我想说什么就说什么，你无从辩白。」露西的口吻很硬。南妮不敢迎女儿的的目光。
「你这是什么意思？」默温特太太怒问。
「你心里明白得很。」露西平平地说，起身往门厅走。
约翰在门边出现了。
「吉姆呢？」他问。
「回家了。」南妮告知。
「嗯，他怎么不辞而别，一声不吭就溜？」
露西的眼里闪了一下，可她只说：「你最好问他。我不知道。」
迪米从厨房跑进来。
「妈妈！汉密尔顿太太要——」他刚开口。
「上床睡觉。有话上楼说。」露西抓住孩子的手。
两人出去了。
「约翰，露西究竟是怎么回事？她待我凶透了。」
「我要是知道才见鬼！」约翰狠狠地喊。「我烦透了。」
「她要是一直这样下去，咱们可怎么得了。」南妮继续道。
「哼，再拖下去，*我*可怎么得了，这我知道！」他的语调大有余味。
「好了约翰，」默温特太太把手搭在他外衣袖上，「看在我的份上，约翰。我为露西已经受够了。」眼泪就要下来。
「我撑得住，尽力而为，南妮。可你为我做的再多，有些事我忍不下。」
南妮叹了口气。
「我看得出来，她每回见斯普拉格先生，病就重一层。」片刻后她意味深长地说。
她和约翰对面站着，你望我，我望你。
「可怜的约翰。」她用双手握住他的一只手。
他领受了她那一握的谢意，随即走开。
「这就睡了？」她不安地问。
「不。出去走走。」他答。

===Original===

And the door slammed after him.

===Chinese===

门在他身后砰地关上了。

## XXVII / 二十七
===Original===

Lucy rose early the following day, and when John and Nannie came downstairs they found her, pale and haggard, moving about the kitchen giving instructions to Katy.
“Now, Lucy,” objected Mrs. Merwent, “you shouldn’t do this. Dr. Hamilton said you were to have absolute rest.” Nannie herself looked fresh and immaculate. “If you don’t stop frowning like that,” she added, “nothing on earth will ever take the wrinkles out of your forehead.”
Without replying to her mother, Lucy finished her marketing list.
“Put breakfast on, Katy,” she said to the servant.
“Yes, you may put breakfast on, Katy,” repeated Mrs. Merwent. “Mr. Winter is waiting.”
“Yes, Miss Nannie. Right dis minute,” replied the negress with alacrity.
The postman’s whistle blew and, as Katy was busy with the dishes, Nannie went to the door. She returned with a letter in her hand.
“I suppose your father has some more insults to heap on me,” she observed acridly, as she gave the envelope to Lucy.
Lucy glanced at it and recognized Mr. Merwent’s handwriting. She left the room.
“Oh, don’t be afraid! I’m not going to ask you what’s in it!” Nannie called after her in a trembling voice.
John was reading the morning paper in the living room and Dimmie was out of doors, so when Lucy mounted the stairs the upper floor was deserted. She entered her bedroom and, locking the door, seated herself weakly on the edge of her bed. She opened the letter.

===Chinese===

第二天露西起得很早。约翰和南妮下楼来，见她脸色苍白、形容憔悴，正在厨房里走来走去指挥凯蒂。
「哎，露西，」默温特太太拦阻道，「这可不行。汉密尔顿医生说了，你要绝对静养。」南妮自己倒显得容光焕发、一尘不染。「你要是总这么皱着眉，」她又补一句，「天底下什么也去不掉你额头上的皱纹了。」
露西不答母亲的话，把买菜的单子开完。
「上早饭吧，凯蒂。」她对女佣说。
「对，你可以上早饭了，凯蒂。」默温特太太跟着说，「温特先生等着呢。」
「好嘞，南妮小姐。这就上。」黑人妇人麻利地应道。
邮差的哨声响了。凯蒂正忙着手里的碗碟，南妮便去开门，回来时手里拿着一封信。
「想必你父亲又添了些骂我的话。」她尖刻地说着，把信封递给露西。
露西瞥了一眼，认出是默温特先生的笔迹。她出了屋。
「嗐，别怕！我可不会问里头写了什么！」南妮在她身后喊，声音发颤。
约翰在客厅看晨报，迪米在屋外，所以露西上楼时楼上空无一人。她进了卧室，锁上门，浑身无力地坐在床沿，拆开了信。

===Original===

> “Dear Daughter,” it began. “We leave in the morning as I expected. Ellen and I had hoped to see you and the boy once more before our departure, but Miss Storms learned through your husband that you were not coming again, and also that you were not well. I can understand that your nerves have been nearly ruined and imagine your condition.
> “It seems strange that peace loving people like you and me and Ellen should be forced to quarrel with each other. I do not blame you (you will say I have no right to do so), nor is it my place to comment on the attitude your husband has taken. Sufficient to say that he has accepted your mother’s assessment of values in family matters.
> “I know that you wish to remain neutral, but I have learned to my own sorrow the impossibility of a neutral peace where your mother is concerned. It seems to me, as things are, that the kindest thing Ellen and I can do in your interest is to do nothing and consider ourselves entirely out of the matter.
> “Remember, Lucy, no matter what you may think of me, that I blame you in nothing, and that I try to regard your husband’s hostility to us with as much detachment as is in accord with my respect and affection for Ellen and the right I feel to demand that respect from others.
> “Kiss the little boy for us both. We hope from our hearts that your indisposition is not serious. You know the cure. Ellen, in particular, regrets the impossible circumstances, etc. But why say more of that?
> “Your friend and father,

“Arthur Merwent.”

===Chinese===

> 「亲爱的女儿，」信是这样开头的。「我们明早动身，一如我所料。埃伦和我本希望在临行前再见你和孩子一面，可是斯托姆斯小姐从你丈夫那里得知你们不来了，还得知你身子不适。我明白，你的神经差不多毁掉了，你的境况我可以想见。
> 「像你我同埃伦这样爱好安宁的人，竟被迫彼此失和，说来奇怪。我不怪你（你会说我没有权利怪你）；你丈夫所采取的态度，也轮不到我来置喙。只消说一句：在家庭事务上，他已经接受了你母亲那一套价值标准。
> 「我知道你愿意恪守中立。可是关于你母亲这个人，中立的不可能，我是拿自己的伤痛学会的。照如今的情况看，埃伦和我为你计，最仁慈的做法就是什么也不做，把自己完全置身事外。
> 「记住，露西，不论你怎样看我，我丝毫不怪你；对你丈夫对我们的敌意，我尽量以超然处之——这既合于我对埃伦的敬重与情谊，也合于我认为自己有权要求他人给予的那份敬重。
> 「替我们两个亲亲小家伙。我们由衷希望你的不适不重。你知道药方在哪里。尤其是埃伦，对这无法两全的处境深表遗憾，云云。可这些又何必多说呢？
> 「你的朋友和父亲

「亚瑟·默温特」

===Original===

Lucy sat for some time with the letter in her hand. When she finally rose it was to begin to dress for the street.
A little later she descended the stairs, wearing her hat and carrying her gloves and purse. Nannie and John were breakfasting when she entered the dining room and took her place at the table.
“You’re not going out this morning, are you, Lucy?” Nannie inquired, staring at her daughter’s apparel. “You know the doctor said you were to keep quiet.”
“Yes, I’m going out,” Lucy told her.
“Now, Lucy, you ought not to disobey the doctor’s orders. You will be much better quiet here at home.”
Lucy did not reply.
“Where are you going?” Mrs. Merwent persisted.
“I’m going down town.”
“Are you going shopping?”
“No.”
“Well, *I* don’t think you ought to go. Do you, John?”
“What I think is of little consequence in this house,” answered John. He scanned the newspaper at his elbow as he spoke.
“Didn’t you say last night that you were going to Benton Harbor again today, John?” Nannie asked somewhat irrelevantly, glancing sharply at Lucy’s face.
“Yes.” John picked up his paper.
“And you won’t be back till night?” she pursued with her eyes still on Lucy’s profile.
“No.” John went on reading.
“Well, I’m certain Lucy ought not to go.” Nannie returned to the first topic with seeming carelessness.
Though Lucy had scarcely tasted her breakfast she rose and, taking up her purse and gloves, went upstairs again. She did not take Dimmie to Mrs. Hamilton’s until her husband and mother had gone to the station.
Dr. Hamilton, after a sleepless night with a bad patient, was sitting with his wife at the table finishing a late breakfast, when Lucy and Dimmie came in.
“I thought you were taking the rest cure,” the doctor greeted Lucy jocularly. He got up and lifted Stella from her high chair beside him. The little girl flew enthusiastically to welcome Dimmie.
“I am.” Lucy smiled rather wanly.
“How is your mother? She ought to have used her influence to keep you at home today,” Dr. Hamilton resumed.
“Mrs. Merwent is very well, thank you.” Lucy stooped to kiss Stella.
“I hope you’ve come to spend the day,” suggested Mrs. Hamilton cordially.
“No. I’m going down town,” replied Lucy.
“I don’t know about that,” objected the doctor doubtfully.
“Please don’t, Dr. Hamilton,” Lucy protested quickly.
He glanced at her keenly.

===Chinese===

露西拿着信坐了好一阵。最后站起身来，是要换上街的衣裳。
过了一会儿她下了楼，戴着帽子，拿着手套和钱包。她进餐厅在桌边坐下时，南妮和约翰正在吃早饭。
「今早你不出门吧，露西？」南妮盯着女儿的装束问，「你知道医生说了要静养。」
「不，我要出门。」露西告诉她。
「哎，露西，医生的话可不能不听。安安静静待在家里，你会好得快。」
露西不答。
「你去哪儿？」默温特太太追问。
「进城。」
「买东西去？」
「不是。」
「反正*我*觉得你不该去。你说呢，约翰？」
「我在这家里想什么，无足轻重。」约翰答道。他一面说，一面扫着肘边的报纸。
「约翰，你昨晚不是说你今天又要去本顿港吗？」南妮问得有点没头没脑，眼睛却锐利地瞟着露西的脸。
「是。」约翰拿起报纸。
「要到夜里才回来？」她追问，目光仍停在露西的侧脸上。
「对。」约翰接着看报。
「哼，反正露西不该去。」南妮看似漫不经心地回到头一个话题上。
露西早饭几乎没动，也起身拿起钱包和手套，又上了楼。直到丈夫和母亲去了车站，她才把迪米送到汉密尔顿太太家。
汉密尔顿医生守着重病人熬了一夜没睡，正同妻子坐在桌边吃迟开的早饭，露西和迪米进来了。
「我记得你在进行休养疗法呢。」医生同露西打趣道。他站起身，把斯特拉从身边的儿童高椅上抱下来。小姑娘欢天喜地飞奔去迎迪米。
「正是。」露西笑得有些苍白。
「你母亲好吗？她本该行使影响力，今天把你留在家里。」汉密尔顿医生接着说。
「默温特太太很好，谢谢。」露西俯身亲了亲斯特拉。
「但愿你是来待一天的。」汉密尔顿太太热络地说。
「不是，我要进城。」露西答道。
「这个我可不赞成。」医生迟疑着反对。
「求您别说，汉密尔顿医生。」露西赶紧央告。
他锐利地看了她一眼。

===Original===

“I think perhaps going out a little may do you good,” he conceded after a moment.
Lucy had seated herself in a bow window commanding the street. Soon she saw Nannie returning from the station.
“When does the train leave, Doctor?” Lucy asked.
“In eight minutes,” he informed her, after consulting his watch.
“Well, then, I’ll be going.” Lucy rose and shook hands.
“Come over whenever you can and bring Dimmie and stay as long as you can, won’t you, Mrs. Winter?” urged Mrs. Hamilton earnestly. “And your mother, too. I’m afraid she’s standing on ceremony.”
“Thank you, Mrs. Hamilton; I will,” Lucy smiled evasively.
In less than an hour she rang the bell at Miss Storms’s apartment.
“Why, Mrs. Winter!” exclaimed the maid as she opened the door and saw Lucy. “Miss Storms is out. She went to see Mr. and Mrs. Merwent off but she’ll be back at ten. Won’t you wait? I’m sure she would be disappointed at not seeing you. I think she thought you’d be at the station.”
“Yes, I’ll wait,” agreed Lucy.
“Come right in and take a chair, Mrs. Winter.”
Lucy entered the pleasant reception room and, taking up a book from a table, sat down by a window. Although the volume was the translation of a Russian novel she had often wished to see, her interest evidently was not in what she read. Most of the time while the book was in her hand she spent in staring over the roofs of the city that stretched before her. Finally Miss Storms’ key was heard in the latch and the door opened.
“Why, Lucy! Bless your heart. I’m so glad you’re better,” Miss Storms ejaculated, kissing her friend’s cheek. “They’ve just gone. If you’d come to the station you could have caught them.”
“I wanted to see you,” replied Lucy, not responding to her hostess’s smile.
Miss Storms was studying Lucy’s face carefully.
“I see you have been reading *Sanin*. What do you think of it?”
“I didn’t read much,” Lucy confessed. “You said that if ever I was in trouble I must come to you.”
“Yes.” Miss Storms’ tone became sympathetic at once and she took the younger woman’s hand.
“Well, I’ve come.” Lucy’s voice trembled slightly.
There was a pause.
“What is it, dear?” Miss Storms asked at last, stroking the hand she held.
“Oh, Miss Storms, I got a note from Papa this morning just before I left. I﻿—I﻿—” Lucy stopped.
Miss Storms nodded comprehendingly.
“I hardly know what to say, dear,” she began.

===Chinese===

「不过出去走走，也许对你有好处。」过了一会儿他让步道。
露西坐在一面临街的凸窗里。不多时，她望见南妮从车站回来了。
「火车几点开，医生？」露西问。
「八分钟后。」他看了看表告诉她。
「那我该走了。」露西起身握手。
「什么时候方便就过来，带上迪米，能待多久待多久，好不好，温特太太？」汉密尔顿太太恳切地劝说，「也带上你母亲。我怕她是在讲客气。」
「谢谢您，汉密尔顿太太，我会的。」露西含笑应着，避开了话头。
不到一个钟头，她按响了斯托姆斯小姐公寓的门铃。
「哎呀，温特太太！」女仆开门一见露西便嚷起来，「斯托姆斯小姐出去了。她去送默温特先生夫妇，十点钟回来。您等一等好吗？她要是见不着您，一定失望。我想她还当您会去车站呢。」
「好，我等她。」露西应道。
「快请进来坐，温特太太。」
露西进了那间惬意的客厅，从桌上拿起一本书，在窗边坐下。那是一部俄国小说的译本，她早就想看了，可她的心思显然不在书上。书捧在手里的那段时间，多半都用来眺望眼前铺展开去的城市屋顶。终于，锁孔里响起斯托姆斯小姐的钥匙声，门开了。
「哎呀，露西！谢天谢地。你好了些，我真高兴。」斯托姆斯小姐喊道，吻了吻朋友的面颊。「他们刚走。你要是去车站，还赶得上见一面。」
「我想见的是你。」露西答道，没有回应主人的笑。
斯托姆斯小姐细细端详着露西的脸。
「我瞧你在读《萨宁》（*Sanin*）。觉得怎么样？」
「没读进去多少。」露西坦白道，「您说过，我若有了难处，一定来找您。」
「说过。」斯托姆斯小姐的声调立刻充满同情，握住了年轻一辈的手。
「我来了。」露西的声音微微发颤。
一时静默。
「怎么了，亲爱的？」斯托姆斯小姐终于开口，抚着握住的那只手。
「唉，斯托姆斯小姐，今早出门前我收到爸爸一张字条。我——我——」露西停住了。
斯托姆斯小姐会意地点了点头。
「我一时不知说什么好，亲爱的。」她开口道。

===Original===

Lucy lifted strange eyes with dilated pupils, then turned away.
“Arthur is terribly hurt by John’s attitude,” Miss Storms continued, scrutinizing Lucy’s half averted profile. “He resents John’s position in regard to Ellen. That’s natural, but don’t think that he ever, for one moment, mistakes the source of it, or holds it against you.”
Lucy’s hands moved uneasily.
“Oh, it isn’t just Papa, Miss Storms!” she burst forth.
Her voice was hoarse. She kept her eyes on the open window where the blue sky showed, overcast by the city smoke.
There was another long pause before Miss Storms went on.
“You must get your mother out of the house, Lucy.” Her tone was emphatic.
“But where can she go? She has no place to go, Miss Storms! Since my grandmother died there’s nobody but Cousin Minnie Sheldon, and she doesn’t want Mamma.” Lucy’s hands beat the air in a gesture of futility.
She rose and, walking to the window, stood with her back to Miss Storms, her shoulders shaking spasmodically.
Finally Lucy regained her self control and faced her friend.
“I’d advise you to poison her, dear, if I weren’t afraid of getting you in worse trouble than you are.”
Lucy smiled slightly but almost simultaneously her lip twitched and the tears started to her eyes.
“Oh, don’t! Please don’t, Miss Storms,” she begged hysterically.
“There, dear. Sit down again.” Miss Storms rose and led her to the chair. “Lucy, I have to confess a spinster’s tendency to meddle, I suppose. I asked John around here the other day and talked to him about this.”
Lucy glanced at her quickly.
“He didn’t tell me he was here,” she said in a low voice, staring at her lap. “Oh, Miss Storms﻿—!”
“Yes, dear?”
Under Miss Storms’ touch Lucy trembled violently.
“John shouldn’t treat me the way he does,” she wailed like a child. “He shouldn’t believe her insinuations. He has no right to be suspicious of me. Oh, Miss Storms, it’s awful! Jim Sprague was at our house last night and I didn’t dare to treat him decently.” Lucy began to weep softly.
“Does he think that you and Jim﻿—” Miss Storms interjected incredulously.
“I don’t know what he thinks!” cried Lucy passionately. “I don’t know anything about him any more. If it wasn’t for Dimmie﻿—” and she broke into violent sobbing.
Miss Storms sat with her chin in her hands, her elbows on the arms of her chair. It was several minutes before she spoke again.

===Chinese===

露西抬起一双瞳孔放大的、陌生的眼睛，随即转过脸去。
「约翰的态度让亚瑟伤心透了。」斯托姆斯小姐接着说，端详着露西半侧过去的脸。「他对约翰在埃伦这件事上的立场心怀怨愤。这很自然。可你别以为他有一时一刻认错了这怨愤的来处，更别以为他会记在你头上。」
露西的手不安地动来动去。
「唉，不只是爸爸的事，斯托姆斯小姐！」她脱口喊道。
她的嗓音沙哑。她眼睛盯着敞开的窗——窗外透出蓝天，蒙着城市的烟霭。
又静了好长一会儿，斯托姆斯小姐才说下去。
「你必须让你母亲离开这个家，露西。」她的语气斩钉截铁。
「可她能去哪儿呢？她没有地方可去呀，斯托姆斯小姐！外婆去世以后，除了表亲明妮·谢尔登再没有别人，她又不要妈妈。」露西双手徒劳地在空中乱拍。
她站起来，走到窗前，背对着斯托姆斯小姐，肩膀一阵阵痉挛地耸动。
露西终于重新控制住自己，转脸对着朋友。
「亲爱的，要不是怕把你陷进比现在更糟的麻烦，我就劝你毒死她算了。」
露西淡淡一笑，可几乎同时嘴唇一抽，眼泪涌了上来。
「唉，别说了！求您别说了，斯托姆斯小姐。」她歇斯底里地央求。
「好了，亲爱的，再坐下。」斯托姆斯小姐起身把她领回椅边，「露西，我只好承认，老姑娘都有爱管闲事的毛病。前几天我请约翰到这儿来，同他谈过这事。」
露西飞快地看了她一眼。
「他没告诉我他来过。」她低声说，眼睛盯着膝头，「唉，斯托姆斯小姐——！」
「怎么，亲爱的？」
斯托姆斯小姐的手一碰，露西剧烈地颤抖起来。
「约翰不该那样待我。」她像个孩子似的哭诉，「他不该如何相信她的含沙射影。他没有权利猜疑我。唉，斯托姆斯小姐，太可怕了！昨晚吉姆·斯普拉格到我们家来，我连好好待他都不敢。」露西轻轻哭起来。
「他莫非以为你和吉姆——」斯托姆斯小姐难以置信地插嘴。
「我不知道他怎么想！」露西激动地喊，「我对他什么都不明白了。要不是为了迪米——」她放声恸哭起来。
斯托姆斯小姐双手托着下巴坐着，胳膊肘撑在扶手上。好几分钟，她才再开口。

===Original===

“I don’t know what to say, Lucy,” she admitted finally. “I realize that your mother is a moral idiot, and that John ought to see through her, but I know more about men and women than you do, dear, and I understand what a woman can do to a man.”
“Ain’t I a woman, too?” Lucy demanded fiercely, relapsing into the language of her childhood.
“Yes, dear, but you’re not the courtesan type. You’re the mother type, and you don’t understand the other. I used to think I was the cerebral type,” Miss Storms pursued musingly, smiling her gently ironical smile, “but I’m not. I’m the mother type, too, and I wish this minute that you were my child.”
“So do I,” said Lucy quickly, returning the pressure of Miss Storms’ hand.
“Sex is the most of life, dear. One can’t escape it any more than one can death, and the most fatal thing is to try to. Look at celibates for example. I have learned a great deal in forty years, Lucy, and I knew when I advised you to marry, that John was not great individually. But he is nature, dear, and the thing to do is to get the other woman out of the way. It frightens me to think of anything else, Lucy.”
“But I want to be loved, Miss Storms.” Tears were in Lucy’s eyes again. “I want﻿—I want﻿—” She could not go on.
“Lucy! Do you love Jim Sprague?” Miss Storms’ voice was sharper.
“No!” cried Lucy vehemently.
“And you do love John, don’t you?” Miss Storms almost pleaded.
“I﻿—I would if I had a chance.” Hesitating over the first words, Lucy finished determinedly.
Miss Storms kissed her cheek.
“The only thing is to get rid of your mother, no matter what the difficulties are,” Miss Storms resumed in a practical tone. “Oh, *why* doesn’t that Mr. Walsh say or do something! At all events we must get her away. And then you musn’t even forgive John. You must put the past out of your mind﻿—never give it a thought. It must disappear as if it had never existed. Make all the responsibility yours. John must never be humiliated, never know that you have suffered.”
“But what about me?” Lucy reminded her indignantly.
Miss Storms’ smile was rather bitter as she answered.
“I know you think I am only considering the man, Lucy,” she replied. “As a rule, I do. I did in your father’s case. Women have to take the responsibility for life. Besides, the man is the potential father, and until the woman has children she worships the potential father.

===Chinese===

「我不知道该说什么，露西。」她终于承认，「我明白，你母亲是个道德上的白痴，约翰本该看穿她。可是男人女人这门学问，我懂得比你多，亲爱的，一个女人能把男人怎样，我懂。」
「难道我就不是女人？」露西激烈地质问，不觉说回了童年时代的口音。
「是，亲爱的，可你不是那一类女人。你是母亲那一类，不懂另一类。我从前还当自己是头脑型的。」斯托姆斯小姐若有所思地说下去，挂着那缕温和讥诮的笑，「其实不是。我也是母亲那一类。这会儿我真希望你是我生的孩子。」
「我也想。」露西立刻说，回握了斯托姆斯小姐的手。
「性是人生的大半，亲爱的。人躲不开它，正如躲不开死；最要命的做法就是偏要躲。看看那些独身的人就知道了。四十年间我学到了很多东西，露西。当初劝你嫁时我就知道，约翰个人并不出众。可他是自然，亲爱的。眼下要做的，是把另一个女人挪开。往别处想，我都害怕，露西。」
「可我要的是被人爱，斯托姆斯小姐。」露西眼里又有了泪，「我要——我要——」她说不下去了。
「露西！你爱吉姆·斯普拉格吗？」斯托姆斯小姐的声音严厉起来。
「不！」露西激烈地喊。
「那你爱约翰，是不是？」斯托姆斯小姐几乎是在恳求。
「我——要是有机会，我会的。」头几个字迟疑着，末了露西说得斩钉截铁。
斯托姆斯小姐吻了吻她的面颊。
「唯一的办法是打发走你母亲，不管有多少难处。」斯托姆斯小姐恢复了就事论事的口吻，「唉，那个沃尔什先生*怎么*就不说句话、不干点什么呢！不管怎样，我们务必把她弄走。然后，你连原谅都不要提。把过去从心里抹掉——想都不要想。让它消失得像从来没有过一样。把责任全担在你自己身上。约翰永远不能受羞辱，永远不能知道你受过苦。」
「那我呢？」露西愤然提醒她。
斯托姆斯小姐回答时，笑意相当苦涩。
「我知道你觉得我只替男人着想，露西。」她答道，「多半时候，是这么回事。你父亲那桩事上我就是。女人必须为生活承担责任。再说，男人是潜在的父亲——女人还没生孩子时，崇拜的就是这个潜在的父亲。

===Original===

Afterward she worships the children. But I’m not ignoring you, dear. I am thinking of you, too. John is still the potential father, to you﻿—of more children, I hope.”
Lucy was silent as she gazed out the window.
“You may think me hard on women, Lucy. Nature is hard on them. You may think there are other men﻿—Jim Sprague, for instance﻿—but there aren’t. The female is the type﻿—the responsible one. They are the race. Men are their possessions. You chose John to be the father of your children﻿—and you didn’t Jim.”
Lucy was still silent.
“I have no highfalutin’ ideas about the sanctity of marriage, or any such nonsense,” Miss Storms went on in the same half musing tone, “although I realize that, in spite of what is perhaps some intellectual breadth, I have an old maid’s emotional idealism. But the fact remains, dear, that sex is greater than we, just as life and death are greater, and we can escape neither its ecstasy nor its agony. We mate to suffer as well as to joy, Lucy, and you are happier than I.”
Here Miss Storms reverted abruptly to her practical mood.
“You just hold on, dear,” she advised. “Men, even the greatest of them, never grow up. I think I have a plan that will do the trick. Let me gnaw over it tonight, and I’ll see you in a day or two. Everything’s going to be all right.” Miss Storms nodded reassuringly.
Lucy hesitated a moment. Then she arose.
“Won’t you stay for luncheon, dear?”
“No. I must go.”
“Well, you will hear from me soon. But on account of John’s attitude toward Arthur and Ellen, and toward me, he must never know﻿—more than he already knows﻿—about my having anything to do with it. Women are always deceiving men for their own good, you know, dear.” Miss Storms jested sourly.
Lucy made no sign and Miss Storms proceeded in a businesslike tone.
“Now, Lucy, you’re strong﻿—women have to be﻿—and sensible. You can handle this situation, dear. You must. I’ll help. I believe my idea will work. If it fails we’ll try another.”
“I don’t think it’s any use,” Lucy confessed hopelessly.
“Oh, yes it is. Don’t lose heart.”
From the apartment Lucy went to the park, and, seeking a secluded spot, seated herself on a bench and leaned her head against her hand. She remained thus for more than an hour. Tears sometimes flowed down her cheeks, but she made no sound.
Finally she rose and turned her steps toward the office.

===Chinese===

往后她崇拜的就是孩子了。可我并没有撇下你不管，亲爱的。我也在为你打算。对你来说，约翰仍旧是潜在的父亲——但愿是更多孩子的。」
露西望着窗外，默默无言。
「你也许觉得我对女人太苛刻，露西。可造化对她们本来就苛刻。你也许以为还有别的男人——比如吉姆·斯普拉格——可没有。女性才是正型——承担责任的那一型。她们就是种族本身。男人不过是她们的产业。你选了约翰做你孩子的父亲——没有选吉姆。」
露西依旧沉默。
「婚姻神圣之类的高调，我一概没有。」斯托姆斯小姐用那种半出神的调子接着说，「尽管我也明白，纵然见识上或许还算开阔，我骨子里仍有老姑娘的感情理想主义。可事实摆在那里，亲爱的：性比我们大，正如生与死比我们大。它的狂喜和它的痛苦，我们都躲不掉。我们结偶，既为享乐也为受苦，露西。你已经比我幸福。」
说到这里，斯托姆斯小姐猛地转回实际的口吻。
「你只管撑住，亲爱的。」她劝道，「男人，哪怕最了不起的，也从来长不大。我想我有个主意能解决问题。让我今夜琢磨琢磨，一两天内来看你。一切都会好起来的。」斯托姆斯小姐点头让她安心。
露西迟疑了一下，随即站起身。
「留下吃午饭吧，亲爱的？」
「不了。我得走了。」
「好，你很快就会有我的信。只是看在约翰对亚瑟和埃伦、对我的态度上——我在其中的参与，他决不能知道——不能比他已经知道的更多。女人为了男人好，总是在骗他们，这你是知道的，亲爱的。」斯托姆斯小姐酸溜溜地打趣。
露西没有表示，斯托姆斯小姐公事公办地说下去。
「好了，露西，你是坚强的——女人非坚强不可——也懂事。这个局面你应付得了，亲爱的。你必须应付。我会帮你。我相信我的主意行得通。万一不成，咱们再换一个。」
「我看没有用。」露西绝望地坦白。
「有用的。别灰心。」
出了公寓，露西去了公园，寻了个僻静的所在，在长椅上坐下，头倚着手。她就这样待了一个多钟头。泪水时时淌下面颊，她却没有出一点声。
最后她站起来，迈步朝事务所走去。

## XXVIII / 二十八
===Original===

The torpor of summer was in the air, but it did not affect Jim’s industry. Coming into the office from a luncheon he had scarcely tasted, he removed his coat, and settled himself for a hard afternoon’s work. The windows were raised but not a breath came to stir the papers on his desk. The murmur of the street sounded as remote as the echo of the ocean in a shell.
The door opened and Lucy came in. Jim glanced up, startled, but did not speak.
“I want to talk to you, Jim,” Lucy said without preamble. Her face was set.
“Sit down,” he replied, looking at her keenly. The pencil in his hand trembled.
“I couldn’t say anything last night, Jim. I am in the greatest trouble of my life.” She picked up a paper weight and examined it as she spoke.
“I know, Lucy,” he said, laying down his pencil.
She shivered as though chilled by the warm summer atmosphere. Her forced calmness forsook her suddenly.
“Oh, Jim!” she sobbed, and leaning forward on the desk, hid her face in her arms.
He reached out his hand to take hers, then withdrew it, and, picking up the pencil again, began tapping the desk with it.
“What shall I do, Jim?” Lucy lifted her head. She had command of herself now.
Jim went on tapping the desk until Lucy reached over and took the pencil from him and laid it down.
“Your mother must go away,” he declared at last.
“But where can she go? She has no money. Cousin Minnie doesn’t want her. She has no other relations, and besides, John won’t hear of her going.”
“She must go anyway,” he repeated.
“I can’t put her in the street. Oh, Jim, I don’t know what to do! She’s changed John so he’s not himself any more. Everything she does is perfect in his eyes. I’m always to blame. Everything I say is wrong. She’s even turned him against Dimmie. What shall I do? What shall I do?” and Lucy wrung her hands, her eyes fixed upon Jim’s in hungry appeal.
Sprague stared at her. His own eyes widened. His self control went to the winds.
“Do, Lucy? Do! Why come to me!” he cried, seizing her hand and gripping it until she winced.
“Jim!” she gasped.
“I’ve stood all I can.” His tone grew more intense with each word, “I’ve never been too honest with myself about you, Lucy, but I knew I loved you a long time ago. I’ve resented seeing your youth slipping by, and John not noticing it. I’ve tried not to covet his wife, because I thought he wanted her. But now I’ve got to tell you, Lucy.

===Chinese===

空气中弥漫着夏日的沉滞，却丝毫不妨碍吉姆的勤勉。午饭几乎没动，他回到事务所，脱下外衣，坐定下来，预备大干一下午。窗子都推上去了，却没有一丝风吹动他桌上的图纸。街上的市声远远的，像贝壳里海涛的回响。
门开了，露西走进来。吉姆吃惊地抬眼一望，却没有说话。
「我要同你谈谈，吉姆。」露西开门见山。她的脸绷得紧紧的。
「坐。」他答道，锐利地看着她。他手里的铅笔在抖。
「昨晚我什么都不能说，吉姆。我这辈子从没陷进过这样的难关。」她拿起一只镇纸，一面说一面端详。
「我知道，露西。」他说着，放下铅笔。
她打了个寒噤，仿佛在这温暖的夏日空气里受了凉。她强撑出来的镇定霎时弃她而去。
「唉，吉姆！」她呜咽一声，伏在桌上，把脸埋进臂弯。
他伸手想去握她的手，又缩回来，重新拿起铅笔，用它笃笃地敲着桌面。
「我该怎么办，吉姆？」露西抬起头。这会儿她又控制住了自己。
吉姆还在敲，直到露西探身把铅笔从他手里抽走，搁下了。
「你母亲必须走。」他终于宣布。
「可她能去哪儿呢？她没有钱。明妮表亲不要她。她再没有别的亲人了，再说，约翰根本听不得一个走字。」
「不管怎样，她必须走。」他重复道。
「我不能把她撵到街上去。唉，吉姆，我不知道该怎么办！她把约翰变得面目全非了。她做的样样事，在他眼里都十全十美。挨骂的永远是我，我说什么都是错。她甚至挑得他也嫌起迪米来了。我该怎么办？我该怎么办？」露西绞着双手，眼睛饥渴地望着吉姆求告。
斯普拉格直勾勾盯着她。他的眼睛也越睁越大。他的自制力烟消云散。
「怎么办？办法！那还用问到我这儿来！」他喊道，一把攥住她的手，攥得她生疼。
「吉姆！」她倒抽一口气。
「我受够了。」他的声调一字比一字激烈，「对你，我对自己从来不够坦白，露西。可爱上你这件事，我早就知道了。眼看你的青春一年年流走，约翰却浑然不觉，我不平过。我一直竭力不去贪慕他的妻子，因为我以为他心里有她。可现在，露西，我必须告诉你——

===Original===

*I* want you. And I don’t want just a mystical idea of you either. I love you, dear, and you won’t have to be afraid of my ever stopping. Let’s leave the people who don’t want us and take each other. We know we’re good enough for each other. Come on, Lucy. I need you. You are the only person in the world I ever trusted implicitly. You must. I won’t give you up. I can’t give you up. Lucy, Lucy﻿—”
She had withdrawn her hand from his and was regarding him now with an expression of wonder.
“Why, Jim, what do you mean?” she asked, aghast at his torrent of words. She had never before seen him under stress of uncontrollable excitement.
“I mean what I say. Can’t you see how I love you?”
Lucy had forgotten the troubles which brought her there and rose from her chair with fear in her eyes.
“Stop, Jim,” she commanded. “I’ve trusted you, too, and you have no right to talk this way.”
Jim had risen also, and now he came around the desk and stood in front of her, his eyes seeking hers insistently.
“Right!” he repeated. “I have the right that my love gives me, a love that never let me think of another woman since I first saw you. You’ve trusted me and you ought to have, because I’m worthy to be trusted. You trusted your husband too, and he allows the first miserable flirt that comes into his house to﻿—”
“I’m going, Jim. I can’t listen to such things.” Lucy moved toward the door.
“Stop, Lucy,” he pleaded. “Don’t go.”
She hesitated. Her manner was hunted.
“I’ll talk reasonably,” he promised. He was very pale. His voice was low again. “Sit down, please,” he added as she remained undecided.
She reseated herself, smoothing out her gloves over and over with shaking fingers. Jim took his place in his chair by the desk and picked up the pencil. They regarded each other with fear and questioning. He had the look of a man prepared to overcome hard things.
“I suppose you’re thinking of John’s confidence in me, and what I owe myself in view of it, and all that,” he began.
“No, I don’t judge you. You have always seemed so like a﻿—no, not like a brother, but like a dear friend and comrade that I could always depend on﻿—I never thought﻿—” The words would not come and Lucy continued smoothing her gloves.
Jim had himself in hand again and began speaking very carefully, drawing circles and squares on a piece of paper as he talked.
“John and I have been together nearly all our lives.

===Chinese===

*我*要你。我要的也不是什么缥缥缈缈的你的影子。我爱你，亲爱的，你永远不必担心我会停止。咱们离开这些不要咱们的人，彼此相守吧。咱们知道，咱们配得上彼此。来吧，露西。我需要你。这世上我毫无保留信过的，只有你一个人。你必须答应。我不放弃你。我放弃不了你。露西，露西——」
她早已把手抽了回去，此刻望着他，一脸惊讶。
「咦，吉姆，你这是什么意思？」她问，被这番话的洪流吓呆了。她从没见过他这样控制不住地激动。
「我说的就是这个意思。难道你看不出我多么爱你？」
露西倒把带她来此的那些愁苦忘了，眼里带着恐惧，从椅子上站起来。
「别说了，吉姆。」她喝道，「我也一直信你，可你没有权利说这种话。」
吉姆也站了起来，绕过桌子站到她面前，目光执拗地追着她的眼睛。
「权利！」他重复道，「我的爱给我的权利。打头一回见你，这份爱就不许我再想过别的女人。你信过我，也该信，因为我配得上这份信任。你也信过你丈夫——可家里一进来个轻贱的卖弄风情的女人，他就由着她——」
「我走了，吉姆。这种话我听不下去。」露西朝门口走去。
「别走，露西。」他恳求，「别走。」
她犹豫了。她的神态像被追猎着。
「我说理就是。」他许诺道。他脸色煞白，嗓音又低了下来。「请坐。」见她还拿不定主意，他又添了一句。
她重新坐下，用发抖的手指把一副手套抚了又抚。吉姆也在桌旁自己的位子上落座，又拿起那支铅笔。两人对望着，彼此的眼里都有恐惧和疑问。他的神情像是个准备着去攻克难事的男子。
「我猜你在想约翰对我的信任，想我顾着这份信任欠了自己什么，等等。」他开了头。
「不，我不评判你。你在我心里一直像个——不，倒不像兄长，像一位可以永远依靠的亲密朋友和同志——我从没想过——」话就是出不来，露西只是继续抚着那副手套。
吉姆又握住了自己，一字一句说得极仔细，一边说一边在一张纸上画着圆圈和方块。
「约翰和我，几乎一辈子都在一处。

===Original===

I am not going to say anything against him, Lucy, but before I knew you I realized that he was﻿—was not as strong as he should be. When I saw you I gave you the devotion of my soul that﻿—I needed somebody, Lucy﻿—that I had been trying to give him, because you were worthy of it. I knew you were the most desirable woman I had ever seen and I acknowledged this to myself without cant. I was proud to give you this honor in my heart, and I was prouder that I gave it to you as you really were. What John gave to you was a hazy outline of a woman seen through a mist of sex idealism. Anyone else would have done as well as you. But I never doubted the reality of his love, such as it was.”
Lucy began to show uneasiness again. Jim noticed it and hastened on.
“When your mother came I realized at once that you were in for a hard time and I stood ready to help John. I saw that she wanted to be a high priestess between you two and I set out to combat her influence over him. I tried my best, but he didn’t want any help. He could have stood with you and it would have been easy, for your mother is too cowardly to carry anything through against opposition. But he fell for any suggestion she put out, and turned against me, which wasn’t so bad, and then against you who were more loyal to him than I even. Every cheap, despicable little woman’s trick that a baby could see through was another excuse for turning away from the straightest and most lovable woman in the world and putting a little contemptible, shallow﻿—”
“Jim, please,” interrupted Lucy, stretching out her hand and touching his.
His body stiffened at the contact, but with a strong effort he restrained himself from another outburst.
“Lucy, I can’t help it. His idea of woman is the harem idea. He doesn’t want you because you are too big and too good. She suits him better. They don’t want you. They don’t want us. We’ve only got each other. We need each other, Lucy. I would never have said a word or made a sign if he’d been square﻿—if he’d appreciated you and wanted you. But you’ve wanted honesty in your relation with him, and he couldn’t stand it. He’s failed. He’s had his chance. You don’t owe him anything more, and I don’t. We’re free, Lucy. We’re free to take each other. My darling, if you knew how I love you﻿—” His voice grew husky with emotion.
Lucy rose again, pale as he.

===Chinese===

我不说他的坏话，露西。可在认识你之前，我就明白他——他没有他应有的那么坚强。见到你之后，我把灵魂里那份忠诚献给了你——那份我原想献给他的忠诚，露西，因为我需要一个人，而你配得上它。我知道你是我平生所见最可倾慕的女子，我对自己承认这一点，不带半句虚矫。能在心里献给你这份敬意，我骄傲；我更骄傲的是，我献给的是你的本来面目。约翰给你的，不过是隔着一层性别理想主义的雾气看到的、一个女人的模糊轮廓。换任何一个女人，对他来说都一样。可他的爱，虽然不过是那样的爱，其真实我从没怀疑过。」
露西又显出不安来。吉姆察觉了，赶紧往下说。
「你母亲一来，我立刻明白你要受难了，我随时准备拉约翰一把。我看得出，她想当你们两人之间的大祭司，我便着手同她在他身上的影响作斗争。我尽力了。可他不要人帮。他本可以站在你那一边——那原是轻而易举的事，因为你母亲那点胆子，遇上一丁点反抗就什么也推行不下去。可她放出什么风来他都信，先是同我翻脸——这还罢了——接着又同你翻脸，而你对他比我忠诚得多。随便哪个婴儿都能看穿的那些下贱卑劣的小女人的伎俩，都成了他一条条借口，好撇开这世上最正直、最可爱的女人，去亲近一个卑鄙浅薄的——」
「吉姆，求你。」露西打断道，伸手碰了碰他的手。
一碰之下他浑身一僵，但他强自按捺，没有再爆发。
「露西，我忍不住。他对女人的观念，是后院的观念。他不要你，因为你太大了、太好了。她更合他的意。他们不要你。他们也不要我们。咱们只剩下彼此了。咱们需要彼此，露西。他若行事光明——若赏识你、要你——我一个字也不会说，一个眼色也不会使。可你在同他的关系中要的是坦诚，他受不了。他败了。他有过机会。你不再欠他什么，我也不欠。咱们自由了，露西。咱们自由了，可以彼此相属。亲爱的，你要知道我多么爱你——」他的嗓音激动得哑了。
露西又站起来，同他一样苍白。

===Original===

Jim sprang to her and placed his hands on her shoulders.
“Lucy! You can’t, you shan’t leave me without saying you love me. I won’t allow you to let a convention rob you of my love. You’re mine, Lucy, mine, mine, mine!” and he strained her to him.
For an instant her resistance was broken and she rested in his arms as though it were at last a place where she could be safe. Then she gently drew herself away and Jim sank into the chair beside her, his head in his hands.
“I’m sorry,” she began in a shaking voice. “I believe you are a noble man, Jim. You stand for more to me now than anyone else in the world, but﻿—I can’t. Poor Jim, I﻿—I﻿—” She was crying softly.
Jim raised his head and looked at her. His eyes hungrily took in the rounded forehead, the straight nose, beautiful in profile, the ear set too high for harmony and spoiling the effect of the line of the jaw, the almost perfect mouth and chin, so appealingly feminine. His gaze devoured her eyes with their clear unflinching depths of honesty, suggesting little of sex as it is generally sought for by men; the brown of her hair with almost golden strands arranged, as always, low on her too generous brow and swept down over her ears with the exact curve that she instinctively knew would hide and pick out all the points that needed suppression or emphasis; her clear skin, so really white and delicate of texture as he knew by the glimpses her morning dresses had given him of her neck down by the shoulder and of the inner surface of her forearms; her hands and feet of normal efficient size, the hands with supple slender fingers widened at the tips; her body, the waist but moderately restricted and the bosom low, suggesting the long and willing nursing of her child﻿—he knew her so well!
Suddenly he groaned. Beads of cold moisture were on his brow.
“I can’t give you up.” He spoke with difficulty. “Lucy, Lucy, he’s not fit for you!”
Lucy did not try to hide the tears that coursed down her cheeks.
“My whole life is yours,” he continued with suppressed intensity. Then, springing to his feet again he strode toward her. “Nothing can keep me away from you, Lucy!” he said hoarsely.
She put out her hand and as it touched his breast he stopped.
“I can’t, Jim! Oh, don’t you see I can’t?” she begged.
Then she turned and went swiftly out of the office.
Jim stared at the closed door for several minutes.

===Chinese===

吉姆一跃而起，双手按住她的肩。
「露西！你不能、不许就这么离开我，连一句你爱我也不说。我不许什么礼俗夺走我的爱。你是我的，露西，我的，我的，我的！」他把她紧紧搂进怀里。
刹那间她的抗拒瓦解了，她偎在他怀里，仿佛那终究是一处可以安身的地方。随即她轻轻挣脱出来。吉姆跌坐在她身边的椅子里，双手抱头。
「对不起。」她声音发颤地开口，「我相信你是个高尚的人，吉姆。如今你在我心里的分量，超过世上任何人。可是——我不能。可怜的吉姆，我——我——」她轻轻哭了。
吉姆抬起头望着她。他的目光如饥似渴地收纳着她：那圆润的前额，那挺直的鼻子——侧影极美，那双生得偏高、坏了下颌线条之和谐的耳朵，还有那几乎无可挑剔的嘴和下巴，那样柔婉动人，一派女性气。他的目光吞噬着她的眼睛——那双清澈而不躲闪的诚实深处的眼睛，几乎不含男人们通常寻索的那种情欲之色；她棕色的头发，掺着近乎金黄的发丝，同往常一样低低拢在过分宽大的额头上，又恰如其分地掠过双耳垂下——她凭本能就知道该用怎样的弧度，掩去该掩的，衬出该衬的；她那清晰的肌肤，真正白皙而质地细腻——这是她晨衣的领口让她瞥见的肩头的脖颈、前臂的内侧告诉过他的；她那双手脚，尺寸合用而利落，手指纤巧柔韧，指尖微宽；她那身子，腰身收束得并不苛刻，胸脯低平——透着长久甘愿哺育过孩子的痕迹——他对她知之太深了！
他突然呻吟了一声。额上沁出冷汗珠子。
「我放弃不了你。」他说得艰难，「露西，露西，他配不上你！」
露西不再掩饰顺着面颊淌下的泪。
「我整个的生命都是你的。」他压抑而激烈地继续说。随即又霍地起身，大步向她逼近。「什么也拦不住我到你身边来，露西！」他声音沙哑地说。
她伸出手，那只手一触到他的胸膛，他站住了。
「我不能，吉姆！唉，你还不明白我不能吗？」她哀求道。
随即她转身快步出了事务所。
吉姆盯着那扇关上的门，望了好几分钟。

===Original===

At last he sat down slowly at his desk. He covered his face with his hands and began to sob.
---
Lucy walked the streets and the park until dusk.
When she reached home neither John nor Nannie was there.
“Dey’s done et deir dinnah. I ’lows dey’s gone to de t’eayter, Miss Lucy,” volunteered Katy.
Dimmie was asleep on the sofa in the dining room. Lucy carried him upstairs and undressed him.
When the child, whom she but half awakened, had gone to sleep again she lay face downward on the bed and wept until John and Nannie were heard ascending the stairs.
---
Lucy did not go to breakfast the next morning. Katy came up with a tray.
“I don’t care for any, thank you,” Lucy said through the locked door.
When she descended the stairs she was surprised to find John still at home, smoking by the dining room table. Mrs. Merwent was not to be seen.
“Aren’t you going to the office?” Lucy inquired of him.
“No. I want to talk to you,” he answered. His face was stern and his voice was cold.
“Where’s Mother?”
“She has gone some place with Miss Powell.”
Lucy seated herself by the window.
“I came back to the office yesterday evening,” John began.
Lucy looked at him.
“As I came around the corner I saw you coming out of the building. I went in to see if there were any letters and Jim Sprague was there alone, and, what is more, he seemed very surprised to see me.”
“Well?” Lucy’s tone was dull.
“I want to know what this means. That’s what I want to know.” His voice grew less controlled as he proceeded.
They gazed at each other with hostile faces. Dimmie entered the room.
“I’m ready to go to Mrs. Hamilton’s,” he announced.
Lucy kissed him, inspected his clothes, and straightened his hat.
“Goodbye, little son,” she smiled.
“Goodbye, Papa,” Dimmie called as he went out.
“Goodbye,” John responded impatiently.
“What do you send that boy over to Mrs. Hamilton’s every day for?” he demanded harshly.
“To go to kindergarten.”
“Kindergarten! There’s no sense in his going to kindergarten in the summer. But if there was, why couldn’t he go from here?”
“He could.”
“Then what’s the use of bothering your neighbors?”
“I send him early so as to get him away from Mother as much as possible,” admitted Lucy.
“We’ll discuss that later,” John’s lip curled, “but first I want an explanation of why you went to the office yesterday.”
“I shall explain nothing, John,” Lucy answered.
“Did you see Jim Sprague?”

===Chinese===

最后他缓缓在桌前坐下，双手掩面，呜咽起来。
---
露西在街上、在公园里走，一直走到黄昏。
到家时，约翰和南妮都不在。
「他俩吃过晚饭走了。俺寻思是看戏去了，露西小姐。」凯蒂主动说。
迪米在餐厅的沙发上睡熟了。露西把他抱上楼，脱了衣裳。
她把半睡半醒的孩子安顿得重新睡去，自己伏在床上哭，直哭到听见约翰和南妮上楼的脚步声。
---
第二天早晨露西没有下楼吃早饭。凯蒂端着托盘上来。
「不想吃，谢谢。」露西隔着锁着的门说。
她下楼时，诧异地发现约翰还没走，正坐在餐厅桌边抽烟。默温特太太不见踪影。
「不去事务所吗？」露西问他。
「不去。我要同你谈谈。」他答道。他脸色严峻，嗓音冰冷。
「母亲呢？」
「同鲍威尔小姐出门去了。」
露西在窗边坐下。
「昨天傍晚我回了一趟事务所。」约翰开了头。
露西看着他。
「我拐过街角，正看见你从楼里出来。我进去看看有没有信件，吉姆·斯普拉格独自一人在那儿。而且——他见了我，好像非常吃惊。」
「然后呢？」露西的语调发木。
「我要知道这是什么意思。我要知道的就是这个。」他越说越按捺不住。
两人横眉对望。迪米进了屋。
「我好了，可以去汉密尔顿太太家了。」他宣布。
露西亲了亲他，检查了衣裳，扶正了帽子。
「再见，小儿子。」她笑着说。
「再见，爸爸。」迪米出门时喊着。
「再见。」约翰不耐烦地应了一声。
「你天天把那孩子往汉密尔顿太太家送，为的什么？」他厉声质问。
「为了上幼儿园。」
「幼儿园！大夏天上什么幼儿园，毫无道理。就算有道理，为什么不能从家里直接去？」
「能。」
「那何必天天烦扰邻居？」
「我早早送他去，为的是让他尽量少待在母亲跟前。」露西承认道。
「这个回头再说。」约翰嘴角一撇，「先说清楚，你昨天到事务所去干什么。」
「我什么也不解释，约翰。」露西答道。
「你见吉姆·斯普拉格了？」

===Original===

“Yes, I did.”
“Well, didn’t you know I wouldn’t be there?”
“I did.”
“Then why did you pick out a day when I would be away?”
“I wanted to see Jim alone.”
“What for?” John’s face grew redder.
“I wanted to consult him.”
“Alone?” sneered John.
“Yes, alone.”
“What did you need to consult him about?” John demanded in a more peremptory tone.
“That is my own affair, John.”
“Well, it just isn’t your own affair. I’m not going to stand this kind of thing any longer.”
“Neither am I.”
“You won’t have to,” snarled John.
“Exactly. I have decided just that.”
He stared at her in astonishment.
“What do you mean, Lucy?” He lowered his voice.
“I mean that I am going to leave.”
“With Jim Sprague?” John almost shouted, springing to his feet and walking back and forth.
“Katy will hear you, John.”
“Damn Katy! I don’t care who hears!” he snapped.
“Very well then. Neither do I,” returned Lucy calmly.
“I’ll just tell you one thing right here,” John continued, “and that is that you and Jim Sprague are not going to take my child away from me.” He was breathing heavily and his eyes were suffused.
“We will settle about the child legally,” replied Lucy, in the same manner, “but you are mistaken about Jim. I am not going away with him. I am going alone.”
“How will you support yourself? I suppose your father’s wife will do that.” John almost ground his teeth.
“She knows nothing about my plans. I shall do anything I can to earn a living for myself and Dimmie. Miss Storms will take me in till I can get started. I can do book binding. I don’t intend ever to be dependent on anybody again.”
A frightened expression came into John’s eyes.
“Lucy, you are crazy! You’re trying to scare me. What reason have you got for leaving your home﻿—your husband and child?”
“I don’t intend to leave my child!” she retorted.
John gazed at her in silence, his face paling. When he spoke again his manner was conciliatory.
“Lucy! Lucy, you don’t know what you are saying.”
“Yes, I do. I know precisely what I am saying.”
“Lucy,” John was on the defensive now. “I can’t understand what has changed you so. I’m not accusing you of anything in regard to Jim Sprague, but it’s natural that I shouldn’t want you to get yourself talked about. You used to think I was always right, but now you seem almost to despise me.” His voice quivered a little. “Let’s talk things over, Lucy, and not go on like this.”

===Chinese===

「见了。」
「那你不知道我不在？」
「知道。」
「那你为什么专挑我不在的日子去？」
「我想单独见吉姆。」
「干什么？」约翰的脸更红了。
「想同他商量事。」
「单独？」约翰冷笑。
「对，单独。」
「什么事非得同他商量？」约翰用更专横的口气逼问。
「那是我自己的事，约翰。」
「哼，偏偏就不是你自己的事。这种事我再也容不下去了。」
「我也容不下去了。」
「你不用容了。」约翰狞声道。
「正是。我拿定的正是这个主意。」
他惊愕地瞪着她。
「你什么意思，露西？」他放低了声音。
「我的意思是，我要走了。」
「跟吉姆·斯普拉格一起？」约翰几乎喊出来，跳起身来回蹿步。
「凯蒂要听见了，约翰。」
「凯蒂见鬼去！谁听见我也不管！」他厉声说。
「那好，我也不管。」露西平静地回。
「我在这里就把一句话挑明。」约翰接着说，「你和吉姆·斯普拉格，休想把我的孩子从我身边夺走。」他呼吸粗重，眼里充血。
「孩子的事，咱们按法律解决。」露西用同样的口气答道，「至于吉姆，你弄错了。我不是同他走，我自己一个人走。」
「你拿什么养活自己？指望你父亲那位太太接济？」约翰几乎咬牙切齿。
「我的打算她一点不知道。为了我和迪米的生计，什么活我都能干。起步之前，斯托姆斯小姐会收留我。我会书籍装帧。我再也不打算依靠任何人。」
约翰的眼里露出惊惶。
「露西，你疯了！你这是吓唬我。你有什么理由离开你的家——离开丈夫和孩子？」
「我没说要离开我的孩子！」她反驳。
约翰默然注视着她，脸色发白。再开口时，他的态度软了下来。
「露西！露西，你不知道自己在说什么。」
「我知道。我一清二楚自己在说什么。」
「露西，」约翰这会儿转入了守势，「我不明白是什么把你变成这样。涉及吉姆·斯普拉格的事，我并没有指控你什么。可我不愿你叫人说闲话，这是人之常情。你从前总觉得我一贯正确，如今你却几乎瞧不起我了。」他的声音有点发颤。「咱们好好谈谈，露西，别再闹下去了。」

===Original===

The last words were almost beseeching.
“Very well, John, if you really want to talk over matters and are ready to see things squarely, I am ready, too, but if not, I have made up my mind and know exactly what I shall do.”
John sat down again and tried to light a cigarette, but his hand shook so that he desisted and put the cigarette in his pocket.
“What is it, Lucy?” he asked at length.
“Well, in the first place, my mother must leave here.”
“Now, Lucy,” he began querulously, “I knew you were going to say that. Why you should be so prejudiced and so vindictive against Nannie is beyond me. What earthly reason is there for putting *her* in the street﻿—for she’s got no place to go if she leaves here!”
“If you hadn’t flattered her and influenced her she would probably have been married by now.”
“Lucy!” John was horror stricken. “Married to that﻿—!” He could not find a word to express his feelings. “You are absolutely without heart, Lucy,” he protested.
“Well, if you insist on her staying,” cried Lucy, rising from her chair, “I will apply for a divorce and the custody of my child and will name my mother as correspondent in the case!”
“Lucy!” articulated John. “You’re crazy!”
“No, I’m not crazy,” she went on tensely. “That’s a dozen times you’ve told me I was since she came, but I’m not. You are the one who is crazy, and if you can’t come to your senses in any other way, I’ll bring you to them right now.”
“You wouldn’t dare do such a thing, Lucy! Why, it’s ridiculous,” John argued. “How could you prove such a fantastic joke? Where are your witnesses?” John grew almost hysterical. “You’re crazy, Lucy!”
“You’ll find out I’m sane.” Lucy’s calm was ominous. “There are witnesses enough to prove what she is﻿—my father, Miss Storms, Jim Sprague, the Hamiltons, the two servants we’ve had, and plenty in Russellville. If you think it’s a joke, John Winter, you just go on and find out who the joke is on. Everybody but you sees through her contemptible tricks. I won’t stand it!” she continued wildly. “I’ll go right now. I’m going to kindergarten to get Dimmie. Oh, to think that I ever thought I loved such a pitiable excuse for a man! Let me get away from the sight of you! I hate you! I despise you! I﻿—I﻿—oh! How can I say what I feel for you! Get out of my way!” and she started swiftly for the hall door.
John caught hold of her hand.
“Don’t touch me again﻿—ever﻿—ever!”

===Chinese===

末了几个字几乎是哀恳。
「好，约翰。你要真想谈，也打算正眼看事情，我也奉陪。不然的话，我的主意已定，要做什么我一清二楚。」
约翰又坐下，想点支烟，手抖得厉害，只得作罢，把烟揣进口袋。
「你要怎样，露西？」他终于问。
「首先，我母亲必须离开这里。」
「瞧，露西，」他抱怨着开了腔，「我就知道你要说这个。你对南妮怎么这样成见深、这样狠心，我真不懂。把*她*撵到大街上去，究竟有什么道理——她离开这儿就无处可去了！」
「若不是你捧着她、依着她，她这会儿八成早嫁人了。」
「露西！」约翰惊骇万分，「嫁给那个——！」他找不出一个词来表达自己的心情。「你简直是没心肝，露西。」他抗议道。
「好，你要是坚持留她，」露西从椅子上霍地站起，「我就去申请离婚，要孩子的监护权，并且在案子里把我母亲列为共同被告！」
「露西！」约翰一字一顿，「你疯了！」
「不，我没疯。」她紧绷着往下说，「她来以后，这话你说了不下十来遍了，可我没疯。疯的是你。你要是不肯用别的法子清醒过来，我现在就让你清醒。」
「你不敢的，露西！这简直荒唐。」约翰辩道，「这么异想天开的笑谈，你拿什么证明？你的证人呢？」约翰几乎歇斯底里了。「你疯了，露西！」
「你会知道我清醒得很。」露西的平静透着不祥。「要证明她是个什么东西，证人多得很——我父亲、斯托姆斯小姐、吉姆·斯普拉格、汉密尔顿一家、我们用过的两个女佣，拉塞尔维尔还有一大帮。约翰·温特，你要当这是个玩笑，那就走着瞧，看玩笑开在谁头上。除了你，人人都看穿了她那些卑劣的把戏。我受够了！」她狂乱地说下去，「我这就走。去幼儿园接迪米。唉，想想我竟当自己爱过这样一个算不得男人的可怜货！让我离你远点！我恨你！我鄙视你！我——我——唉！我对你的感觉叫我怎么说得出！让开！」说着她快步冲向门厅的门。
约翰一把抓住她的手。
「不许再碰我——永远——永远——！」

===Original===

she almost screamed, wrenching herself free.
“Lucy, Lucy﻿—please﻿—how can you say such things!” John was cowed. He regarded her pleadingly. Tears were in his eyes.
Lucy stood pale and defiant, her eyes shining as if with exaltation.
“Then choose this instant between me and my mother!” she commanded hotly.
“I will, Lucy﻿—I will. If you think best for her to go, I won’t say anything more. But, Lucy﻿—”
“There are no ‘buts’ about it,” interrupted Lucy remorselessly.
“I don’t mean that I want her to stay,” explained John hastily, “but you hurt me so when you say such awful things. You have told me a thousand times how you looked up to me, and now, after what you have said, I can never feel that you respect me.” He appeared almost ridiculously like a small boy.
“You’ve hurt *me*, John, for a long time, and that’s why I said them,” Lucy replied more gently.
John sat down in a chair and buried his face in his hands. Suddenly he began to sob, his shoulders heaving and the tears dripping through his fingers. Lucy hesitated and then came slowly up to him and laid her hand on his hair.
“Don’t, John,” she entreated in a soft, almost maternal tone.
“You’ve hurt﻿—me﻿—so,” he faltered abjectly, his voice choked.
“Have I, John? Poor boy! Oh, John, *why* couldn’t you see without all this?” she whispered wistfully.
“Lucy﻿—” John reached up and put his arm around her. “Lucy﻿—you didn’t mean all those things you said about﻿—about﻿—me﻿—about my being﻿—only an excuse for a man, and about﻿—despising me?” He still kept one hand over his eyes.
Lucy hesitated an instant.
“No﻿—John. I don’t think I really meant them,” she admitted.
John reached for his handkerchief and wiped his eyes.
“And you didn’t mean that you hated me?” He pulled her down to his knee. “You still love me, don’t you, Lucy?”
“Yes, John, I still love you.”
They sat several minutes in silence.
“Now I’m going down to the office.” He lifted Lucy up gently and rose from his chair.
She followed him to the hall.
“Goodbye,” he said. He took up his hat and held out his arms. She came into them and he kissed her forehead as of old.
“Goodbye, dear. Come home as early as you can.” Lucy smiled at him as he went down the steps.
She watched him as he halted in the gateway to light a cigarette.

===Chinese===

她几乎是尖叫着挣脱了。
「露西，露西——求你——这种话你怎么说得出口！」约翰被慑住了。他哀恳地望着她，眼里含着泪。
露西站在那儿，苍白而决绝，眼睛亮得像燃着亢奋。
「那就此刻做个选择，要我，还是要我母亲！」她厉声下令。
「我选，露西——我选。你觉得她该走就走，我不再多说。可是，露西——」
「没有『可是』。」露西毫不留情地打断。
「我不是想留她，」约翰急忙解释，「可你说那些可怕的话，把我伤得太狠了。你跟我说过一千遍，说你多么敬仰我。如今经过你这些话，我再不能觉得你敬我了。」他那样子，简直像个委屈的小男孩，近乎可笑。
「伤*我*，你伤了很久了，约翰。所以我才说那些话。」露西答得温和了些。
约翰坐进椅子，脸埋进双手。突然他呜呜哭起来，肩膀耸动，眼泪从指缝里滴下来。露西迟疑了一下，慢慢走到他跟前，把手放在他头发上。
「别哭了，约翰。」她用一种近乎母性的温柔声调央求。
「你把我——伤得——好苦。」他凄凄惨惨地说，声音哽住。
「是吗，约翰？可怜的人！唉，约翰，*为什么*不等到这一步你才看见呢？」她怅然低语。
「露西——」约翰抬手搂住她。「露西——你方才那些话，说——说我——说我算不得男人，说——鄙视我，都不是真心的吧？」他仍用一只手捂着眼。
露西迟疑了一瞬。
「不是真心的——约翰。我想我并不是真那个意思。」她承认。
约翰摸出手帕擦了擦眼睛。
「你也不是真恨我？」他把她拉到自己膝上，「你仍然爱我，是不是，露西？」
「是，约翰。我仍然爱你。」
两人默默坐了几分钟。
「好了，我去事务所了。」他轻轻把露西抱起来，从椅子里站起身。
她跟着他到门厅。
「再见。」他说着拿起帽子，张开了双臂。她走进去，他像从前那样吻了她的额头。
「再见，亲爱的。尽量早回来。」露西望着他走下台阶，对他微笑。
他在院门口停下点烟，她一直望着。

## XXX / 三十
===Original===

When John awoke it was late and he hastened to dress. On reaching the lower hall he found the front door open. Mrs. Merwent stood on the porch staring up and down the street.
“Where in the world is Lucy?” she began. “I heard her go to Mr. Sprague’s room last night, but the door was open this morning when I went past to go down to the kitchen.”
John had a frightened expression.
“She must have gone out for an early walk,” suggested Nannie, after a minute’s time. “She used to do such things often when she was a girl, whenever she got angry.”
“It looks like it was going to rain, too,” commented John worriedly.
He reentered the hall and took his hat from the stand.
“Did you have a quarrel last night?” Nannie catechised in a matter of fact way.
“No,” he denied shortly.
“Well, come on in, John. Don’t go out now. Wait a minute.” He had made a motion toward the door. “Eat something first. She’ll probably be back before we finish.”
He hesitated, turning his hat in his hands.
“Come on,” she coaxed. “There’s nothing to worry about.” Then, as he vacillated, she caught hold of his arm. “I’ll give you some coffee right away. The waffles won’t be fit to eat if you let them get cold, and Katy has already put them on the table. You can go and look for Lucy afterwards. She’s not gone far.”
As they turned to go in Dimmie appeared on the stairs in his night clothes.
“I want my breakfast,” he clamored. “Where’s Mamma? She ain’t in her room.”
“Mamma will be back in a little while, Jimmie,” said Mrs. Merwent soothingly. “Come on and I’ll give you your breakfast.”
---
Lucy was in the train bound for the city.
She sat staring straight before her at the back of the next car seat. There were very few people going to town so early, and no one was seated beside her. Her lips moved as the train sped on.
“Jim is the only one,” she repeated over and over.
She was, if possible, paler than ever, and her eyes shone with a peculiar light.
At the station she boarded an electric car that passed the apartment house where Jim lived. She and John had been there several times together. In front of her sat a little boy in a wide sailor hat, and on the same seat was a middle-aged woman. The boy evinced all a child’s interest in his surroundings, and at length, turning around, wriggled to his knees and smiled up into Lucy’s face.

===Chinese===

约翰醒来时已经不早，赶紧穿衣。下楼到门厅，见前门开着，默温特太太站在门廊上，朝街两头张望。
「露西到底去哪儿了？」她开口就说。「昨儿夜里我听见她去了斯普拉格先生屋里，可今儿早晨我下楼去厨房，路过时那屋门开着。」
约翰一脸惊慌。
「她准是一大早散步去了，」南妮过了一分钟提议道，「她做姑娘那阵，一生气就常干这个。」
「看这天，还像要下雨呢。」约翰担忧地说。
他回到门厅，从架上取帽子。
「你们昨晚吵架了？」南妮像查户口似的问。
「没有。」他简短地否认。
「行了，进来吧，约翰。这会儿别出去。等一等。」他刚朝门挪步。「先吃点东西。没等咱们吃完，她多半就回来了。」
他捏着帽子转来转去，犹豫不决。
「来嘛，」她哄道，「没什么可愁的。」见他还在摇摆，她一把抓住他的胳膊。「我马上给你弄咖啡。华夫饼凉了就不能吃了，凯蒂已经摆上桌了。回头你再去找露西。她走不远。」
两人转身要进屋，迪米穿着睡衣出现在楼梯上。
「我要吃早饭，」他嚷嚷，「妈妈呢？她不在屋里。」
「吉米，妈妈一会儿就回来，」默温特太太安抚道，「来，我给你开早饭。」
---
露西在进城的火车上。
她端坐着，直望前方椅背。这么早进城的人寥寥，她身边空着。火车飞驰，她的嘴唇翕动。
「只有吉姆了。」她一遍又一遍地重复。
她比先前任何时候都苍白——若还能更白的话——眼里闪着一种异样的光。
到了车站，她上了途经吉姆所住公寓的有轨电车。她同约翰一起来过几回。她前排坐着一个戴宽水手帽的小男孩，同座是个中年妇人。那孩子对周围的一切充满孩子气的好奇，末了转过身，扭着跪起来，仰头朝露西笑。

===Original===

His blue eyes, bobbed hair, and fresh color really suggested Dimmie, and to Lucy, in her overwrought state, the resemblance was startling.
“I can’t! Oh, I can’t do it!” she murmured passionately, to the child’s intense surprise.
His eyes opened wide and he stopped smiling, half frightened as she bent over and kissed his cheek. The middle-aged woman jerked him back into the seat and scrutinized Lucy suspiciously. Lucy rang the bell and descended from the car at the next corner.
“I’ll go back! I’ll go back!” she whimpered, as a punished child submits to an angry parent.
It had begun to drizzle. She was unmindful of the rain falling on her hat and of her bedraggled skirts. Tears rolled down her cheeks and fell with the rain drops to the pavement. She walked unsteadily and her breast heaved. Reaching the station she took the next train to Rosedene.
Midway between the station and the house she met John, who was striding along quickly, and anxiously scanning the street up and down.
“Where in the world have you been, Lucy? I’ve been worried almost crazy!” he exclaimed pettishly, coming up to her.
Staring straight before her, she did not answer or pause. John followed her to the house, glancing furtively, from time to time, at her immobile profile.
“Lucy! What in the world do you go out without telling anybody for?” cried Nannie who had been waiting in the hall. “It’s been raining, too. John and I were nervous about you.”
Without replying, Lucy proceeded upstairs. Dimmie ran through the upper hall to meet her.
“Hello, Mamma!” he called. “I thought you’d runned away. Nannie dressed me.”
Lucy caught him in her arms and went into her room, locking the door. Pulling him down beside her on the bed, she burst into a terrifying paroxysm of weeping. Dimmie soon began to cry also, from fright, and Lucy grew calmer.
“Oh, little son, little son!” she moaned again and again.
Soon Dimmie slipped to the floor and began to play with her work box, while she remained in the same position, utterly exhausted.
“It’s past time for you to go to Mrs. Hamilton’s, dear,” she observed finally, looking at the alarm clock on the table by the bed, and, at the same time, rising.
She brushed his hair and brought his hat from the wardrobe.
“Kiss Mother.”
Dimmie obeyed, then clattered down the stairs.

===Chinese===

那双蓝眼睛、剪短的头发、水灵的气色，活脱脱像迪米；在露西那过度紧张的境地，这相像叫她心头一惊。
「我办不到！哦，我办不到！」她激动地喃喃，把孩子吓了一跳。
小家伙睁大眼睛，收了笑，半惊半怕地任她俯身亲了亲脸颊。中年妇人一把将他拽回座里，狐疑地打量露西。露西拉铃，在下一个街口下了车。
「我回去！我回去！」她呜咽着，像个受罚的孩子顺从盛怒的家长。
天落起了毛毛雨。她全不理会打在帽子上的雨和拖脏的裙裾。泪珠滚下面颊，同雨点一齐落在人行道上。她走得摇摇晃晃，胸囗起伏。到了车站，她搭下一班车回罗斯登。
在车站与家宅的中途，她迎面撞上约翰——他正大步流星，焦急地扫视着街道两头。
「露西，你到底跑哪儿去了？我急得快疯了！」他气冲冲地迎上来。
她直望前方，不答，也不停。约翰跟着她走回家，不时偷瞟她那张纹丝不动的侧脸。
「露西！你出门怎么也不跟人说一声？」一直守在门厅的南妮喊道。「又下着雨。约翰和我都替你紧张死了。」
露西不答，径自上楼。迪米穿过楼上门厅跑来迎她。
「哈罗，妈妈！」他喊，「我还当你跑丢了呢。南妮给我穿的衣裳。」
露西一把搂住他，进了自己房间，反锁上门。她拉他一同坐倒在床边，爆发出一阵骇人的恸哭。迪米很快也吓得哭起来，露西反倒平静了些。
「哦，小儿子，小儿子！」她一声声呻吟。
不久迪米溜下地去玩她的针线盒，她还保持着原先的姿势，疲惫不堪。
「宝贝，你去汉密尔顿太太家已经迟了。」她终于望着床头柜上的闹钟说，同时站起身。
她给他梳好头发，从衣橱取来帽子。
「亲亲妈妈。」
迪米照办，然后噼噼啪啪下楼去了。

===Original===

Once alone, Lucy rearranged her hair carefully, put on a fresh house dress, and descended to the living room.
John was walking up and down when Lucy appeared. They could hear Nannie in the dining room instructing Katy regarding luncheon. The windows were open and the breeze that stirred the curtains smelled of the rain and of some aromatic weed that grew high and thick in the adjoining meadow.
“You’ll miss your train, John,” Lucy suggested gently as she entered the room. She had regained her usual composure.
John consulted his watch.
“It’s gone long ago,” he replied moodily.
“Well, you can catch the next one if you start at once.” Lucy picked up the small clock that stood beside Nannie’s metronome on the piano. “Go on,” she continued as he hesitated. “There’s no need for your staying.”
“Well, Lucy, if you’re going to do things like this at a moment’s notice, how can I﻿—?”
“I’m not going to do anything more,” she returned calmly. “Go on to the office. You needn’t worry about me any more.”
“Well, if I was certain that you﻿—”
“You can be certain,” assured Lucy quickly.
“Well, goodbye, then.” John moved slowly toward the hall.
“Goodbye, John,” Lucy answered.
The front door had scarcely closed after him when Nannie came in from the dining room.
“I was just giving Katy orders about luncheon, Lucy,” remarked Nannie suavely, “but of course if you want something different you can tell her and it will be all right.”
“It’s all right as it is, Mamma,” said Lucy.
Mrs. Merwent raised her eyebrows.
“Well! I’m glad you’ve started to call me by my right name.” Lucy did not speak and Nannie continued virtuously. “It’s just as you say about luncheon, Lucy. You are the mistress. I’m sure *I* don’t want to do or say anything that will make unpleasantness. John has enough to worry about without us disagreeing. I wish we could get along without so many misunderstandings, Lucy. I’m sure I do *my* part.”
“I wish so too, Mamma.” Lucy’s tone was free from irritation or sarcasm.
“Well, Lucy, maybe we can from now on. I know nobody tries harder or wants more to have things pleasant than I do.”
“Did you notice where my mending was put, Mamma?” Lucy inquired after a moment in the same propitiatory manner.
“Wait a minute. I think it’s under that flower stand.” Nannie brightened appreciably. “Did you find it?” she called back, on her way to the kitchen.
“Oh, yes. Here it is.”

===Chinese===

只剩一人，露西仔细重新拢好头发，换上一件干净的家常衣裙，下楼来到客厅。
露西出现时，约翰正在屋里踱来踱去。饭厅里听得见南妮在向凯蒂交代午饭。窗子开着，拂动窗帘的风里有雨的气息，还有隔壁草场里长得又高又密的某种香草的气味。
「约翰，你要误车了。」露西进屋时温和地提醒。她已恢复了平素的镇定。
约翰看了看表。
「早误过了。」他闷闷地答。
「那马上动身还赶得上下一班。」露西拿起钢琴上南妮节拍器旁那只小钟。「去吧，」见他犹豫，她继续道，「你没有留下的必要。」
「可是露西，你要是动不动就来这么一出，叫我怎么——？」
「我不会再来什么了，」她平静地回道，「去事务所吧。不必再为我担心。」
「好，要是我拿得准你——」
「你拿得准。」露西迅速向他保证。
「那好，再见。」约翰慢慢朝门厅挪。
「再见，约翰。」露西答。
前门刚在他身后关上，南妮就从饭厅进来了。
「露西，我正给凯蒂交代午饭呢，」南妮温温吞吞地说，「当然，你想换个花样，尽管吩咐她，没关系的。」
「就这样挺好，妈妈。」露西说。
默温特太太扬起眉毛。
「哦！高兴你又叫我的正称呼了。」露西不响，南妮正气地继续。「午饭的事就依你，露西。你是主妇。我自然无意说什么做什么惹出不愉快。咱们再闹别扭，约翰的愁就愁不完了。露西，我真希望咱们能少些误会。我敢说，*我*这一份是一直尽着的。」
「我也希望如此，妈妈。」露西的口吻不带火气，也不带讥诮。
「好，露西，兴许从今往后就能了。我知道，谁也不会比我更出力、更盼着家里和顺。」
「妈妈，您留意我的针线活搁哪儿了吗？」片刻后，露西用同样讨好的口气问。
「等等。我记得是搁在那花架子底下了。」南妮明显地高兴起来。「找着了吗？」她往厨房去，回头喊。
「哦，找着了，在这儿。」

At almost the same moment that Lucy took up her sewing Jim was walking slowly down the boulevard, his fists in his coat pockets and his eyes fixed on the ground.
He did not notice Miss Storms’s car until it was drawn up to the kerb beside him, almost within reach.
She opened the door and leaned out.
“Get in, Jim. I want to talk to you a little,” she called. Jim obeyed without a word.
“Where were you going?” she asked, shaking hands with him.
“Nowhere in particular,” he answered.
Miss Storms gave an order through the speaking trumpet to her chauffeur, and the car started slowly.
“Jim, you’re a good boy,” she began.
“I’d rather have you think so than almost anyone,” he replied with a slight smile.
“But you’re not very deep,” she continued, smiling back at him.
“That’s no news,” he returned.
“I’ve been thinking of Lucy night and day lately. It’s up to you to straighten this thing out, Jim. I’ve tried and I can’t.”
Jim stared at the passing vehicles for several moments before he spoke.
“I know what you mean, Miss Storms. It’s no go. I’ve tried too, and only balled things all up.” He looked straight at her an instant, then glanced away once more, coloring painfully.
“But I’m helpless, Jim.”
“So am I.”
“Just wait a second.” Miss Storms touched his sleeve with her gloved fingers. “It isn’t just that Lucy’s mother thinks I’ve aided and abetted Ellen Low in stealing her husband. The trouble is that I’m not a man.”
Jim’s smile was wry.
“Being a man wouldn’t help you any, Miss Storms.” He fumbled a tassel on the window curtain at his side. “I’ve tried to help Lucy all I could﻿—and John,” he added.
Miss Storms scrutinized him with kindly eyes. He grew red to his hair.
“You poor boy!” she said at length, squeezing his hand. “Do you know, I’m inclined to fall in love with you myself.”
“Miss Storms,” he stammered, “that is﻿—I’m afraid you don’t﻿—”
“I’m afraid I do.” She contradicted him affectionately, releasing him and turning to the window. “Do you want to smoke?” she resumed abruptly.
“I’m afraid I’ll contaminate your cushions and curtains,” he objected.
“I wish they never had anything worse than you and your pipe around them!” Her expression was whimsical.
Jim lit his pipe.
“You’re a bad strategist, Jim,” she went on. “What is needed is attack and not defence.

她推开车门，探出身来。
「上车，吉姆。我想同你谈谈。」她唤道。吉姆一言不发地服从了。
「你这是去哪儿？」她同他握手后问。
「没准去处。」他答。
斯托姆斯小姐通过话筒吩咐了司机一句，汽车缓缓开动。
「吉姆，你是个好孩子。」她开腔。
「这话我宁愿从你嘴里听到，胜过几乎任何人。」他微微笑答。
「可是你不深。」她继续道，笑着回望他。
「这不算新闻。」他回敬。
「这些日子我日夜想着露西。把这件事理顺，就靠你了，吉姆。我试过，办不动。」
吉姆盯着过往的车辆看了好一阵才开口。
「我明白你的意思，斯托姆斯小姐。没用的。我也试过，只会越搞越糟。」他直望了她一瞬，随即又把目光挪开，脸涨得生疼。
「可我无能为力呀，吉姆。」
「我也一样。」
「等一等。」斯托姆斯小姐用戴手套的指尖碰碰他的衣袖。「不光是露西的母亲认定我帮着埃伦·洛抢了她的丈夫。麻烦在我不是个男人。」
吉姆苦笑了一下。
「是个男人也帮不了您什么，斯托姆斯小姐。」他摸索着身旁车帘上的流苏。「我已经尽力帮露西了——还有约翰。」他补了一句。
斯托姆斯小姐用慈祥的眼光端详他。他红到了头发根。

「斯托姆斯小姐，」他结结巴巴，「那是——恐怕您并不——」
「恐怕我正是。」她疼爱地反驳他，放开手，转向车窗。「想抽烟吗？」她忽然换话。

吉姆点着了烟斗。

Jim regarded her with a curious expression.

“Ruthlessness appears to be a purely female trait.” Miss Storms’ half humorous air was full of bitterness.

“We’re only two blocks from the office. Let me out here,” requested Jim, observing the buildings. “Goodbye.”
“Goodbye, Jim.” They shook hands warmly.

John’s expression as he came into the house in the evening was worried and uncertain.
He had left the office a little earlier than usual and reached Rosedene on a train which arrived there before the customary hour. He found Lucy, Dimmie, and Nannie sitting in the dining room. Lucy was reading aloud to Dimmie from a book of fairy tales and Mrs. Merwent was embroidering some underwear she had purchased on the previous day in consultation with Miss Powell. As John entered both women ceased their employment.
“Did you find everything all right at the office?” Lucy inquired with her usual simple manner.
“And are you all tired out, John?” added Nannie, smiling at him.
“No, I’m not tired,” he answered. “I was worried, that’s all.” As he spoke, he seated himself and lit a cigarette.
“Don’t worry, John,” Lucy said, drawing Dimmie to her side.
“No,” supplemented Mrs. Merwent. “There’s nothing to worry about, you poor boy.”
“Well, I’m glad if there isn’t.” He drew a long breath.

当晚约翰进家门时，神情忧惚而不定。
他比平时提早离开事务所，搭的是到罗斯登比往常早的一班。他见露西、迪米和南妮坐在饭厅里。露西正给迪米读一本童话书，默温特太太在绣一件内衣——前一天她同鲍威尔小姐商量着买的。约翰一进门，两个女人都停了活计。
「事务所里一切都顺当吗？」露西用她惯常的朴素态度问。
「约翰，累坏了吧？」南妮笑着补上一句。
「不累，」他答，「就是担心来着。」说着他落座，点上一支烟。
「别担心了，约翰。」露西说着，把迪米拉到身边。
「是啊，」默温特太太附和，「没什么可担心的，你这可怜的孩子。」
「嗯，没有就好。」他长长吸了一口气。

“I’m going down town again in the morning,” Nannie continued a moment later. “I just must get some more of this lingerie. It’s perfectly lovely. I’m going to show you *part* of what I bought after dinner, John.” She laughed coquettishly.
“Anyone would think you were making a trousseau,” he commented jealously.
Nannie became suddenly serious.
“No, John. Not for the present, at least.”
There was a brief pause.
“I see Carter’s are selling out. You might add to your collection there, Mamma.” Lucy’s hands moved uneasily over Dimmie’s hair as she talked. As she turned toward the child the light falling on her face revealed its haggard outlines.
No one responded to her remark. She glanced up and caught John’s gaze fixed on her mother. He drew another sigh of evident relief.
“Do you know,” he began with something of his old expansive manner, pushing his hair back from his forehead, “I went to see the sculptures of that new Polish artist today at lunch time. He is the beginning of a new movement away from the Rodin worship of sex and emotion in art.”
“I don’t think they ought to allow some statues to be exhibited,” declared Nannie.
“It isn’t so much the moral or ethical objection that influences me,” John elaborated, “but, as I have said before, I don’t believe in the emotional and literary in art. The artist’s business is to create beauty, not to comment upon it. The public is intelligent enough to understand and appreciate real artistic achievement, without tricks to call their attention to it. You really ought to see the exhibit, Lucy.”
“I should like to,” said Lucy encouragingly.
“And I too,” seconded Nannie.
“Well, we’ll all go,” he proposed.
John talked much during dinner. When it was over and Lucy had accompanied Dimmie up to bed, Nannie turned to John sweetly.
“I’ll sing to you a little, John,” she suggested.
“Yes, Nannie,” he acquiesced. He stretched his arms over his head in a gesture of well being. “I feel in the mood for music tonight,” he added comfortably.
Nannie seated herself at the piano. Lighting a cigarette, John reclined in the Morris chair beside her.

“Oh, everybody’s well,” John answered, and, picking up his mail, began to whistle again. “Nannie is coming down town on the nine-thirty to do some shopping,” he observed as he was opening a letter a moment later.
Jim glanced up again, but was silent.
The two worked on without further conversation until about nine o’clock, when Jim rose. He reached for his hat.
“When will you be back?” John inquired, tilting his chair toward the wall and opening his cigarette case.
“Not till after lunch,” Jim informed him. “I’m going several places, Layard’s among them.”
On reaching the street, however, Jim boarded a car from which he descended near the station that was the terminus for suburban trains from Rosedene, and when Mrs. Merwent emerged from the building he was one of the first pedestrians she encountered.
“How do you do, Mrs. Merwent?” He greeted her pleasantly, raising his hat as he spoke. “Aren’t you lost in this great city all alone?”
“Why, how do you do, Mr. Sprague?” cooed Nannie, shaking hands. “What a pleasant surprise! I thought you were at the office with John.”
“I was until a few minutes ago,” he explained, turning and walking beside her. “I’m out hunting for lath and cement now, but I’m glad to have run across you. I’ve been wanting to have a little talk with you for a long time.”
“Yes?” responded Nannie, scrutinizing him sharply from under her hat.
“Are you in a hurry now?” continued Jim. “Can’t I take you somewhere where we can sit down and have a soft drink?”
“Why, thank you, Mr. Sprague,” she declined, “but I *am* rather in a hurry. I am anxious to get back to Lucy, you know. She depends so on me, and just now she’s not as well as usual.”
“Is she worse?” Jim half halted.
“No. Just about as usual.” Nannie took note of his perturbation. “I think the summers here are trying on her. I wish she could get away to the country for a couple of months.”
Jim quickened his pace.
“Well, of course I don’t want to detain you, Mrs. Merwent, but what I want to say won’t take more than ten minutes. Suppose we walk about in this little park a moment. It’s almost empty and we’ll be practically alone.”
“Why, Mr. Sprague,” Nannie laughed nervously, “you almost frighten me. You talk like a﻿—conspirator!”
“Don’t be afraid. I’m not dangerous. Here, let’s go this way.” And he led her from the street into a shady promenade.

Nannie, hesitating slightly at first, smiled and conversed about the weather, the occasional people they met in the course of their walk, and other irrelevant topics.

“Well, it is this, Mrs. Merwent. I am going to Russellville.”
Nannie, of course, could not change her artificial color, but her eyes and mouth opened their widest.
“On business?” she inquired uncomfortably.
“No. I am going to see Professor Walsh, and Mr. and Mrs. Sheldon, and Mr. Blair, and Judge Dodd, and several other friends of yours.”
Nannie sat down on a bench by the path. Jim seated himself on the same bench, a little distance from her. His brown eyes appeared yellowish in the sunlight as they looked straight into hers.
